coefficients of $R_0^{-7}$ and $R_0^{-8}$ in \eqref{dpn:eq:inverseE2} are
$\Delta_7$ and $\Delta_8$ of Table~\ref{dpn:cp:tab:inverse}.  The core
equation is an instance of the dominant-block theorem of the
transseries-and-inversion volume \cite{tai}, and
Theorem~\ref{dpn:jh:thm:threshold} replaces the $O(1)$ rounding bracket by a
$1+o(1)$ localization from the exact saddle.}

\section{Conditioned geometry: a logistic boundary and a Gumbel maximum}
Conditioning the Bernoulli law \eqref{dpn:eq:pk} on $S=n$ gives exactly
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:eq:conditioned}
 \Prob_n(\lambda)=\frac{\normp(\lambda)^\alpha}{a_\alpha(n)}.
\end{equation}
No approximation is involved in this identification.

\subsection{The boundary profile}
Fix distinct integer sites $k_{1,n},\ldots,k_{d,n}$ with
$(k_{j,n}-M)/H\to x_j\in\R$. Their unconditioned probabilities tend to
$p(x_j)=(1+e^{x_j})^{-1}$ by Lemma~\ref{dpn:lem:scales}.
For any prescribed pattern $\epsilon_j\in\{0,1\}$, independence gives
\begin{align}\label{dpn:eq:pattern}
 &\Prob_n(X_{k_{j,n}}=\epsilon_j\text{ for all }j)\notag\\
 &\quad=\prod_{j=1}^d p_{k_{j,n}}^{\epsilon_j}
                       (1-p_{k_{j,n}})^{1-\epsilon_j}
 \frac{\Prob_t\{S^{\rm rem}=n-\sum_jk_{j,n}\epsilon_j\}}
      {\Prob_t(S=n)}.
\end{align}
The shifted target differs from the remaining mean by $O(M)=o(\sqrt B)$.
The uniform local limit theorem in Section~\ref{dpn:sec:uniform} makes the ratio
of point probabilities tend to one. This proves both the logistic limit and
finite-site asymptotic independence. Similarly, a site at $\gamma M$ has
limiting occupation $1$ for $0<\gamma<1$, and $0$ for $\gamma>1$.

\subsection{The correct centering of the maximum}
The large solution of \eqref{dpn:eq:bdef} exists uniquely for sufficiently small
$t$. With $z=tb$, the equation is $z^\alpha e^{-z}=t^{\alpha+1}$, so
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:eq:blambert}
 b(t)=-\frac\alpha t\,
 W_{-1}\left(-\frac{t^{1+1/\alpha}}\alpha\right).
\end{equation}
Solving its logarithm,
$z-\alpha\log z=-(\alpha+1)\log t$, with $t=\alpha s e^{-s}$ yields
\begin{align}\label{dpn:eq:bexpand}
 \frac bM={}&1+\frac1\alpha
 +\frac{-\log s+\alpha\log(\alpha+1)-(\alpha+1)\log\alpha}
                {\alpha s}
 +O_\alpha\left(\frac{\log s}{s^2}\right).
\end{align}
Keeping only $b\sim(1+1/\alpha)M$ is not adequate for the Gumbel
centering: the omitted shift has size $M\log s/s$, larger than $1/t$.
Keeping all the displayed terms in \eqref{dpn:eq:bexpand} does give an
$o(1/t)$ centering error. The exact definition \eqref{dpn:eq:bdef} or
\eqref{dpn:eq:blambert} avoids this truncation bookkeeping altogether.

\begin{proof}[Proof of the maximum assertion in Theorem~\ref{dpn:thm:geometry}]
Fix $x\in\R$ and put $m=\lfloor b+x/t\rfloor$. Then $tm\to\infty$ and
$m/M\to1+1/\alpha>1$. In the tail $k>m$, all odds tend uniformly to zero.
A sum--integral comparison followed by integration by parts gives
\begin{align}\label{dpn:eq:tailint}
 \sum_{k>m}p_k
 &\sim\sum_{k>m}k^\alpha e^{-tk}
 \sim\frac{m^\alpha e^{-tm}}t
 \longrightarrow e^{-x}.
\end{align}
For the second equivalence, substitute $v=tx$ in the integral and use
$\int_z^\infty v^\alpha e^{-v}\dd v
=z^\alpha e^{-z}(1+O_\alpha(z^{-1}))$.
The endpoint discretization has relative size $O(t)$.
The last limit follows from $b^\alpha e^{-tb}/t=1$ and
$m/b\to1$. Also, $\sup_{k>m}p_k=O(t)$, so $\sum_{k>m}p_k^2=o(1)$.
Therefore the unconditioned probability of no part above $m$ is
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:eq:tailzero}
 \prod_{k>m}(1-p_k)\longrightarrow e^{-e^{-x}}.
\end{equation}

It remains to justify conditioning, rather than silently replacing one
ensemble by another. The removed mean and variance satisfy
\[
 \sum_{k>m}kp_k=O(M),\qquad
 \sum_{k>m}k^2p_k(1-p_k)=O(M^2).
\]
These bounds follow from the same tail-integral calculation with one or two
extra powers of $k$. The remaining sum $S_{\le m}$ thus has mean
$n-O(M)$ and variance $B(1+o(1))$. Its central block around $M$ is intact.
The uniform local limit estimate gives
\[
 \frac{\Prob_t(S_{\le m}=n)}{\Prob_t(S=n)}\longrightarrow1.
\]
Independence of the retained and removed sites now gives
\[
 \Prob_n(L_n\le m)=
       \prod_{k>m}(1-p_k)
       \frac{\Prob_t(S_{\le m}=n)}{\Prob_t(S=n)}
       \longrightarrow e^{-e^{-x}}.
\]
This proves the Gumbel limit. Its tightness, \eqref{dpn:eq:bexpand},
$M\sim K$, and $(tM)^{-1}=1/(\alpha s)\to0$ imply
$L_n/K\to1+1/\alpha$ in probability.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{The two locations are genuinely different.}
The occupation boundary lies near $M\sim\sqrt{2n}$, while the maximum lies
near $(1+1/\alpha)M$. For $\alpha=1$, a typical largest part is asymptotic
to $2\sqrt{2n}$, not to $\sqrt{2n}$. Individual occupancies far above the
boundary are small, but there are enough possible sites to move the extreme
by a macroscopic multiple of $M$. This is why a boundary profile alone does
not determine the largest part.

\subsection{Explicit centring in \texorpdfstring{$\log\sqrt{2n}$}{log sqrt(2n)} \src{48}}\label{dpn:48:sec:gumbel}
Source 48 proves the Gumbel law independently, with the centring written in
$K=\sqrt{2n}$ and $r=\log K$ instead of the exact root $b(t_n)$.  For positive
integer $\alpha$ the measure $\Prob_n$ is the size distribution obtained by
choosing uniformly among the coloured objects; for every $t>0$ it is exactly
the conditional distribution of $\{k:X_k=1\}$ given $S=n$ \src{48}.

\begin{corollary}[Gumbel law with explicit centring; sources 48 and 40]\label{dpn:48:thm:gumbel}
Define
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:48:eq:centering}
 \beta_\alpha(r)=(\alpha+1)r-\log r+\alpha\log(\alpha+1)-(\alpha+1)\log\alpha.
\end{equation}
For every fixed real $x$,
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:48:eq:gumbel}
 \Prob_n\left(\frac{\alpha r}{K}L_n-\beta_\alpha(r)\le x\right)
                 \longrightarrow\exp(-e^{-x}).
\end{equation}
For A022629 ($\alpha=1$), an equivalent form is
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:48:eq:gumbels1}
 \frac{L_n-\left(2K-\frac{K}{r}\log(r/2)\right)}{K/r}
                  \ \Longrightarrow\ G,
 \qquad \Prob(G\le x)=e^{-e^{-x}}.
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}
Given Theorem~\ref{dpn:thm:geometry}, the corollary amounts to
$t_nb(t_n)-\beta_\alpha(r)\to0$: by \eqref{dpn:eq:bexpand},
$t_nb=\alpha s\,b/M$ agrees with
$(\alpha+1)s-\log s+\alpha\log(\alpha+1)-(\alpha+1)\log\alpha$ up to
$O_\alpha(\log s/s)$, and $s=r+O(r^{-2})$, $t_n=(\alpha r/K)(1+O(r^{-2}))$ by
\eqref{dpn:48:eq:xr} and \eqref{dpn:48:eq:sfirst}.  (The intake compared the
two centrings numerically for $\alpha=1,2,5$; the difference decays like
$\log s/s$.)  Source 48 proves it directly.

\begin{proof}[Proof \src{48}]
Work first with the unconditioned Bernoulli variables at the exact saddle,
with $s,M,t$ as in \eqref{dpn:eq:phase}.  Set
\[
 d=(\alpha+1)s-\log s+\alpha\log(\alpha+1)-(\alpha+1)\log\alpha,
 \qquad m=\left\lfloor\frac{d+x}{t}\right\rfloor.
\]
Then $tm\to\infty$ and $m/M\to(\alpha+1)/\alpha>1$.  Summation versus
integration for a decreasing tail, followed by one integration by parts (or
its standard real-power analogue), gives
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:48:eq:tailasympt}
 \sum_{k>m}k^\alpha e^{-tk}
   \sim t^{-\alpha-1}(tm)^\alpha e^{-tm}.
\end{equation}
For real $\alpha>0$, the relative error from the integral asymptotic is
$O_\alpha((tm)^{-1})$; the relative discretization error is $O_\alpha(t)$.
Now $\log(1/t)=s-\log s-\log\alpha$ and
$\log(d+x)=\log s+\log(\alpha+1)+o(1)$.  Hence
\[
 (\alpha+1)\log(1/t)+\alpha\log(tm)-tm=-x+o(1),
\]
and the tail in \eqref{dpn:48:eq:tailasympt} tends to $e^{-x}$.  The maximum
individual odds on this tail are $O_\alpha(t)$ and tend to zero, so replacing
odds by $p_k$ changes the sum by $o(1)$, and $\sum_{k>m}p_k^2=o(1)$.
Independence gives
\[
 \Prob_t(X_k=0\text{ for every }k>m)=\prod_{k>m}(1-p_k)\longrightarrow e^{-e^{-x}}.
\]

It remains to justify conditioning on total size, which cannot be omitted.
Let $T=\sum_{k>m}kX_k$ and $S^{\le m}=S-T$.  The same tail calculation with
extra factors of $k$ gives $\E_tT=O_\alpha(M)$ and $\Var_t(T)=O_\alpha(M^2)$.
By Lemma~\ref{dpn:lem:scales}, $\E_tT/\sqrt B\to0$ and $\Var_t(T)/B\to0$.  The
entire active interval near $M$ lies below $m$ for large $n$.  Thus the
shifted local limit estimate \eqref{dpn:48:eq:shiftedllt} applies to the
truncated sum, and $\Prob_t(S^{\le m}=n)/\Prob_t(S=n)\to1$.  Independence of
the tail and the truncated sum now gives
\[
 \Prob_n(L_n\le m)
 =\Prob_t(T=0)\frac{\Prob_t(S^{\le m}=n)}{\Prob_t(S=n)}
 \longrightarrow e^{-e^{-x}}.
\]
This proves the result in the exact saddle coordinates.  Finally,
\eqref{dpn:48:eq:xr} and \eqref{dpn:48:eq:sfirst} imply $M/K=1+O_\alpha(r^{-2})$,
$s=r+O_\alpha(r^{-2})$, and $t=(\alpha r/K)(1+O_\alpha(r^{-2}))$.  The change in
the normalized extreme-value coordinate is $o(1)$, since $L_n/M$ is tight by
the limit just proved.  Replacing $s,M,t$ by $r,K,\alpha r/K$ therefore proves
\eqref{dpn:48:eq:gumbel}; tightness of the Gumbel coordinate also gives
$L_n/K\to1+1/\alpha$.  For $\alpha=1$, $\beta_1(r)=2r-\log(r/2)$, which is
\eqref{dpn:48:eq:gumbels1}.
\end{proof}

The upper transition is near $M\sim K$, but the largest part is near
$(1+1/\alpha)K$.  There is no contradiction: a long tail of individually rare
occupancies produces an extreme well beyond the transition \src{48}.

Source 48 also proves the logistic part of Theorem~\ref{dpn:thm:geometry} (its
Proposition~7.2): for fixed distinct reals $u_1,\dots,u_d$ and
$k_j=\lfloor M+u_j/t\rfloor$ at the exact saddle, the inclusion indicators
converge jointly to independent Bernoulli variables with probabilities
$(1+e^{u_j})^{-1}$.  Since $tH=s/(s-1)\to1$, its coordinate $u=t(k-M)$ and
source 40's $x=(k-M)/H$ have the same limit, and its proof is the argument
around \eqref{dpn:eq:pattern}: the log odds at $k_j$ are $-u_j+o(1)$, and
fixing finitely many indicators moves the remaining target by $O_\alpha(M)$
and removes variance $O_\alpha(M^2)$, both negligible.
\mergenoteb{Source 75-06 proves both parts of Theorem~\ref{dpn:thm:geometry}
independently, for any number $\mu$ of layers
(Theorem~\ref{dpn:cp:thm:geometry}); at $\mu=1$ its centring equation is
\eqref{dpn:eq:bdef}.}

\section{A proved exponential scale from Fourier resonances}\label{dpn:sec:resonance}
The bound \eqref{dpn:eq:noncentral} suggests an exponentially small scale not
visible in either the logarithmic block or the central Edgeworth series.
We can identify its constant at each fixed integer resonance.

\begin{theorem}[Fixed-resonance modulus]\label{dpn:thm:resonance}
For every fixed nonzero integer $\ell$, with $\phi_t$ as in
Section~\ref{dpn:sec:llt},
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:eq:resonance}
 -\log\left|\phi_t\left(\frac{2\pi\ell}{M}\right)\right|
 \sim\frac{2\pi^4\ell^2}{3}\frac{H^3}{M^2}
 \sim\frac{2\pi^4\ell^2}{3\alpha^3}\frac M{s^3}.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The exact modulus formula is
\[
 \log|\phi_t(\theta)|
  =\frac12\sum_{k\ge1}\log\left(1-4p_k(1-p_k)
                                    \sin^2(k\theta/2)\right).
\]
At $\theta=2\pi\ell/M$, write $k=M+xH$. For bounded $x$,
\[
 \sin^2(\pi\ell k/M)
 =\pi^2\ell^2 x^2H^2/M^2+O_\ell(x^4H^4/M^4).
\]
The logarithm is therefore $-4p_k(1-p_k)$ times this sine square to leading
order. Lemma~\ref{dpn:lem:scales}, applied with moments $2$ and $4$, gives
\[
 -\log|\phi_t(2\pi\ell/M)|
 \sim\frac{2\pi^2\ell^2H^3}{M^2}
     \int_\R x^2\frac{e^x}{(1+e^x)^2}\dd x.
\]
One may first restrict to $|x|\le s^\delta$ with small fixed $\delta>0$;
Taylor remainders are then uniformly controlled. Outside this interval,
the exponential boundary tails and the lower-endpoint bounds in
Lemma~\ref{dpn:lem:scales} are negligible at the displayed scale. The quadratic
logarithm error is $O(H^5/M^4)=o(H^3/M^2)$.
Finally,
\[
 \int_\R x^2\frac{e^x}{(1+e^x)^2}\dd x
 =4\sum_{j\ge1}\frac{(-1)^{j-1}}{j^2}=\frac{\pi^2}{3},
\]
by expansion on the positive half-line. This proves the theorem.
\end{proof}

For fixed $\alpha$, the scale $\exp[-C_\alpha M/s^3]$ is smaller than
every power of $s/M$, because $M/s^3$ dominates $\log M$. However, its
constant deteriorates as $\alpha^{-3}$. This makes the fixed-$\alpha$
asymptotic theorem compatible with large visible resonances at moderate $n$.

The theorem concerns the \emph{modulus at a specified point}. It does not
yet determine the shifted secondary saddles, their phases, or the contribution
of their full neighborhoods to the coefficient integral. Calling it a
complete multi-saddle transseries would be incorrect. It does identify a
rigorous exponential scale and a concrete next target for exponential
improvement.
\writenoted{In the critical window of growing powers,
$\alpha\asymp M^{1/3}/\log M$, the action $H^3/M^2$ of
Theorem~\ref{dpn:thm:resonance} stays bounded, and source 100-93 determines all
three: the shifted centres $2\pi jB_{RS}/B$, the phases $-2\pi j\mu_R$ and the
full-cell contributions, with relative error $o(1)$
(Theorem~\ref{dpn:ct:thm:theta} and Section~\ref{dpn:ct:sec:relation}).  For
fixed $\alpha$ they remain undetermined.}

\section{Reproducible computation and finite-size diagnostics}\label{dpn:sec:checks}
\subsection{Exact calculations}
The supplied \file{code/40-norm-moments-verify.py} computes the five integer sequences through
$n=5000$ by descending polynomial multiplication. It compares their initial
sixteen terms against the consulted OEIS entries and independently checks
$n\le150$ by a logarithmic-derivative recurrence. Explicitly, define
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:eq:exactB}
 C_\alpha(m)=\sum_{d\mid m}(-1)^{m/d+1}d^{1+\alpha m/d}.
\end{equation}
Then $a_\alpha(0)=1$ and
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:eq:exactrec}
 n a_\alpha(n)=\sum_{m=1}^n C_\alpha(m)a_\alpha(n-m).
\end{equation}
This follows by expanding $qA_\alpha'(q)/A_\alpha(q)$ formally. The two
algorithms have different arithmetic paths, so their agreement is a useful
implementation check. It is not a proof of the asymptotic theorems.

All five exact prefix checks, all recurrence checks through $150$, and strict
monotonicity checks through $5000$ passed. The complete computed lists are
included as plain text. They were independently generated here, not copied
from OEIS b-files.

\subsection{The central approximation is excellent for one column, not all}
Let $G=e^{F(t_n)+nt_n}/\sqrt{2\pi B}$, with the infinite sums evaluated by
high-precision truncation. The following are numerical diagnostics, not
interval-certified bounds. Values shown are rounded from the included CSV.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{rrrrr}
\toprule
$\alpha$ & $n$ & $a_\alpha(n)/G$ & $a_\alpha(n)/(G(1+\mathcal E_1))$
 & $a_\alpha(n)/(G(1+\mathcal E_1+\mathcal E_2))$\\
\midrule
1 & 100 & 0.989159702 & 0.999826713 & 0.999908591\\
1 & 1000 & 0.997526322 & 0.999995495 & 0.999999984\\
1 & 5000 & 0.999084078 & 0.999999367 & 0.999999999\\
2 & 5000 & 0.999545155 & 1.000036354 & 1.000036527\\
3 & 5000 & 0.961625508 & 0.961944240 & 0.961944315\\
4 & 5000 & 1.230518103 & 1.230825332 & 1.230825386\\
5 & 5000 & 1.581097698 & 1.581414178 & 1.581414223\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
At $\alpha=1,n=5000$, two central corrections reduce the relative error to
about $1.1\times10^{-9}$. At $\alpha=5,n=5000$, the central approximation
is plainly not accurate, and adding central corrections does not repair it.
This is evidence about finite-size behavior, not a contradiction of a
fixed-parameter limit. In particular, these tests must not be advertised as
numerical confirmation of rapid convergence for all five columns.
\mergenoteb{Source 75-01's tests at $\alpha=2,3$, $n=2500$ show the same
effect, with residuals that do not change from $J=1$ to $J=3$: a noncentral
contribution, not slow Edgeworth convergence (Section~\ref{dpn:jh:sec:numerics}).}

The resonance diagnostic computes the exact product modulus at
$\theta=2\pi/M$, using the same numerical saddle:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{rrr}
\toprule
$\alpha$ & $n$ & $|\phi_t(2\pi/M)|$\\
\midrule
1 & 5000 & $2.33482\times10^{-15}$\\
2 & 5000 & $2.72621\times10^{-5}$\\
3 & 5000 & $0.0173352$\\
4 & 5000 & $0.150645$\\
5 & 5000 & $0.361805$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The increase in these noncentral moduli is consistent with the observed
failure of a central-only approximation. Their values do not by themselves
predict the sign of the coefficient error; the omitted phases matter.
\writenoted{A numerical decomposition of these residuals into Fourier cells
is recorded in Section~\ref{dpn:jh:sec:numerics}; it is evidence, not a
formula.}

\subsection{Logarithmic expansions are not monotone numerical improvements}
For $\alpha=1,n=5000$, the exact logarithm is approximately
$401.567349464797$. Subtracting the leading expression $K(r-1)$ leaves
$41.05033086599$. Subtracting the expansion through $P_3$ instead leaves
$-2.72431695876$, while truncating through $P_7$ leaves $-7.67561845406$.
The additional terms are not a promise of monotone improvement at a fixed
small $r$. The missing prefactor and endpoint contributions are also on
scales suppressed by the logarithmic theorem but relevant numerically.

An independent continuum check evaluates the integral and mean in
Lemma~\ref{dpn:lem:smoothing}, without using the rational-function expansion,
and compares the normalized remainder after $P_7$ with $P_8$:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{rrr}
\toprule
$\alpha$ & $s$ & Scaled remainder $r^8(\text{entropy}/(\alpha K)-\mathscr H_{\alpha,7}(r))$\\
\midrule
1 & 24 & 7881.08213\\
1 & 48 & 5997.09948\\
1 & 96 & 5424.47363\\
\multicolumn{2}{r}{Predicted limit $P_8(\pi^2/6)$:} & 4963.76905\\
3 & 24 & 15.7012553\\
3 & 48 & 9.85270737\\
3 & 96 & 9.49113831\\
\multicolumn{2}{r}{Predicted limit $P_8(\pi^2/54)$:} & 9.15764483\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The approach is slow, but has the expected direction in these samples.
These are continuum diagnostics at nonintegral implied $n$, not exact
integer-sequence computations at exponentially large indices.

\subsection{What the numerical implementation guarantees}
The integer arithmetic and recurrence comparisons are exact. For the saddle
calculations, the program uses arbitrary-precision arithmetic and chooses a
cutoff from a geometric majorant for
$\sum k^{\alpha+6}e^{-tk}$. This bounds the tails relevant to all cumulants
through order six, apart from harmless fixed constants. The test used
50 requested decimal digits with guard digits; the saved mean residuals are
far smaller than the displayed precision. Nevertheless, the majorant and
Newton iteration are evaluated in floating point, not outward-rounded
interval arithmetic. Accordingly, the CSV values are diagnostics rather than
certificates of rigorous numerical error bounds.

\subsection{Source 48's computations for A022629 \src{48}}\label{dpn:48:sec:computations}
\paragraph{Two exact coefficient constructions.}
For an integer exponent $\alpha\ge1$, multiply the product factors
successively.  Starting with $a_0=1$ and $a_n=0$ for $n>0$, process each $k$ by
updating in \emph{decreasing} order of $n$:
$a_n\leftarrow a_n+k^\alpha a_{n-k}$ $(n\ge k)$.  Descending order enforces at
most one use of size $k$; increasing order would compute a different
partition problem.  The independent check is the logarithmic-derivative
recurrence \eqref{dpn:eq:exactrec}.  That formal identity is valid regardless
of the analytic restriction in Proposition~\ref{dpn:48:prop:zeros}: only
finitely many terms contribute to a fixed degree.  The program
\file{code/48-a022629-verify.py} compares the two constructions through degree
100, checks the initial OEIS values and strict monotonicity, and computes the
whole exact table through $n=6400$ for $\alpha=1$
(\file{data/48-a022629-exact_coefficients.csv}).
\mergenote{On the common range $n\le5000$ this table equals source 40's
\file{data/40-norm-moments-A022629_computed.txt} entry for entry; the intake
also recomputed both from the product through $n=6400$.}

\paragraph{Saddle diagnostics.}
The exact saddle is found by Newton iteration,
$t_{\rm new}=t+(-F'(t)-n)/B(t)$, with a positivity safeguard.  The first four
cumulants use $b_2=p(1-p)$, $b_3=p(1-p)(1-2p)$ and
$b_4=p(1-p)\{1-6p(1-p)\}$.  For $\beta\ge0$ the explicit tail bound
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:48:eq:numerictail}
 \sum_{k>k_0}k^\beta e^{-tk}
 \le e^tt^{-\beta-1}\Gamma(\beta+1,t(k_0+1))
\end{equation}
follows by integrating $(u+1)^\beta e^{-tu}$ over $u\ge k_0$.  It controls
truncation of the log product, mean, variance, and fixed-order cumulants after
multiplication by a suitable polynomial constant.  This addresses the infinite
sums, but the implementation does not turn floating-point rounding into
certified interval arithmetic.

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering\small
\begin{tabular}{r r r r}
\toprule
$n$ & $\log a(n)$ & Gaussian relative error & Corrected relative error\\
\midrule
100  & 31.57034412  & $1.096\cdot10^{-2}$ & $1.733\cdot10^{-4}$\\
400  & 80.97262500  & $4.427\cdot10^{-3}$ & $1.377\cdot10^{-5}$\\
1600 & 197.60929471 & $1.849\cdot10^{-3}$ & $2.527\cdot10^{-6}$\\
6400 & 467.20404426 & $7.888\cdot10^{-4}$ & $4.704\cdot10^{-7}$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Computed diagnostics for A022629 ($\alpha=1$), source 48.  Relative
error means approximation divided by the exact integer coefficient, minus one.
The corrected approximation includes the two cumulant terms of
\eqref{dpn:eq:E1}.  Calculations used 45 decimal digits.}
\label{dpn:48:tab:numerics}
\end{table}

At $n=6400$, the bare logarithmic core underestimates $\log a(n)$ by about
$45.36$.  Adding the first four inverse-logarithmic terms instead
overestimates it by about $3.55$.  Thus that four-term logarithmic
approximation is still poor as a \emph{relative coefficient} approximation at
this size, despite being a valid asymptotic expansion; the corrected
exact-saddle formula has relative error approximately $4.7\cdot10^{-7}$.  This
is why Theorems~\ref{dpn:thm:log} and~\ref{dpn:thm:saddle} are stated
separately.  The recorded file \file{data/48-a022629-numerical_checks.json}
gives the saddle, variance, cumulant correction, mean residual, cutoff, and
tail estimate as well as the errors in Table~\ref{dpn:48:tab:numerics}.  Its
decimal values are computational evidence only; the exact integers and
symbolic polynomial checks are exact computations.  No Lean executable was
run for either source.
\mergenote{The $n=100$ row agrees with source 40's $\alpha=1$ row in
Section~\ref{dpn:sec:checks} ($1/0.989159702-1\approx1.096\cdot10^{-2}$,
$1/0.999826713-1\approx1.733\cdot10^{-4}$), and source 48's saddle at $n=100$
agrees with \file{data/40-norm-moments-run_alpha1.txt} to all printed
digits.}

\section{Further research questions and proposed projects}\label{dpn:sec:future}
The proved results leave several substantial and concrete directions.

\paragraph{1. An effective multi-saddle expansion.}
Locate the secondary saddles near $2\pi\ell/M$, compute their phases and
Gaussian factors, and sum the relevant arcs with a uniform remainder. Theorem
\ref{dpn:thm:resonance} supplies the first exponential action but not the full
contribution. The moderate-$n$ failures for $\alpha=3,4,5$ make this a practical
as well as theoretical priority.
\writenoted{Answered at leading order in the critical window of growing powers
only (Section~\ref{dpn:ct:sec:theta}, source 100-93): there the satellites near
$2\pi j/M$ are located, their phases and Gaussian factors computed, and all
cells summed, with relative error $o(1)$ but no effective remainder.  At fixed
$\alpha$ the project stays open; Section~\ref{dpn:jh:sec:numerics} records an
uncertified cell-by-cell decomposition of the residuals at $\alpha=2,\dots,5$.}

\paragraph{2. A complete two-scale coefficient transseries.}
Refine the continuum-to-lattice passage sufficiently to retain endpoint terms,
Euler--Maclaurin corrections, the central determinant, and the resonance
sectors in one consistent normalization. Determine which of these expansions
are Gevrey and whether optimal truncation produces a useful exponential
improvement. The present all-logarithmic series alone does not settle this.
\writenoted{Source 100-89 supplies the endpoint and Euler--Maclaurin part in
one normalization: the lattice-minus-integral difference is smooth with
explicit coefficients (Theorem~\ref{dpn:rb:thm:smooth}), and the relative
expansion keeps the exact integral and finitely many of them
(Theorem~\ref{dpn:rb:thm:allorders}).  The resonance sectors, the Gevrey
question and optimal truncation remain open (question~100.1).}

\paragraph{3. The crossover as the tilt vanishes.}
The fixed-$\alpha$ expansion is singular as $\alpha\downarrow0$, while the
unweighted distinct-partition count has a different growth law. A natural
crossover parameter is $\alpha\log K$. Derive a uniform family of saddle
profiles when this parameter stays bounded, and match it to both the
unweighted regime and the fixed-positive-tilt regime. The shrinking-tilt
analysis in Gikunda's thesis provides an important comparison point
\cite{gikunda}, but matching the full regimes still requires work.

\paragraph{4. Growing moments and concentration near maximal norm.}
Allow $\alpha=\alpha(n)\to\infty$. The fluctuation width $H$ and resonance
action show that several thresholds can occur before the model collapses onto
maximal-norm partitions. Determine the sharp parameter ranges for a local
limit theorem, for single-saddle dominance, and for localization near an
extremal partition. The fixed-$\alpha$ proof does not justify substituting an
arbitrary growing parameter.
\writenoted{Source 100-93 finds one sharp range: for $\alpha\asymp M^{1/3}/\log M$
($\Lambda=\alpha(s-1)/M^{1/3}$ in a compact subset of $(0,\infty)$) a local
theorem holds with a theta multiplier, and single-saddle dominance fails
(Theorem~\ref{dpn:ct:thm:theta}, Corollary~\ref{dpn:ct:cor:obstruction}).
Whether single-saddle dominance holds for every $\alpha=o(M^{1/3}/\log M)$, and
the localization near maximal-norm partitions as $\Lambda\to\infty$, stay open
(questions 100-93.4 and 100-93.5).}

\paragraph{5. The joint law of the largest several parts.}
The independent tail calculation suggests a Poisson process with intensity
$e^{-x}\dd x$ for the rescaled upper parts. Prove its conditioned point-process
limit and obtain joint distributions of the largest $j$ parts, including
second-order corrections to their centering. The maximum theorem is the
one-dimensional starting point, not a substitute for tightness of the process.

\paragraph{6. Fluctuations of the number of parts and of the log norm.}
A multivariate conditioned local limit theorem should describe the number of
occupied sites and the logarithm of the norm under fixed positive tilt.
Conditioning removes a covariance direction and can cancel a leading
variance term; computing the surviving scale requires more than plugging the
unconditioned variance into a Gaussian formula.
\writenoted{In the critical window of Section~\ref{dpn:ct:sec:theta} the
variance of the number of parts at least two, conditioned (by regression) on
the size, is $Q_\perp\sim\pi^2H^3/(3M^2)$, while its unconditioned variance is
$\sim H$: the cancellation anticipated here, exact in \eqref{dpn:ct:eq:Qexact}
and proved for growing $\alpha$ only.  The fixed-$\alpha$ question is open.}

\paragraph{7. More general weights.}
Replace $k^\alpha$ by $k^\alpha(\log k)^\beta$ away from the harmless small
indices, or by a smooth regularly varying weight. Identify hypotheses that
preserve a single large crossing, the logistic boundary, and a computable
inverse expansion. Arithmetic thinning of the allowed part sizes would also
require a replacement for the consecutive-block lemma and may introduce
persistent lattice obstructions.
\writenotec{A different generalization, integer slot multiplicities
$k^\beta$ with the weight $k^\alpha$ kept, is proved by source 77-51
(Theorem~\ref{dpn:fp:thm:family}); the weight perturbations asked for here
remain open.}

\paragraph{8. Certified numerics and formal verification.}
Give explicit constants in the Fourier bounds, add interval-enclosed saddle
solving, and certify a chosen relative-error target at a stated $n$ and
$\alpha$. For Lean, a sensible modular route is: the formal Euler product and
recurrence; the Bernoulli coefficient identity; the consecutive-block
inequality; an abstract triangular-array local-limit theorem; and finally the
boundary smoothing lemma. The algebraic generation of $P_j$ and the inverse
coefficients can be separately certified by finite polynomial identities.
This would connect directly to the repository's transseries infrastructure
without pretending that formal algebra alone proves analytic asymptotics.

\subsection{Source 48's proposed projects \src{48}}
Source 48 lists eight next steps, stated for A022629 and the real family.
They are proposed research tasks, not claims that the corresponding problems
are all globally open.  Its numbers are kept; overlaps with the projects above
are marked.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textbf{48.\arabic*}, leftmargin=*]
\item \textbf{Resolve all subdominant asymptotic blocks} (overlaps project 2).
The upper-transition calculation determines every $K/r^j$ term.  Determine the
smaller contributions from the lower endpoint and the sum--integral
discrepancy, including any powers of $K$, logarithms, and constants that
appear for different $\alpha$.  Prove a fully explicit multiplicative
equivalent with no implicit exact saddle sums.  Distinguish this task from
merely computing more terms in \eqref{dpn:eq:logall}.
\writenoted{Source 100-89 determines the lower-endpoint and sum--integral
part: $\Xi_\alpha(t)=\sum_{r\le D}\xi_r(\alpha)t^r+O(t^{D+1})$ with explicit
$\xi_r$ (Section~\ref{dpn:rb:sec:boundary}), and
Corollary~\ref{dpn:rb:cor:first} is a multiplicative equivalent with no lattice
sum, though it keeps the exact integral and its saddle.  A fully explicit
equivalent without saddle quantities remains open.}
\item \textbf{The transition as $\alpha\downarrow0$} (overlaps project 3).
At $\alpha=0$ the model is ordinary distinct-part enumeration; for every fixed
$\alpha>0$ the leading logarithm has the different scale $\alpha K\log K$.
Develop uniform asymptotics in a double limit, for example when $\alpha\log n$
stays bounded.  The present proof explicitly does not cover this regime, and
the change in the active interval makes it a useful test case for uniform
asymptotic methods.
\item \textbf{The number of parts after conditioning} (overlaps project 6).
The finite-dimensional occupation profile is simple, but conditioning on total
size creates long-range correlations.  Determine the mean, variance, and
limiting law of the total number of parts to several orders.  A naive
unconditioned variance calculation is not sufficient because number and size
are strongly correlated in the narrow transition region.
\item \textbf{Joint extremes and point processes} (overlaps project 5).
Upgrade Corollary~\ref{dpn:48:thm:gumbel} to joint laws of the largest several
parts and convergence of the rescaled tail point process.  Prove quantitative
error bounds, uniform tail estimates, and asymptotics of the expectation and
variance of the largest part.  Distributional convergence alone does not
justify taking expectations.
\item \textbf{Stable weight perturbations} (overlaps project 7).
Replace $k^\alpha$ by $k^\alpha(\log k)^\beta$ or by
$k^\alpha\exp(c_1/k+c_2/k^2+\cdots)$, with a positive convention for small
$k$.  Determine which coefficients and extreme-value centring terms are
universal and which remember the perturbation.  Establish explicit uniform
hypotheses rather than assuming regular variation is automatically enough.
\item \textbf{Optimal saddle remainder scales.}
The absolute cumulant bound gives the safe parameter $s/M$.  Signed cumulant
cancellations can make actual errors much smaller, as
Table~\ref{dpn:48:tab:numerics} suggests.  Determine the sharp first relative
correction, its complete expansion, and the optimal error after each
correction.  This would improve both numerical evaluation and rigorous
inverse brackets.
\writenote{Partly answered by Section~\ref{dpn:jh:sec:sharp}
(sources 75-01 and 75-06): the sharp signed scales
$\kappa_j\sim\frac{j!}2M^{j+1}/(\alpha s)^{j-1}$, the sharp first correction
$\mathcal E_1\sim-3/(8\alpha Ms)$, and the error $O((\alpha Ms)^{-J-1})$ after
each correction, with the threshold localization of
Theorem~\ref{dpn:jh:thm:threshold}.  A complete expansion of $\mathcal E_1$ in
$1/s$, and optimality beyond these orders, remain open.}
\item \textbf{Effective inversion and formalization} (overlaps project 8).
Turn the asymptotic $O$ constants into explicit bounds, combine them with
finite exact verification, and produce a certified algorithm for
$N_\alpha(Y)$.  A suitable ProveIt formalization can separate finite
coefficient algebra, the positive product measure, the block minor-arc
estimate, remainder transport, and integer-threshold bracketing.  None of
those formalization tasks should be replaced by unchecked numerical fits.
\writenote{Theorem~\ref{dpn:jh:thm:threshold} \src{75-01} localizes
$N_\alpha(Y)$ to within $1+o(1)$ of an exact-saddle inverse, but with implicit
constants; the explicit bounds asked for here are still open.}
\item \textbf{OEIS and literature follow-through.}
Submit a concise, independently reviewed proof of \eqref{dpn:eq:conjecture}
and the first sharper correction to the OEIS editors.  Continue the
literature audit for products with coefficients \emph{inside} their factors,
rather than mistaking $\prod(1+k^\alpha q^k)$ for $\prod(1+q^k)^{k^\alpha}$.
Compare any earlier theorem at the level of hypotheses and error terms before
making a priority claim.
\writenotec{The intermediate products $\prod(1+k^\alpha q^k)^{k^\beta}$ are
treated in Section~\ref{dpn:fp:sec:family} (source 77-51), which also cites
Naranjo and Ram\'irez \cite{NR}; the broader literature audit asked for here
is still open.}
\end{enumerate}

\section{Conclusion}
The five checked OEIS conjectures share a single analytic mechanism. Filling
parts up to a moving boundary produces the dominant factorial-scale entropy;
smoothing that boundary produces the $\pi^2$ correction and its all-orders
inverse-logarithmic successors. A conditioned Bernoulli model supplies the
local probability that converts this entropy into an actual coefficient,
and also gives logistic occupation and a Gumbel extreme. Lambert-$W$
inversion turns the same expansion into a quantitative threshold law.

The resulting package contains explicit proofs rather than numerical
extrapolation. It also records an important limitation: central asymptotics
can converge extremely slowly for higher moments because noncentral
resonances are only suppressed on the scale $\exp[-C_\alpha K/(\log K)^3]$.
That proved scale and the visible finite-size discrepancies identify a
well-defined next research problem: a phase-sensitive, exponentially
improved multi-saddle expansion.

Source 48 reaches the same conclusions for A022629 by partly different routes:
an elementary proof with a stronger additive remainder, an explicit
differential operator that generates the entire dominant logarithmic block by
finite exact algebra, a Lambert-based inverse for the integer threshold, a
precise comparison with the largest individual partition weight, and a Gumbel
law centred explicitly in $\log\sqrt{2n}$.  The remaining
distinctions---formal versus convergent series, logarithmic versus relative
accuracy, and proved mathematics versus formal-machine verification---are
essential parts of the result rather than editorial qualifications
\src{48}.

\mergenoteb{Sources 75-01 and 75-06 reach the same conclusions a third and a
fourth time and add what follows: the sharp saddle scale and threshold
(Section~\ref{dpn:jh:sec:sharp}), the eventual shape of the sequence
(Section~\ref{dpn:jh:sec:shape}), and the convolution powers
(Section~\ref{dpn:cp:sec:powers}).}
\mergenotec{Source 77-51 reaches them a fifth time and adds the slot
multiplicities $k^\beta$ and A266891 (Section~\ref{dpn:fp:sec:b77}).}
\mergenoted{Sources 100-82 and 100-89 reach them a sixth and a seventh time
and identify the lattice-minus-integral sector (Section~\ref{dpn:rb:sec:b100});
source 100-93 shows that once $\alpha$ grows like $M^{1/3}/\log M$ the
resonances of Section~\ref{dpn:sec:resonance} become part of the leading
order (Section~\ref{dpn:ct:sec:theta}).}

\section{Addendum (batch 75): two further independent sources}\label{dpn:sec:b75}
Batch 75 of ProveIt's incoming-reports intake delivered two more manuscripts
on the family $\prod_{k\ge1}(1+k^\alpha q^k)$, both dated October 1, 2026 and
both pinned to the ProveIt commit \texttt{29aca108e}.  Neither cites sources 40
or 48, and neither cites the other.
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Source 75-01} (batch-75 manuscript 01), \emph{Norm-weighted
  distinct partitions: A proof of the A022629 growth conjecture, all-order
  asymptotics, Lambert inversion, and eventual Jensen hyperbolicity}: the
  family for real power $s>0$ (here $\alpha$), five printed logarithmic
  coefficients, an exact-saddle expansion with a sharp remainder, two
  inversions, eventual strict log-concavity, fixed-degree Jensen
  hyperbolicity, and exact data to $n=10000$.
\item \textbf{Source 75-06} (batch-75 manuscript 06), \emph{Norm-weighted
  distinct partitions: A022629, all-order expansions, and inversion}: the
  two-parameter family $\prod_{k\ge1}(1+k^rq^k)^s$, real power $r>0$ (here
  $\alpha$) and integer number of layers $s\ge1$ (here $\mu$), eight printed
  logarithmic and seven printed inverse coefficients, the relative saddle
  expansion, and logistic and Gumbel laws, all for every number of layers.
\end{itemize}
These are the third and fourth independent proofs in this report of
Proposition~\ref{dpn:prop:elementary} and
Theorems~\ref{dpn:thm:log}, \ref{dpn:thm:saddle}, \ref{dpn:thm:inverse}
(and, for source 75-06, \ref{dpn:thm:geometry}), with identical coefficients.
Following Section~\ref{dpn:sec:merge}, none of these results is printed
again.  Table~\ref{dpn:tab:b75dup} records where each source proves them;
Section~\ref{dpn:sec:b75routes} keeps the parts of their proofs and remarks
that differ from the routes already printed.  The genuinely new material
follows in Sections~\ref{dpn:jh:sec:sharp} (sharp saddle scales and the
near-integer threshold), \ref{dpn:jh:sec:shape} (log-concavity and Jensen
polynomials) and~\ref{dpn:cp:sec:powers} (convolution powers), and the two
sources' research questions in Section~\ref{dpn:sec:b75questions}.  Their
notation is translated by the second table of Section~\ref{dpn:sec:notation}.
Source 75-01 is not a version of source 48, although their archive names
differ only in letter case (\file{A022629_research_package.zip} and
\file{A022629_Research_Package.zip}): their texts share 0.87\,\% of their
word 8-grams in each direction, and their theorem sets are different.

\paragraph{Stale statements and priority caveats.}
Both pins are the ProveIt commit \texttt{29aca108e} (1 October 2026, 19:03;
full identifier in Appendix~\ref{dpn:app:prov}).
It already contained the archives of sources 40 and 48, unopened, and predates
their placement (\texttt{9df4ba51a}).  Source 75-01's sentence ``A repository
code search for A022629 returned no match'' was accurate for that snapshot and
is stale now: the repository's treatment is this report.  Source 75-06 makes
no such statement, but its repository reading was blind to the same two
archives.  Likewise, the proof
of the five Kote\v{s}ovec conjectures that both sources present as their
principal contribution is, in this report, the third and fourth proof; their
new contributions are those of Sections~\ref{dpn:jh:sec:sharp}--\ref{dpn:cp:sec:powers}.
Both keep explicit priority caveats, which this report keeps.  Source 75-01:
its search ``located no prior proof of these precise fixed-$s$ statements'',
which is ``not a claim that no such proof exists anywhere, nor a claim that a
longstanding major conjecture in partition theory has been settled''; Kumar
and Rana's 2026 article on statistical inequalities for norm moments
\cite{kumar} ``should also be considered in any eventual publication-level
priority review''; the Hermite-limit mechanism is Griffin, Ono, Rolen and
Zagier's \cite{gorz}, and no new general Hermite-limit theorem is claimed.
Source 75-06: its targeted search ``does not establish bibliographic
priority''; Lagarias and Sun's reciprocal-supernorm ensemble \cite{lagarias}
is a different ensemble and not a proof of these results; the conditioning
method is Fristedt's \cite{fristedt}, applied with every estimate proved for
this model.  Source 75-06 read the repository's \file{Analysis/Transseries}
README, and source 75-01 the repository map and the Lambert-$W$ drafts of the
Fabius tree \cite{proveit01}; neither uses a repository theorem as a premise, and neither
claims Lean or Rocq verification.

\subsection{Second routes and remarks that differ}\label{dpn:sec:b75routes}
\paragraph{Analytic inverse and Cauchy bounds \src{75-01}.}
Source 75-01's proof of Lemma~\ref{dpn:lem:smoothing} controls the Taylor
remainder of the boundary inverse by analyticity rather than by repeated use
of \eqref{dpn:eq:Urec}.  The local inverse $u_s(v)$ of $h_s$ is analytic on
$|v|\le cs$ for a fixed small $c>0$: either by the inverse function theorem with
uniform bounds on $u\in[1/2,3/2]$, or by solving $u-1=(v+\log u)/s$ by
contraction on a fixed complex disk around $1$.  Cauchy's estimates then bound
the remainder after $m$ Taylor terms of $u_s'$ by a constant times
$s^{-m-1}|v|^m$, and truncating the $v$-integral at $cs$ loses an
exponentially small quantity.  Derivatives in $s$ are handled by
differentiating the localized integral and its explicit remainder before
estimating, so no differentiability of an unqualified remainder is assumed.

\paragraph{The mean without differentiating a remainder \src{75-06}.}
Source 75-06 proves the mean relation \eqref{dpn:eq:Q} directly, as source 48
does (Lemma~\ref{dpn:48:lem:sommerfeld}): the odd kernel
$(1+e^U)^{-1}-\ind_{U<0}$ has moments $2(2j+1)!\,\eta(2j+2)$, and the identity
$\partial_tX=-XX'$ for the upper inverse $X(U)$ of $tx-\alpha\log x$ shows that
the directly justified moment series equals the formal negative $t$-derivative
of the boundary expansion.  Its first terms reproduce
\eqref{dpn:48:eq:Bfirst}, including the vanishing coefficient of $s^{-4}$ in
$Q_\alpha(s)-1$ (equivalently of $s^{-2}$ in $\Bop$), which source 75-06 calls
``a useful algebraic check''.

\paragraph{Coefficient structure \src{75-06}.}
The formal construction gives $P_j(0)=0$ and
$\deg_cP_j\le\lfloor(j+1)/2\rfloor$.  Source 75-06 tracks this by assigning
degree $2$ to $c$: a term of $c$-degree $a$ in the normalized entropy cannot
begin before $r^{-(2a-1)}$; the initial inverse-derivative series has that
filtration, and multiplication, composition and the square-root reversion
preserve it.  These identities in a formal parameter do not make the analytic
theorem uniform as $\alpha\downarrow0$.  $P_1,\dots,P_8$ attain the bound
(checked by the intake).

\paragraph{Prefactor bookkeeping \src{75-06}.}
At the saddle,
$\log a_\alpha(n)=F(t_n)+nt_n-\tfrac12\log(2\pi B)+O(s/M)$, so the probability
prefactor contributes only $O(s)$ to the logarithm, below every scale
$K/r^R$ (source 75-06's Corollary~5.3; compare \eqref{dpn:eq:logsaddle}).

\paragraph{Two finite-size readings of the logarithmic block.}
For $\alpha=1$, $n=10000$, source 75-01 records
$\log a_1(10000)=613.21132139736055145\ldots$.  The leading expression
$K(r-1)$ is low by about $54.3504$; adding the first inverse-logarithmic
correction leaves about $7.37119$; the five-term truncation is high by about
$4.65395$.  Source 75-06 records the normalized first correction
$\mathcal R(n)=(r/K)(\log a_1(n)-K(r-1))$, whose limit is $\pi^2/6\approx1.6449$
by \eqref{dpn:48:eq:firstlimit}: exact values give $\mathcal R(100)\approx1.5450$,
$\mathcal R(1000)\approx1.8190$ and $\mathcal R(10000)\approx1.9030$, and
saddle evaluations at $n=10^6,10^8,10^{10}$ give about $1.8800$, $1.8274$ and
$1.7900$.  The last three are saddle estimates, not comparisons with exact
coefficients.  Both sources draw the conclusion of
Section~\ref{dpn:sec:checks}: the logarithmic block is not a high-accuracy
numerical exponent at these sizes, and plots alone can obscure the correct
next coefficient.

\section{Sharp saddle scales and near-integer thresholds}\label{dpn:jh:sec:sharp}
This section is source 75-01's Sections~3, 4 and~6.2, translated into the
notation of this report, with source 75-06's Proposition~5.4 as a second proof
of the cumulant scales.  Throughout, $t$, $s$, $M$ and $H$ are as in
\eqref{dpn:eq:phase}, and, beside the safe parameter $\eps=s/M$ of
\eqref{dpn:eq:normcumulants}, we use the \emph{sharp} parameter
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:jh:eq:epsh}
 \epsh=\frac1{\alpha Ms}=\frac{\eps}{\alpha s^2}.
\end{equation}

\subsection{Signed cumulant scales}
\begin{lemma}[Signed cumulant scales; sources 75-01 and 75-06]\label{dpn:jh:lem:cumulants}
For fixed $\alpha>0$, as $s\to\infty$ with $t=\alpha s/M$,
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:jh:eq:cumscale}
 \kappa_1\sim\frac{M^2}{2},\qquad
 \kappa_j\sim\frac{j!}{2}\,\frac{M^{j+1}}{(\alpha s)^{j-1}}\quad(j\ge2\text{ fixed}).
\end{equation}
Consequently, at the exact saddle,
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:jh:eq:normalized}
 \frac{\kappa_j}{B^{j/2}}=O_{\alpha,j}\bigl(\epsh^{(j-2)/2}\bigr),\qquad j\ge3.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}[Proof \src{75-01}]
Sum--integral comparison for $x^jb_j(p(x))$, where
$p(x)=(1+e^{tx-\alpha\log x})^{-1}$, has error $O_{\alpha,j}(M^j)$:
differentiate the summand in $x$, split at $M/2$ and $2M$, and use exponential
decay beyond $2M$.  For $j\ge2$, $b_j$ has a factor $p(1-p)$ and its other
factors are bounded on $[0,1]$.  For $j=1$ the bulk integral is
$\int_0^Mx\dd x=M^2/2$ up to lower-order errors, which is
\eqref{dpn:eq:meanleading}.

For $j\ge2$ the upper moving edge supplies the leading term.  On $x\ge M/2$
the substitution $y=tx-\alpha\log x$ is increasing for large $s$; let $x(y)$
be its inverse there and $w(y)=e^y/(1+e^y)^2$.  Since $p=(1+e^y)^{-1}$ and
$\mathrm dp/\mathrm dy=-p(1-p)$, the recursion
$b_{j+1}(p)=p(1-p)b_j'(p)$ gives
\[
 b_j\bigl((1+e^y)^{-1}\bigr)=(-1)^{j-2}w^{(j-2)}(y),\qquad j\ge2.
\]
Integration by parts $j-2$ times transfers all these derivatives to
$x(y)^jx'(y)$, and the signs cancel.  The boundary terms at $M/2$ are
exponentially smaller in $s$ than the edge term, and those at infinity
vanish.  For bounded $y$,
\[
 x(y)=M\{1+O_{\alpha,y}(s^{-1})\},\qquad
 x'(y)=\frac M{\alpha s}\{1+O_{\alpha,y}(s^{-1})\}.
\]
Repeated differentiation, using $x'=1/(t-\alpha/x)$, gives
\[
 \frac{\mathrm d^{j-2}}{\mathrm dy^{j-2}}\bigl\{x(y)^jx'(y)\bigr\}
 =\frac{j!}{2}\,\frac{M^{j+1}}{(\alpha s)^{j-1}}\bigl(1+o_{\alpha,j}(1)\bigr):
\]
the leading operator is $t^{-1}\mathrm d/\mathrm dx$, and differentiating its
denominator costs a relative $O_{\alpha,j}(s^{-1})$.  The kernel $w$ has
integral one and exponential tails; localization first to $|y|\le C\log s$,
then $C\to\infty$, justifies integrating the expansion.

The region below $M/2$ contributes $O_{\alpha,j}(M^{j+1-c_\alpha}s^{C_j})$ for
some $c_\alpha>0$ (split again at $x=M^{1/2}$ and use $p(1-p)$ above it), which
is smaller than $M^{j+1}/s^{j-1}$, and so is the sum--integral error.  This
proves \eqref{dpn:jh:eq:cumscale}, and dividing by $B^{j/2}$, with
$B=\kappa_2\sim M^3/(\alpha s)$, gives \eqref{dpn:jh:eq:normalized}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Second proof of \eqref{dpn:jh:eq:cumscale} \src{75-06}]
After the same reduction to the upper branch and the same $j-2$ integrations
by parts, the integrand is
$\frac{\mathrm d^{j-2}}{\mathrm dy^{j-2}}(x(y)^jx'(y))\,w(y)$.  On bounded
$y$-intervals $x\sim M$ and $x'\sim A:=M/(\alpha s)$, and every occurrence of a
higher derivative of $x$ costs a further factor $O(1/s)$.  So the leading term
comes from the affine function $x=M+Ay$:
\[
 \frac{\mathrm d^{j-2}}{\mathrm dy^{j-2}}\bigl((M+Ay)^jA\bigr)\Big|_{y=0}
 =\frac{j!}{2}\,M^2A^{j-1}
 =\frac{j!}{2}\,\frac{M^{j+1}}{(\alpha s)^{j-1}}.
\]
Exponential decay of $w$, whose integral is $1$, permits restriction to a
growing bounded neighbourhood followed by dominated estimates.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Why the absolute cumulant bound is not sharp; source 75-01]\label{dpn:jh:rem:naive}
Taking absolute values before the integrations by parts loses the
cancellations in $b_j$ and gives $M^{j+1}/s$, which is
\eqref{dpn:eq:mombound}, instead of $M^{j+1}/s^{j-1}$.  The signed estimate is
what identifies $\epsh=(\alpha Ms)^{-1}$, rather than $\eps=s/M$, as the
algebraic saddle parameter.  The weaker absolute estimate is still the right
tool for a high-order Taylor remainder, but not for the retained coefficients.
\end{remark}

\subsection{The sharp exact-saddle remainder}
\begin{theorem}[Sharp exact-saddle expansion; source 75-01]\label{dpn:jh:thm:saddle}
For fixed $\alpha>0$ and every fixed integer $J\ge0$, at the exact saddle
$t=t_n$,
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:jh:eq:rel}
 a_\alpha(n)=\frac{\exp(F(t_n)+nt_n)}{\sqrt{2\pi B}}
 \left(1+\sum_{\ell=1}^{J}\mathcal E_\ell+O_{\alpha,J}\bigl(\epsh^{J+1}\bigr)\right),
\end{equation}
with $\mathcal E_\ell$ as in \eqref{dpn:eq:edgepoly}.  Here
$\mathcal E_\ell=O_{\alpha,\ell}(\epsh^\ell)$ and
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:jh:eq:E1lead}
 \mathcal E_1\sim-\frac{3}{8\alpha Ms}\sim-\frac{3}{8\alpha Kr}.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}[Proof \src{75-01}]
The Fourier reduction and the bounds \eqref{dpn:eq:centralbound} and
\eqref{dpn:eq:noncentral} are those of Section~\ref{dpn:sec:llt}; outside
$|\theta|\le c_0/M$ the integral is smaller than $B^{-1/2}\epsh^A$ for every
fixed $A$.  This is an eventual estimate: its unspecified $\alpha$-dependent
constant is not a useful finite-$n$ accuracy certificate.

In the central interval put $x=\theta\sqrt B$.  The exponent is
$-x^2/2+\sum_{j=3}^{R-1}\kappa_j(\ii x)^j/(j!B^{j/2})$ plus a Taylor
remainder.  For each fixed $R\ge2$ the absolute $R$th derivative on
$|\theta|\le c_0/M$ is $O_{\alpha,R}(M^{R+1}/s)$: for $k\le2M$ the complex
Bernoulli denominators stay comparable to their positive values, and for
$k>2M$ the odds are small and exponentially decreasing.  Hence the normalized
remainder is at most $C_{\alpha,R}(s/M)^{R/2-1}|x|^R$.  For the expansion
through order $J$ choose $R=2J+6$; then $(s/M)^{J+2}=o_\alpha(\epsh^{J+1})$,
since $s^{2J+3}/M\to0$.

Restrict first to $|x|\le\sqrt{C\log M}$.  By \eqref{dpn:jh:eq:normalized}
and the remainder bound, the exponential may be expanded with an integrable
Gaussian majorant.  Give the $j$th normalized cumulant weight $j-2$: a monomial
of weight $w$ is $O(\epsh^{w/2})$ times a fixed polynomial in $x$.  Retain
weights through $2J+1$.  Odd weights give odd polynomials, which integrate to
zero on the symmetric interval; the first discarded even weight is $2J+2$,
which contributes $O_{\alpha,J}(\epsh^{J+1})$.  Expanding sufficiently many
ordinary Taylor terms makes this a finite remainder estimate.  The rest of
$|\theta|\le c_0/M$ is algebraically negligible by
\eqref{dpn:eq:centralbound}, for $C$ large.  The Gaussian moments
$(2\pi)^{-1/2}\int e^{-x^2/2}(\ii x)^T\dd x=(-1)^{T/2}(T-1)!!$ for even $T$
give exactly \eqref{dpn:eq:edgepoly}, and the bound on $\mathcal E_\ell$
follows from its weight.  Finally, Lemma~\ref{dpn:jh:lem:cumulants} gives
\[
 \mathcal E_1=\frac{\kappa_4}{8B^2}-\frac{5\kappa_3^2}{24B^3}
 \sim\frac{12}{8\alpha Ms}-\frac{45}{24\alpha Ms}=-\frac{3}{8\alpha Ms}.
\]
\end{proof}

Theorem~\ref{dpn:jh:thm:saddle} sharpens Theorem~\ref{dpn:thm:saddle}: after
the corrections $\mathcal E_1,\dots,\mathcal E_{J-1}$ the relative error is
$O(\epsh^J)=O((\alpha Ms)^{-J})$ rather than $O((s/M)^J)$, a gain of
$(\alpha s^2)^J$, and the first correction is negative, of exact order
$3/(8\alpha Kr)$, so the bare Gaussian factor overestimates $a_\alpha(n)$.  At
one layer, source 75-06's Proposition~5.4 states the first refinement
\[
 a_\alpha(n)=\frac{\exp(F(t_n)+nt_n)}{\sqrt{2\pi B}}
 \left(1-\frac{3+o(1)}{8\alpha Ms}\right),
\]
deduced from its remainder $O(\eps^2)=o(\epsh)$; Section~\ref{dpn:cp:sec:powers}
gives its version for $\mu$ layers.  The second correction $\mathcal E_2$ is
\eqref{dpn:eq:E2} (source 75-01 prints the same display), and source 75-01's
program computes $\mathcal E_3$ from the general formula
\eqref{dpn:eq:edgepoly} rather than from a hard-coded expression.

\writenote{This theorem and Lemma~\ref{dpn:jh:lem:cumulants} partly answer
source 48's project~48.6, ``Optimal saddle remainder scales''
(Section~\ref{dpn:sec:future}): they give the sharp signed scales, the sharp
first relative correction, and the error order $\epsh^{J+1}$ after each
correction.  Neither source gives the complete expansion of $\mathcal E_1$ in
powers of $1/s$, nor any optimality statement beyond these orders; that part
of the question stays open.}

\subsection{Saddle accuracy at finite size}\label{dpn:jh:sec:numerics}
Let $G_J(n)=G\sum_{\ell=0}^J\mathcal E_\ell(t_n)$ with $\mathcal E_0=1$ and
$G$ as in Section~\ref{dpn:sec:checks}.  Table~\ref{dpn:jh:tab:errors} gives
source 75-01's signed relative errors $G_J(n)/a_1(n)-1$, computed with $40$
decimal digits in the finite sums, together with the two extra rows of source
75-06.  A $60$-digit rerun at $n=10000$ changes the recorded errors by at most
$7.0\times10^{-38}$; this stability test is not an interval-arithmetic
certificate, and neither source's floating-point computation is
directed-rounding certified.
\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering\small
\begin{tabular}{rrrrr}
\toprule

200\textsuperscript{*}  & $6.90519\cdot10^{-3}$ & $2.59790\cdot10^{-5}$ & & \\
500   & $3.84109\cdot10^{-3}$ & $1.07637\cdot10^{-5}$ & $1.45599\cdot10^{-7}$ & $8.06153\cdot10^{-8}$\\
1000  & $2.47981\cdot10^{-3}$ & $4.50477\cdot10^{-6}$ & $1.57836\cdot10^{-8}$ & $-2.77601\cdot10^{-9}$\\
2000\textsuperscript{*} & $1.60998\cdot10^{-3}$ & $1.92292\cdot10^{-6}$ & & \\
2500  & $1.40254\cdot10^{-3}$ & $1.46485\cdot10^{-6}$ & $3.61741\cdot10^{-9}$ & $1.17092\cdot10^{-11}$\\
5000  & $9.16761\cdot10^{-4}$ & $6.32944\cdot10^{-7}$ & $1.05783\cdot10^{-9}$ & $2.22571\cdot10^{-12}$\\
10000 & $6.02038\cdot10^{-4}$ & $2.75808\cdot10^{-7}$ & $3.11999\cdot10^{-10}$ & $4.66600\cdot10^{-13}$\\

\caption{Signed relative errors $G_J(n)/a_1(n)-1$ for A022629 ($\alpha=1$),
