For larger powers the picture changes.  At $\alpha=2$, $n=2500$, source 75-01
finds $G_J/a_2-1\approx-2.2561\cdot10^{-4}$, $-2.2601\cdot10^{-4}$ and
$-2.2601\cdot10^{-4}$ for $J=1,2,3$; at $\alpha=3$, $n=2500$, the errors for
$J=0,1,2,3$ are about $0.05070$, $0.05016$, $0.05016$ and $0.05016$.  Source
75-01 reads this as slow convergence (``Higher powers can converge slowly'')
and states that the residuals ``have not been decomposed here into separate
oscillatory sectors''.
\mergenoteb{The residual is independent of $J$ once $\mathcal E_1$ is included,
so it is not a truncation effect of the central Edgeworth series, which the
corrections already resolve to $10^{-9}$.  It is the noncentral contribution
that Theorem~\ref{dpn:thm:resonance} and Remark~\ref{dpn:rem:effective}
identify, invisible to every central correction.  It agrees in sign and
order of magnitude with source 40's rows $\alpha=2,3$ at $n=5000$ in
Section~\ref{dpn:sec:checks} (there $G(1+\mathcal E_1)/a_\alpha-1\approx
-3.6\cdot10^{-5}$ and $+0.040$), whose growth with $\alpha$ the resonance moduli
of that section track.  Neither source computes the phases that would turn
this identification into a formula.}
\writenoted{A decomposition into Fourier cells supports this identification
numerically.  The intake of batch 100 computed $\sqrt{2\pi B}\,\Prob_t(S=n)$ at
the exact saddles of Section~\ref{dpn:sec:checks}, $n=5000$, cell by cell over
$|M\theta-2\pi j|\le\pi$, $|j|\le3$ (composite Gauss--Legendre quadrature of the
exact characteristic function in double precision; uncertified):
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{rrrrrrr}
\toprule
$\alpha$ & $j=0$ & $j=\pm1$ & $j=\pm2$ & $j=\pm3$ & total & ratio of Section~\ref{dpn:sec:checks}\\
\midrule
2 & 0.999509 & $+3.66\cdot10^{-5}$ & $-4.7\cdot10^{-8}$ & $-3.7\cdot10^{-9}$ & 0.999545 & 0.999545\\
3 & 0.999669 & $-0.0383$ & $+2.8\cdot10^{-4}$ & $-2.2\cdot10^{-5}$ & 0.961625 & 0.961626\\
4 & 0.999750 & $+0.2244$ & $+0.0068$ & $-2.7\cdot10^{-4}$ & 1.230648 & 1.230518\\
5 & 0.999800 & $+0.652$ & $-0.080$ & $+0.013$ & 1.584610 & 1.581098\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
At $\alpha=5$ the cells $|j|>3$ are not negligible.  The cell $j=0$ is the
central Edgeworth contribution ($\approx1+\mathcal E_1$); the residuals sit
in the cells around $\pm2\pi/M$, with signs that the modulus of
Theorem~\ref{dpn:thm:resonance} does not give.  This is numerical evidence
only.  Source 100-93's theta formula does not explain it: at fixed $\alpha$ the
lower bulk is not frozen and $\Lambda\to0$, and its formula with the exact
moments gives $1.0000055$, $0.98057$ and $1.19429$ at $\alpha=3,4,5$
(Section~\ref{dpn:ct:sec:relation}).  A formula for these cells is
question~100.6.}

\subsection{Near-integer localization of the threshold}\label{dpn:jh:sec:threshold}
For real $x>0$ let $t(x)$ be the unique solution of $-F'(t(x))=x$, so that
$t_n=t(n)$.  The inverse function theorem gives the exact rules
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:jh:eq:xder}
 \frac{\mathrm dt}{\mathrm dx}=-\frac1B,\qquad
 \frac{\mathrm d\kappa_j(t(x))}{\mathrm dx}=\frac{\kappa_{j+1}}{B}.
\end{equation}
Define the Gaussian inverse
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:jh:eq:psi0}
\begin{aligned}
 \Psi_0(x)&=xt(x)+F(t(x))-\tfrac12\log\bigl(2\pi B(t(x))\bigr),\\
 \Psi_0'(x)&=t(x)-\frac{\kappa_3}{2B^2}
 =t(x)+O_\alpha(M^{-2})\sim\frac{\alpha s}{M}>0,
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
and let $n_*(Y)$ be the unique sufficiently large solution of
$\Psi_0(n_*(Y))=Y$.  As in Theorem~\ref{dpn:thm:inverse}, $N_\alpha(Y)$ is the
least $n$ with $\log a_\alpha(n)\ge Y$ (source 75-01 writes
$\mathcal N_s(A)$ with $A=e^Y$).

\begin{theorem}[Near-integer localization; source 75-01]\label{dpn:jh:thm:threshold}
For fixed $\alpha>0$, as $Y\to\infty$,
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:jh:eq:threshold}
 |N_\alpha(Y)-n_*(Y)|\le1+o(1).
\end{equation}
More precisely, at an exact sequence value, with $s$ taken at $t_n$,
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:jh:eq:atvalue}
 n_*\bigl(\log a_\alpha(n)\bigr)-n=-\frac{3}{8\alpha^2s^2}\bigl(1+o_\alpha(1)\bigr).
\end{equation}
If $\Psi_0$ is replaced by $\Psi_1=\Psi_0+\mathcal E_1(t(x))$, the displacement
at $Y=\log a_\alpha(n)$ is $O_\alpha(M^{-1}s^{-3})$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}[Proof \src{75-01}]
Theorem~\ref{dpn:jh:thm:saddle} with $J=1$ gives
$\log a_\alpha(n)=\Psi_0(n)+\mathcal E_1(t_n)+O_\alpha(\epsh^2)$.  Divide this
logarithmic error by the slope \eqref{dpn:jh:eq:psi0} and apply the mean value
theorem in an $o(1)$ neighbourhood of $n$: the leading displacement is
\[
 \frac{-3/(8\alpha Ms)}{\alpha s/M}=-\frac{3}{8\alpha^2s^2},
\]
which proves \eqref{dpn:jh:eq:atvalue}.  With $\Psi_1$ the logarithmic error is
$O_\alpha(\epsh^2)$, giving $O_\alpha(M^{-1}s^{-3})$ after division by the
slope.  For general $Y$ put $m=N_\alpha(Y)$, so that
$\log a_\alpha(m-1)<Y\le\log a_\alpha(m)$.  Monotonicity of the eventual
inverse $n_*$ places $n_*(Y)$ between $m-1+o(1)$ and $m+o(1)$, proving
\eqref{dpn:jh:eq:threshold}.
\end{proof}

No universal rounding instruction follows from \eqref{dpn:jh:eq:threshold}: a
height arbitrarily close to a coefficient threshold can require deciding on
which side it lies.  An effective finite-input certificate would also need
explicit error constants and validated numerical evaluation; source 75-01's
inverse routine solves the approximating equation, not that certification
problem.
\mergenoteb{The two steps are instances of the transseries-and-inversion
volume \cite{tai}: the passage from the logarithmic residual to the
displacement is its residual-to-root conversion
(\texttt{p0:thm:backward-error}) with slope bounds
$\Psi_0'\asymp\alpha s/M$, and the passage to the integer threshold is its
staircase theorem (\texttt{p0:thm:staircase}, parts (1)--(2)), whose
separation condition is exactly the obstruction to a universal rounding rule
just described.  That separation step is formalized in Lean as
\texttt{Fabius.staircase\_separation} (with \texttt{Fabius.staircase\_ceil})
\cite{proveitLean}; nothing else in this section is formalized.  Compared with
Section~\ref{dpn:sec:inverse}, whose brackets carry the fixed-order
inverse-logarithmic errors plus an $O(1)$ rounding, the exact-saddle inverse
localizes the threshold to $1+o(1)$, at the price of evaluating the exact
saddle.  It does not answer project~48.7, which asks for explicit constants.}
\writenoted{Source 100-89's Theorem~\ref{dpn:rb:thm:inverse} gives, for every
truncation order, exact recovery at sequence values and the threshold off an
$O(t^{J-1})$-neighbourhood of the integers, at the conservative scale; it
agrees that no rounding rule holds for arbitrary thresholds.  In the critical
window of growing powers the theta factor moves the threshold by an
oscillating amount of order at most $M^{2/3}$, and of exactly that order away
from two phases per period (Remark~\ref{dpn:ct:rem:shift}; dated note,
5~October 2026: first written ``of order $M^{2/3}$'').}

For computation one can solve directly in $t$: with
$Z(t)=F(t)+t\kappa_1(t)-\tfrac12\log(2\pi B(t))$ and
$Z'(t)=-tB+\kappa_3/(2B)$, Newton iteration for $Z(t)=Y$, initialized by
\eqref{dpn:eq:Wlead}, returns $n_*=\kappa_1(t)$.  This uses only positive real
sums, not the inversion of a long truncated power series.  At
$Y=\log a_1(10000)$, source 75-01 finds
\[
 n_*(Y)=9999.9838291094228371832520930128038\ldots,
\]
a displacement of about $-0.0161708905772$, which is $1.02387586805\ldots$
times the predicted leading value $-3/(8s^2)$ at the saddle.  This is
consistent with \eqref{dpn:jh:eq:atvalue}, but a single ratio is not a proof
of its limit.  (The recorded saddle tables
\file{data/75-01-jensen-saddle_s2_dps40.csv} and
\file{data/75-01-jensen-saddle_s3_dps40.csv} also list this scaled displacement for $\alpha=2,3$:
about $1.69$ and $0.83$ at $\alpha=2$, $n=1000,2500$, and about $52$ and $118$
at $\alpha=3$, the finite-size residual of Section~\ref{dpn:jh:sec:numerics}
seen through the inverse.)

%% ---------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Eventual log-concavity and Jensen hyperbolicity}\label{dpn:jh:sec:shape}
This section is source 75-01's Section~7.  It needs errors much smaller than
those of the logarithmic block, and differentiating a pointwise $O$-term for
$\log a_\alpha(n)$ would not be legitimate.  Instead a smooth approximant is
built from exact cumulants, and the discrete error is controlled separately.

Let
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:jh:eq:Dr}
 D_r(t)=[z^r]\log\Bigl(1+\sum_{j\ge1}\mathcal E_j(t)z^j\Bigr),\qquad
 \mathcal L_J(x)=\Psi_0(x)+\sum_{r=1}^JD_r(t(x)).
\end{equation}
Thus $D_1=\mathcal E_1$, $D_2=\mathcal E_2-\mathcal E_1^2/2$, and every $D_r$ is
a finite expression.  (Here $r$ is a summation index, not $\log K$.)  For each
fixed $J$, Theorem~\ref{dpn:jh:thm:saddle} gives
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:jh:eq:smootherror}
 \log a_\alpha(n)=\mathcal L_J(n)+O_{\alpha,J}\bigl(\epsh^{J+1}\bigr).
\end{equation}
Equations \eqref{dpn:jh:eq:xder} and \eqref{dpn:jh:eq:cumscale} give, for
fixed $r$ and $k$,
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:jh:eq:Eder}
 \frac{\mathrm d^k}{\mathrm dx^k}\mathcal E_r(t(x)),\
 \frac{\mathrm d^k}{\mathrm dx^k}D_r(t(x))
 =O_{\alpha,r,k}\bigl(\epsh^rM^{-2k}\bigr):
\end{equation}
differentiate each finite cumulant monomial with
$\mathrm d\kappa_j/\mathrm dx=\kappa_{j+1}/B$; every differentiation costs
$O_{\alpha,j}(M^{-2})$.  If $S(x)=xt(x)+F(t(x))$, then $S'=t$ and $S''=-1/B$,
so
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:jh:eq:Lder}
 \mathcal L_J'(x)=t+O_\alpha(M^{-2}),\qquad
 \mathcal L_J''(x)=-B^{-1}+O_\alpha(M^{-4}),\qquad
 \mathcal L_J^{(k)}(x)=O_{\alpha,J,k}(sM^{1-2k})\ (k\ge2),
\end{equation}
locally uniformly when $x$ changes by a bounded amount.

\subsection{Strict log-concavity}
\begin{theorem}[Eventual strict log-concavity; source 75-01]\label{dpn:jh:thm:concavity}
For every fixed $\alpha>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:jh:eq:gap}
 \log\frac{a_\alpha(n)^2}{a_\alpha(n-1)a_\alpha(n+1)}
 \sim\frac1{B(t_n)}\sim\frac{\alpha\log\sqrt{2n}}{(2n)^{3/2}}.
\end{equation}
In particular $a_\alpha(n)$ is strictly log-concave for all sufficiently large
$n$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}[Proof \src{75-01}]
Take $J=3$ in \eqref{dpn:jh:eq:smootherror}; the error is
$O_\alpha(\epsh^4)=o_\alpha(s/M^3)$.  Taylor's formula and
\eqref{dpn:jh:eq:Lder} give
\[
 2\mathcal L_3(n)-\mathcal L_3(n-1)-\mathcal L_3(n+1)
 =B^{-1}+O_\alpha(M^{-4})+O_\alpha(sM^{-7}).
\]
Adding the three discrete errors does not change the leading term, and
$B\sim M^3/(\alpha s)$, $M\sim K$, $s\sim r$ give \eqref{dpn:jh:eq:gap}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Hermite limits of Jensen polynomials}
For integers $d\ge1$ and $n\ge0$ define the Jensen polynomials
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:jh:eq:jensen}
 \mathcal J_\alpha^{d,n}(X)=\sum_{j=0}^d\binom dj a_\alpha(n+j)X^j,
\end{equation}
and the Hermite polynomials $\mathsf H_d$ by
$e^{Xz-z^2}=\sum_{d\ge0}\mathsf H_d(X)z^d/d!$, so that $\mathsf H_0=1$,
$\mathsf H_1=X$, $\mathsf H_2=X^2-2$, $\mathsf H_3=X^3-6X$.  ($\mathsf H_d$ is
not the transition width $H$.)

\begin{theorem}[Fixed-degree Jensen hyperbolicity; source 75-01]\label{dpn:jh:thm:jensen}
For every fixed $\alpha>0$ and fixed integer $d\ge1$, let
$\delta_n=(2B(t_n))^{-1/2}$.  Then, coefficientwise in $X$,
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:jh:eq:hermite}
 \frac{\mathcal J_\alpha^{d,n}\bigl(e^{-t_n}(\delta_nX-1)\bigr)}{a_\alpha(n)\,\delta_n^d}
 \longrightarrow\mathsf H_d(X).
\end{equation}
Consequently $\mathcal J_\alpha^{d,n}$ has $d$ distinct negative real roots for
all sufficiently large $n$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}[Proof \src{75-01}]
Choose $J$ with $J+1>3d/2$; then the discrete error in
\eqref{dpn:jh:eq:smootherror} is $o_\alpha(\delta_n^d)$.  Put
$\tau_n=\mathcal L_J'(n)$ and $\tilde\delta_n^2=-\tfrac12\mathcal L_J''(n)>0$ for
large $n$.  Taylor expansion for each $0\le j\le d$ gives
\[
 \log\frac{a_\alpha(n+j)}{a_\alpha(n)}
 =\tau_nj-\tilde\delta_n^2j^2+\sum_{k=3}^d\gamma_k(n)j^k+o_\alpha(\tilde\delta_n^d),
 \qquad \gamma_k=\frac{\mathcal L_J^{(k)}(n)}{k!},
\]
where the next Taylor derivative contributes $o(\delta_n^d)$, and
$\gamma_k(n)/\tilde\delta_n^k=O_{\alpha,k}((Ms)^{1-k/2})\to0$ for $k\ge3$.  These
are the hypotheses of the Hermite-limit criterion of Griffin, Ono, Rolen and
Zagier \cite[Theorem~3]{gorz}.

The mechanism can also be seen directly.  Expand the exponential of the last
display as a polynomial in $j$ through degree $d$.  The coefficient of $j^k$,
divided by $\tilde\delta_n^k$, tends to the coefficient of $z^k$ in $e^{-z^2}$.
In the normalized Jensen sum the $d$th finite difference annihilates every
polynomial of degree below $d$, and the surviving coefficients combine to
$\mathsf H_d(X)$; the local $o(\tilde\delta_n^d)$ error is exactly what this
cancellation needs.  Finally \eqref{dpn:jh:eq:Lder} gives
$\tilde\delta_n/\delta_n\to1$ and $(\tau_n-t_n)/\delta_n\to0$, so the affine
change of normalized variable tends to the identity and $\tau_n$,
$\tilde\delta_n$ may be replaced by $t_n$, $\delta_n$.  Hermite polynomials
have distinct real roots; on disjoint small intervals around them the endpoint
signs alternate, coefficientwise convergence preserves those signs, and degree
$d$ allows no further roots.  The Jensen polynomial has positive coefficients
and a nonzero constant term, so its real roots are negative.
\end{proof}

Neither the theorem nor its proof gives a bound uniform in $d$.  It does not
assert that every Jensen polynomial of every degree is hyperbolic at one fixed
index, and it should not be conflated with infinite iterated log-concavity of
the sequence.  Source 75-01's contribution here is the verification of the
Griffin--Ono--Rolen--Zagier hypotheses, including an error scale small enough
to survive finite differences; the Hermite-limit mechanism itself is theirs.

\subsection{Exact finite data}
Source 75-01 evaluates the positive product recursion exactly through $n=10000$
for $\alpha=1$ and through $n=2500$ for $\alpha=2,3$, and checks the signed
recurrence \eqref{dpn:eq:exactrec} against it through $n=200$ for each of
$\alpha=1,2,3$.  For $\alpha=1$, among the tested indices $1\le n\le9999$,
strict log-concavity fails exactly at
\[
 n=1,2,4,6,8,11,13,16,17,19,23,26,29,32,34,38,40,41,43,46,53,59,64,76,
\]
so every tested index from $77$ through $9999$ is strictly log-concave.  This
does \emph{not} prove that $77$ is the eventual threshold.  For $\alpha=2$ the
tested range $1\le n\le2499$ has $162$ failures, the last at $n=1033$; for
$\alpha=3$ it has $904$ failures, persisting to $n=2499$, the end of the range
(counts from \file{data/75-01-jensen-exact_audit_s2.json} and
\file{data/75-01-jensen-exact_audit_s3.json}).  These observations caution against uniformity in the
power.

Exact rational real-root counting gives the numbers of real roots of
$\mathcal J_1^{d,n}$ in Table~\ref{dpn:jh:tab:roots}; every polynomial there was
also checked to be squarefree.
\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering\small
\begin{tabular}{rrrrr}
\toprule
$n$ & $d=2$ & $d=3$ & $d=4$ & $d=5$\\
\midrule
100 & 2 & 1 & 0 & 1\\
1000 & 2 & 3 & 4 & 1\\
5000 & 2 & 3 & 4 & 5\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Real roots of the Jensen polynomials $\mathcal J_1^{d,n}$ (source
75-01): the onset of hyperbolicity depends on the degree.}\label{dpn:jh:tab:roots}
\end{table}
\writenote{Source 75-06's research question on shape inequalities
(Section~\ref{dpn:sec:b75questions}, item 75-06.9) asks for eventual
log-concavity; Theorem~\ref{dpn:jh:thm:concavity} answers that part for one
layer.  Ratio monotonicity, higher finite-difference inequalities, and the
case of $\mu\ge2$ layers stay open.}

%% ---------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{Convolution powers}\label{dpn:cp:sec:powers}
This section is source 75-06's two-parameter extension (its Sections~1.2, 2
and~4--9 for more than one layer), translated: source 75-06's power $r$ is
$\alpha$ here and its number of layers $s$ is $\mu$.  For fixed $\alpha>0$ and
a fixed integer $\mu\ge1$ put
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:cp:eq:family}
 A_\alpha(q)^\mu=\prod_{k\ge1}(1+k^\alpha q^k)^\mu=\sum_{n\ge0}a_{\alpha,\mu}(n)q^n,
 \qquad K_\mu=\sqrt{2n/\mu},\qquad r_\mu=\log K_\mu .
\end{equation}
For $\mu=1$ this is $a_\alpha(n)$, with $K_1=K$, $r_1=r$.  An object is an
ordered $\mu$-tuple of distinct partitions of total size $n$, weighted by the
product of their $\alpha$th norm powers; for integral $\alpha$ the weights
count colourings.  The analysis also applies to real $\alpha>0$, when the
coefficients are positive reals.

\paragraph{OEIS identifications \src{75-06}.}
For $\alpha=1$ and $\mu=2,3$ the coefficients are \OEIS{A022630} and
\OEIS{A022631} \cite{oeis22630}; for $\alpha=1$ and fixed $\mu\ge1$ they are
the positive columns of the array \OEIS{A297321} \cite{oeis297321}; for integer
$\alpha\ge1$ and $\mu=1$ they are the positive columns of \OEIS{A292189}
\cite{oeisarray}.  Every statement below is column by column, with the column
index fixed; no uniform assertion is made for a growing index.
\mergenoteb{The intake confirmed on 2 October 2026 that the OEIS names of
A022630 and A022631 are $\prod_{m\ge1}(1+mq^m)^2$ and $(\cdots)^3$, that their
first fifteen terms equal the coefficients computed from the product, and
that A297321's column $k$ is $\prod_{j\ge1}(1+jx^j)^k$.  Source 75-06's
proposed-notes file further says A297321 cross-references its first 32
columns to A022629--A022660; the intake did not check that.}

\paragraph{The $\mu$-layer model.}
Let $X_{k,i}$ ($k\ge1$, $1\le i\le\mu$) be independent Bernoulli variables with
parameter $p_k$ of \eqref{dpn:eq:pk}, and $S=\sum_{k,i}kX_{k,i}$.  Then
$\log\E e^{-\cdot}$ is $\mu F(t)$, the cumulants are $\mu\kappa_j$, and
$a_{\alpha,\mu}(n)=e^{\mu F(t)+nt}\Prob_t(S=n)$; the conditional law given
$S=n$ is the weighted law on $\mu$-layer objects of size $n$.  The saddle
equation is $-\mu F'(t)=n$.
\mergenoteb{So the $\mu$-layer saddle at $n$ is the one-layer saddle
$t(n/\mu)$ of Section~\ref{dpn:jh:sec:threshold} at the real argument $n/\mu$,
and $s$, $M$, $H$ are its one-layer values there.  This is why the logarithmic
coefficients below do not depend on $\mu$.  Source 75-06 does not phrase it
this way; its proofs treat $\mu$ layers directly.}

\begin{theorem}[Growth and all orders for $\mu$ layers; source 75-06]\label{dpn:cp:thm:log}
Fix $\alpha>0$ and an integer $\mu\ge1$.
\begin{enumerate}
\item $\log a_{\alpha,\mu}(n)=\mu\alpha K_\mu(r_\mu-1)+O_{\alpha,\mu}(K_\mu/r_\mu)$.
\item For every fixed $R\ge1$, with the polynomials $P_j(c)$ of
  Theorem~\ref{dpn:thm:log},
  \begin{equation}\label{dpn:cp:eq:logall}
  \log a_{\alpha,\mu}(n)=\mu\alpha K_\mu\left(r_\mu-1+\sum_{j=1}^R\frac{P_j(c)}{r_\mu^j}
  +O_{\alpha,\mu,R}(r_\mu^{-R-1})\right).
  \end{equation}
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}[Proof \src{75-06}]
(1) Lower bound: split $n$ into $\mu$ integers $N_1,\dots,N_\mu$ differing by
at most one, and use the partition $1,2,\dots,k-1,k+d$ of
Proposition~\ref{dpn:prop:elementary} in each layer.  Each layer contributes
$\alpha\log k!=\alpha\sqrt{2N_i}(\log\sqrt{2N_i}-1)+O_\alpha(\log n)$, and the
sum is $\mu\alpha K_\mu(r_\mu-1)-O_{\alpha,\mu}(r_\mu)$.  Upper bound: with
$t=\alpha r_\mu/K_\mu$, positivity gives $\log a_{\alpha,\mu}(n)\le nt+\mu F(t)$.
Write $\log(1+e^u)=u_++\log(1+e^{-|u|})$ with $u=\alpha\log x-tx$: the
positive part integrates to $\alpha K_\mu(r_\mu/2-1)+O_\alpha(1)$; on
$x\ge K_\mu/2$ the slope bound $|u'|\ge\alpha(r_\mu-2)/K_\mu$ makes the
smoothing integral $O_\alpha(K_\mu/r_\mu)$; on $0<x\le\sqrt{K_\mu}$ use
$\log2$; on $\sqrt{K_\mu}\le x\le K_\mu/2$ convexity gives
$u\le-\alpha r_\mu/2+O_\alpha(1)$ and a contribution $O(K_\mu^{1-\alpha/2})$.
With the sum--integral error $O_\alpha(r_\mu)$ this gives
$\mu F(t)=\mu\alpha K_\mu(r_\mu/2-1)+O(K_\mu/r_\mu)$, and $nt=\mu\alpha
K_\mu r_\mu/2$.

(2) The boundary and mean expansions of Sections~\ref{dpn:sec:sommerfeld}
and~\ref{dpn:sec:eliminate} hold for $\mu F$ and $-\mu F'$ with the factor
$\mu$; at the saddle $K_\mu^2=M^2Q_\alpha(s)$, which is \eqref{dpn:eq:rfroms}
with $r_\mu$ for $r$, and $\mu F(t)+nt=\mu\alpha K_\mu\asym(r_\mu)$ to every
fixed order.  The local probability contributes $O(s)$ to the logarithm
(source 75-06's Corollary~5.3), below every scale $K_\mu/r_\mu^R$.  Taking
enough guard terms in the two independently proved expansions gives the
remainder.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Relative expansion for $\mu$ layers; source 75-06]\label{dpn:cp:thm:saddle}
Fix $\alpha>0$, an integer $\mu\ge1$ and $J\ge0$.  At the exact saddle
$-\mu F'(t_n)=n$, with $\eps=s/M$,
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:cp:eq:rel}
 a_{\alpha,\mu}(n)=\frac{e^{\mu F(t_n)+nt_n}}{\sqrt{2\pi\mu B}}
 \left(1+\sum_{\ell=1}^J\mathcal E^{(\mu)}_\ell+O_{\alpha,\mu,J}(\eps^{J+1})\right),
\end{equation}
where $\mathcal E^{(\mu)}_\ell$ is \eqref{dpn:eq:edgepoly} with every $\kappa_j$
(including $B=\kappa_2$) replaced by $\mu\kappa_j$, and
$\mathcal E^{(\mu)}_\ell=O(\eps^\ell)$.  Moreover
$\mu\kappa_j\sim\mu\,\tfrac{j!}2M^{j+1}/(\alpha s)^{j-1}$ and
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:cp:eq:E1}
 \mathcal E^{(\mu)}_1\sim-\frac{3}{8\mu\alpha Ms},\qquad
 a_{\alpha,\mu}(n)=\frac{e^{\mu F(t_n)+nt_n}}{\sqrt{2\pi\mu B}}
 \left(1-\frac{3+o(1)}{8\mu\alpha Ms}\right).
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
The proof is that of Theorem~\ref{dpn:thm:saddle} and
Lemma~\ref{dpn:jh:lem:cumulants}: a consecutive block of length $\asymp M/s$
around $M$ in any one layer gives \eqref{dpn:eq:centralbound} and
\eqref{dpn:eq:noncentral}, and the Taylor estimates are summed over the $\mu$
copies.
\mergenoteb{Each monomial of \eqref{dpn:eq:edgepoly} has
$\sum_jm_j-T/2=-\ell$, so $\mathcal E^{(\mu)}_\ell(t)=\mu^{-\ell}\mathcal
E_\ell(t)$: the $\mu$-layer corrections are the one-layer ones at $t(n/\mu)$,
divided by $\mu^\ell$.  Source 75-06 states the remainder with the safe
parameter $\eps$; the sharp remainder of Theorem~\ref{dpn:jh:thm:saddle} is
proved only for $\mu=1$.}

\paragraph{Monotonicity and exact computation \src{75-06}.}
For $\mu=1$ and $n\ge1$, $a_{\alpha,1}(n+1)>a_{\alpha,1}(n)$ by the injection
of Section~\ref{dpn:sec:inverse}; convolution with a positive nondecreasing
sequence preserves nondecrease, and the extra summand and the strict
inequalities give eventual strict increase for every fixed $\mu$.  For
integers $\alpha,\mu\ge1$ the coefficients through degree $N$ follow from the
finite product: for each $k$, repeat $\mu$ times the descending update
$A[n]\leftarrow A[n]+k^\alpha A[n-k]$ ($n=N,\dots,k$), which costs
$O(\mu N^2)$ integer operations and $O(N)$ stored integers (an arithmetic
count, not a bit-complexity bound).  Logarithmic differentiation gives
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:cp:eq:rec}
 n\,a_{\alpha,\mu}(n)=\mu\sum_{m=1}^nC_\alpha(m)\,a_{\alpha,\mu}(n-m),
\end{equation}
with $C_\alpha$ as in \eqref{dpn:eq:exactB}, also as an identity of real formal
power series; the alternating divisor sum can be badly conditioned in floating
point.  Source 75-06's \file{code/75-06-powers-checks.py} checks the product
against \eqref{dpn:cp:eq:rec} through $n=120$ for $\alpha,\mu\in\{1,2,3\}$ and
the two-layer product against the self-convolution through $n=200$.

\begin{theorem}[Threshold inversion for $\mu$ layers; source 75-06]\label{dpn:cp:thm:inverse}
Let $N_{\alpha,\mu}(Y)$ be the least $n$ with $\log a_{\alpha,\mu}(n)\ge Y$, and put
\[
 w=W_0\Bigl(\frac{Y}{\mu\alpha e}\Bigr),\qquad R_0=1+w,\qquad
 K_0=\frac{Y}{\mu\alpha w}=e^{R_0},\qquad n_0=\frac{\mu K_0^2}2 .
\]
For every fixed $R\ge2$, as $Y\to\infty$,
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:cp:eq:inverse}
 N_{\alpha,\mu}(Y)=n_0\left(1+\sum_{j=2}^R\frac{\Delta_j(c)}{R_0^j}
 +O_{\alpha,\mu,R}(R_0^{-R-1})\right),
\end{equation}
with the polynomials of Table~\ref{dpn:cp:tab:inverse}.
\end{theorem}
\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering\small
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.3}
\begin{tabular}{cll}
\toprule
$j$ & $\Delta_j(c)$ & source 75-06's $d_j(u)$, $u=6c$\\
\midrule
2 & $-2c$ & $-u/3$\\
3 & $-2c$ & $-u/3$\\
4 & $4c^2-2c$ & $u(u-3)/9$\\
5 & $\tfrac65c^2-2c$ & $u(u-10)/30$\\
6 & $-2c-\tfrac{77}5c^2-8c^3$ & $-u(20u^2+231u+180)/540$\\
7 & $-2c-57c^2-\tfrac{1336}{105}c^3$ & $-u(668u^2+17955u+3780)/11340$\\
8 & $-2c-138c^2-\tfrac{6226}{35}c^3+16c^4$ & $u(140u^3-9339u^2-43470u-3780)/11340$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Inverse coefficients.  $\Delta_2,\dots,\Delta_6$ are those of
\eqref{dpn:eq:inverseE2} and \eqref{dpn:48:eq:inversefull}; $\Delta_7$ and
$\Delta_8$ are new.  Source 75-06's $d_j(u)$ are unrelated to $d_\alpha(s)$.}
\label{dpn:cp:tab:inverse}
\end{table}
\begin{proof}[Proof \src{75-06}]
Write $B(r)=\sum_{j\ge1}P_j(c)r^{-j}$ and $K_\mu=K_0e^h$.  The formal inverse
equation is $e^h(R_0+h-1+B(R_0+h))=R_0-1$, that is
\eqref{dpn:eq:Einverse} with $E=e^h$, or
\[
 h=-\log\Bigl(1+\frac{h+B(R_0+h)}{R_0-1}\Bigr).
\]
It has a unique formal solution $h=O(R_0^{-2})$, since the right side has
formal derivative $O(1/R_0)$ in $h$, and $N_{\alpha,\mu}/n_0=e^{2h}$ gives
Table~\ref{dpn:cp:tab:inverse}.  For the integer threshold, use a high finite
truncation of Theorem~\ref{dpn:cp:thm:log}: its smooth main term has
$n$-derivative asymptotic to $\alpha r_\mu/K_\mu>0$; moving $n$ by a large
constant times $n_0/R_0^{R+1}$ changes it by order $K_0/R_0^R$, enough to
bracket the retained residual and the forward remainder; monotonicity then
brackets the threshold between neighbouring integers, and the $O(1)$ rounding
is absorbed.  Only explicit smooth approximants are differentiated.
\end{proof}
\mergenoteb{The intake recomputed $\Delta_2,\dots,\Delta_8$ by solving
\eqref{dpn:eq:Einverse} with $P_1,\dots,P_8$ in exact rational arithmetic; they
equal source 75-06's $d_j(6c)$.  As in Section~\ref{dpn:sec:inverse}, the core
$K(\log K-1)=Y/(\mu\alpha)$ is, after taking logarithms with
$w=\log K-1$, the equation $w+\log w=\log(Y/(\mu\alpha e))$, an instance of the
dominant-block theorem \texttt{p0:thm:lambert-core} of \cite{tai} on the
principal branch; the corrections are its perturbed inversion
(\texttt{p0:thm:perturbed-inversion}) and the integer step its staircase
theorem.  The principal branch $W_0$ inverts the growth; the lower branch
$W_{-1}$ parametrizes the moving boundary.}
\writenoted{The next two inverse coefficients, $\Delta_9$ and $\Delta_{10}$, are
in Proposition~\ref{dpn:rb:prop:P910} (source 100-82's exact record, confirmed by
source 75-06's program at order~10); that record also reproduces $\Delta_7$ and
$\Delta_8$.}

\begin{theorem}[Boundary profile and largest part for $\mu$ layers; source 75-06]\label{dpn:cp:thm:geometry}
Let $\Prob_n$ be the weighted law on $\mu$-layer objects of total size $n$, at
the saddle of Theorem~\ref{dpn:cp:thm:saddle}.
\begin{enumerate}
\item Fix distinct reals $x_1,\dots,x_d$ and layers $i_1,\dots,i_d$, and let
  $k_{n,j}=\lfloor M+Hx_j\rfloor$.  The occupation indicators of these
  site--layer pairs converge jointly to independent Bernoulli variables with
  parameters $(1+e^{x_j})^{-1}$; the same holds for any fixed collection of
  distinct site--layer pairs with convergent rescaled positions, including
  different layers at one site.  A site $\lfloor yM\rfloor$ is occupied with
  probability tending to $1$ for fixed $0<y<1$ and to $0$ for fixed $y>1$.
\item Let $L_n$ be the largest occupied size in any layer, and $b$ the large
  solution of
  \begin{equation}\label{dpn:cp:eq:bcenter}
   \frac{\mu}{t}\,b^\alpha e^{-tb}=1,\qquad
   b=-\frac\alpha t\,W_{-1}\Bigl(-\frac t\alpha\Bigl(\frac t\mu\Bigr)^{1/\alpha}\Bigr).
  \end{equation}
  Then $\Prob_n\{t_n(L_n-b)\le x\}\to\exp(-e^{-x})$ for every real $x$, and
  $L_n/K_\mu\to1+1/\alpha$ in probability; for the A022629 ensemble the limit
  is $2$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
At $\mu=1$ this is Theorem~\ref{dpn:thm:geometry}, and its proof is the proof
given there, with the following changes \src{75-06}.  The local limit estimate
of Section~\ref{dpn:sec:uniform} survives deleting finitely many site--layer
variables at sizes $O(M)$ (choose a longest surviving subinterval of the
central block, still a fixed fraction of $M/s$ long), or every variable above
a cutoff $m$ with $m/M\to\gamma>1$ and deleted variance $O(M^2)$; the target
may move by $o(\sqrt{\mu B})$.  For the maximum, $tb\to\infty$ and
$b/M\to(\alpha+1)/\alpha$, the tail intensity is
$\mu\sum_{k>m}p_k=(\mu/t)m^\alpha e^{-tm}(1+O(1/(tm)+t)+o(1))\to e^{-x}$ for
$m=b+x/t$, and the no-tail probability is $\prod_{k>m}(1-p_k)^\mu\to
e^{-e^{-x}}$; the deleted tail has mean $O(M)$ and variance $O(M^2)$.  The two
scales differ for the reason given after Theorem~\ref{dpn:thm:geometry}: a site
at $yM$ with $y>1$ is occupied with probability about $M^{-\alpha(y-1)}$, there
are order $M/s$ sites in an extreme window, and the balance occurs at
$y=1+1/\alpha$.

\paragraph{Finite-size checks \src{75-06}.}
At $n=500$ the first-corrected relative errors for
$(\alpha,\mu)=(1,2),(1,3),(2,1)$ are about $6.36\cdot10^{-6}$,
$4.72\cdot10^{-6}$ and $1.76\cdot10^{-3}$.  The less impressive last case is
kept deliberately: finite-$n$ behaviour depends on the parameters, and the
theorems are not uniform small-$n$ guarantees.

\section{Research questions of sources 75-01 and 75-06}\label{dpn:sec:b75questions}
Both sources close with research directions, kept here with their own
numbers; overlaps with Section~\ref{dpn:sec:future} are marked, and questions
answered in this report are re-scoped.  They are proposed tasks, not claims
that the problems are globally open.

\paragraph{Source 75-01.}
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textbf{75-01.\arabic*}, leftmargin=*]
\item \textbf{Is 77 the exact log-concavity threshold for A022629?}
  Theorem~\ref{dpn:jh:thm:concavity} reduces this to effective analytic bounds
  plus a finite check, but its constants are implicit; an explicit minor-arc
  bound is the main bottleneck.  For the other columns, find the minimal
  thresholds and their dependence on $\alpha$.
\item \textbf{How fast may the Jensen degree grow?}  Find a nontrivial
  $d(n)\to\infty$ for which hyperbolicity is still guaranteed; this needs
  simultaneous control of high cumulants, remainders and Hermite root spacing.
\item \textbf{The crossover as $\alpha\downarrow0$} (overlaps project~3 and
  48.2): connect the central limit regime near zero tilt to the moving edge,
  for example when $\alpha\log n=O(1)$.
\item \textbf{Growing moment order} (overlaps project~4): the diagonal
  \OEIS{A292190} of A292189 is covered by no theorem here; determine where a
  continuum edge stops being accurate and maximizing partitions take over.
  \textit{[Batch 100, 5 October 2026.]} The window
  $\alpha\asymp M^{1/3}/\log M$ is now treated (Section~\ref{dpn:ct:sec:theta});
  the diagonal has $\Lambda\to\infty$ and is still not covered
  (question~100-93.5).
\item \textbf{The subdominant Fourier sectors} (overlaps project~1): locate the
  noncentral saddle contributions, their phases and amplitudes, and explain
  the finite-size residuals at $\alpha=2,3$
  (Section~\ref{dpn:jh:sec:numerics}).  Theorem~\ref{dpn:thm:resonance}, which
  source 75-01 did not have, supplies the action at each fixed resonance.
  \textit{[Batch 100, 5 October 2026.]} The noncentral contributions are
  computed in the critical window of growing powers
  (Section~\ref{dpn:ct:sec:theta}); for the fixed-$\alpha$ residuals only an
  uncertified cell decomposition exists (Section~\ref{dpn:jh:sec:numerics}).
\item \textbf{Extend the weight class} (overlaps project~7 and 48.5), e.g.\
  $k^\alpha(\log(k+1))^\beta$ or regularly varying weights, preserving the
  cumulant scale, the local expansion and eventual Jensen hyperbolicity.
\item \textbf{Joint limit laws under norm weighting} (overlaps project~6,
  48.3 and 48.4): number of parts and largest part, with the correct
  conditional fluctuation scales.
\item \textbf{A validated inverse} (overlaps 48.7 and project~8): combine the
  tail bound below with interval evaluation and explicit constants in
  Theorem~\ref{dpn:jh:thm:saddle}, returning the exact threshold or a
  certified pair of candidates without all earlier coefficients.  Source
  75-01's tail bound: for $b\ge0$ and $k_0+1\ge2b/t$,
  $\sum_{k>k_0}k^be^{-tk}\le(k_0+1)^be^{-t(k_0+1)}/(1-e^{-t/2})$.
\item \textbf{Formalize the finite algebra, then the analysis} (overlaps
  project~8): the coefficient recurrence, the Bernoulli-cumulant recursion,
  the formula for $\mathcal E_r$, the Lagrange coefficients and the formal
  inverse recurrence first; then the block bound, the sum--integral estimates
  and the local Fourier remainder.  No such formalization is claimed.
\item \textbf{The signed companion.}  \OEIS{A022661}, $\prod_{k\ge1}(1-kq^k)$,
  asks whether its coefficient at $n=9$ is its only zero (Robert Israel,
  June 2, 2015) \cite{oeissigned}.  The positivity method does not settle it;
  competing phases make it a substantially different target.
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{Source 75-06.}
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textbf{75-06.\arabic*}, leftmargin=*]
\item \textbf{Complete the lower-scale transseries} (overlaps project~2 and
  48.1): determine the endpoint, discretization and determinant terms jointly;
  appending only $-\frac12\log(2\pi B)$ would be incorrect.
\item \textbf{Large-order growth and optimal truncation} (overlaps project~2):
  growth of $P_j(c)$ for fixed $c$, a Gevrey bound, control by the complex
  singularities of the boundary inverse, Borel summability and resurgence.
\item \textbf{Weak norm weighting} (overlaps project~3 and 48.2):
  $\alpha=\alpha(n)\downarrow0$ with $\alpha\log n$ bounded or slowly growing.
\item \textbf{Growing moments and growing layer counts} (overlaps project~4):
  $\alpha$ or $\mu$ depending on $n$; the width $M/(\alpha(s-1))$ may stop
  diverging, and diagonal entries of both arrays are not covered.
  \textit{[Batch 100, 5 October 2026.]} For one layer, the window in which
  that width is of order $M^{2/3}$ is Section~\ref{dpn:ct:sec:theta}.
\item \textbf{Slowly varying activities} (overlaps project~7 and 48.5):
  $k^\alpha\ell(k)$, persistence of the logistic and Gumbel laws, multiscale
  forward and inverse expansions.
\item \textbf{Fluctuations in the number of parts} (overlaps project~6 and
  48.3): a two-dimensional local theorem for $\prod(1+zk^\alpha q^k)^\mu$; no
  conditional variance formula is asserted.
  \textit{[Batch 100, 5 October 2026.]} In the critical window, for one layer,
  the regression variance $Q_\perp$ of the number of parts at least two is
  computed (Section~\ref{dpn:ct:sec:edge}); the two-dimensional local theorem
  is still open (question~100-93.7).
\item \textbf{The full extreme point process} (overlaps project~5 and 48.4),
  including multiplicities across layers and joint convergence with the
  boundary occupations.
\item \textbf{Quantitative error bounds and certified computation} (overlaps
  project~8 and 48.7), including when saddle evaluation beats the quadratic
  exact product.
\item \textbf{Shape inequalities and discrete regularity}: eventual
  log-concavity, ratio monotonicity, higher finite-difference inequalities.
  \textit{[Batch 75, 2 October 2026.]} Eventual strict log-concavity and
  fixed-degree Jensen hyperbolicity are proved for $\mu=1$ by source 75-01
  (Theorems~\ref{dpn:jh:thm:concavity} and~\ref{dpn:jh:thm:jensen}); the rest
  stays open.
\item \textbf{Large deviations for the norm}: a joint theory for size and log
  norm, with rate functions under uniform and norm-tilted measures, first
  uniformly in $\alpha$ on compact intervals.
\item \textbf{Formal verification in ProveIt} (overlaps project~8): exact
  identities and rational reversion first; formalizing the elementary proof
  would give a checked resolution of the leading conjecture before the
  probability theory.
\item \textbf{OEIS and bibliographic follow-through} (overlaps 48.8): formula
  updates with exact quantifiers, and a broader search (norm-weighted strict
  partitions, multiplicative statistics, variable-activity fermionic products)
  before any novelty claim; independent review is the next validation step.
\end{enumerate}

\section{Addendum (batch 77): a fifth independent source and slot multiplicities}\label{dpn:fp:sec:b77}
Batch 77 of ProveIt's incoming-reports intake delivered one more manuscript on
this family: \textbf{source 77-51} (batch-77 manuscript 51),
\emph{Asymptotic expansions and inversion for fixed power weighted
partitions}, dated October 1, 2026, author line ``Research report'', archive
\file{oeis-fixed-power-asymptotics-result.zip} (arrival commit
\texttt{096ee7b87}; 11-page PDF, not shipped).  It names no ProveIt commit,
records no repository search, and cites none of sources 40, 48, 75-01 and
75-06.  It treats A022629 in detail and the two-parameter family
\[
 A^{[\beta]}_\alpha(q)=\prod_{k\ge1}(1+k^\alpha q^k)^{k^\beta},
 \qquad\alpha>0\text{ real},\ \beta\ge0\text{ an integer},
\]
whose case $\beta=0$ is this report's $A_\alpha(q)$.  Its symbols are
translated by the third table of Section~\ref{dpn:sec:notation}.

For this report, source 77-51 is a fifth independent proof of
Proposition~\ref{dpn:prop:elementary} for every fixed $\alpha$, of
Theorems~\ref{dpn:thm:log} and~\ref{dpn:thm:inverse} for $\alpha=1$ (all
orders) and for every fixed $\alpha$ (first two orders), and of
Theorem~\ref{dpn:thm:saddle} for every fixed $\alpha$, with identical
coefficients.  Table~\ref{dpn:fp:tab:dup} records them, following
Section~\ref{dpn:sec:merge}; they are not printed again.  Its new material is
Section~\ref{dpn:fp:sec:family} (slot multiplicities $k^\beta$, $\beta\ge1$)
and Section~\ref{dpn:fp:sec:oeis} (OEIS \OEIS{A266891}), the resummed
``thermal'' forms of Proposition~\ref{dpn:fp:prop:thermal}, a further
citation (Section~\ref{dpn:fp:sec:checks}), and four research questions
(Section~\ref{dpn:fp:sec:questions}).  Material from this source is marked
\src{77-51}.

\paragraph{Stale statements and priority caveats.}
Source 77-51's abstract and README present the proofs of the A022629 and
A092484 conjectures as its contribution (``proves the logarithmic OEIS
conjectures for A022629, A092484 and A266891''); in this report they are the
fifth proofs, and only the A266891 statement is new.  It makes no priority
claim: ``neither an exponentially complete transseries nor a literature-wide
priority claim is asserted''; ``Our proof does not depend on an
absence-of-literature assertion''; its comparison with Gikunda's thesis
``neither discounts the thesis nor claims that no other source treats fixed
tilts''.  Its asymptotic constants ``depend on fixed parameters and
truncation order'' and ``have not been made effective''; uniformity at zero
tilt and proof-assistant verification are not asserted, and ``No external
submission or publication'' was made (its README).

\paragraph{Neighbouring reports of batch 77.}
\file{oeis-sequence-asymptotics/a301746-divisor-weighted-asymptotics}, written
on the same day in the same style, applies the same exact-saddle sum and
consecutive-block lemma to $\prod(1+q^k)^{d(k)^2}$; its independent audit
cites ``the independently reviewed A022629 proof'', which is source 77-51's
audit (\file{77-51-fp-ind-AUDIT.md} here).
\file{oeis-sequence-asymptotics/a291698-moving-fugacity-partitions} treats
distinct partitions with one global fugacity $u=n^\alpha$ attached to every
part, $[q^n]\prod_k(1+uq^k)$, rather than the per-part weights $k^\alpha$ of
this report: a different weighting, with no result in common.

$\Delta_2=\Delta_3=-2c$ for every $\alpha$ (Proposition~\ref{dpn:fp:prop:thermal}).
Its relative errors (Table~\ref{dpn:fp:tab:numerics}) are $a_1(n)/G_J-1$,

\subsection{Second routes and forms that differ}\label{dpn:fp:sec:routes}
\begin{proposition}[Thermal forms; source 77-51]\label{dpn:fp:prop:thermal}
For fixed $\alpha>0$, with $K$, $r$, $c$ as in \eqref{dpn:eq:Kr} and $K_0$,
$R_0$ as in \eqref{dpn:eq:Wlead},
\begin{align}
 \log a_\alpha(n)&=\alpha K(r-1)+\frac{\alpha cK}{r-1}+O_\alpha(K/r^3),
 \label{dpn:fp:eq:thermal}\\
 N_\alpha(Y)&=\frac{K_0^2}{2}\left(1-\frac{2c}{R_0(R_0-1)}
 +O_\alpha(R_0^{-4})\right).\label{dpn:fp:eq:thermalinv}
\end{align}
\end{proposition}
Here $\alpha c=\pi^2/(6\alpha)$; for $\alpha=1$ source 77-51 writes
$m(L-1)+Cm/(L-1)$ and $\frac{m_*^2}2(1-\pi^2/(3L_*(L_*-1)))$, with $m=K$,
$L=r$, $C=\pi^2/6$, $m_*=K_0=Y/W_0(Y/e)$, $L_*=R_0$.
\begin{proof}
Expanding $1/(r-1)$ and $1/(R_0(R_0-1))$ in powers of $1/r$ and $1/R_0$ shows
that the two displays agree with Theorem~\ref{dpn:thm:log} ($P_1=P_2=c$) and
with \eqref{dpn:eq:inverselead} ($-2c$, $-2c$) through the orders kept, so
they follow from those theorems.  Source 77-51 proves them directly
\src{77-51}: at $t=\alpha s/M$ the first-order case of the boundary smoothing
(Lemma~\ref{dpn:lem:smoothing}) gives $F(t)=\alpha M(s/2-1)+\pi^2M/(6\alpha(s-1))
+O(M/s^3)$, through the inverse branch $x(v)$ of $tx-\alpha\log x$ with
$x'(0)=M/(\alpha(s-1))$ and the kernel integral
$\int_\R\log(1+e^{-|v|})\dd v=\pi^2/6$; the mean relation gives
$M=K(1+O(r^{-2}))$; replacing $M$ by $K$ in the hard exponent and in the
thermal term costs $O(K/r^3)$; and the exact-saddle theorem adds only
$O(r)$.  For the inverse it substitutes $K=K_0\{1-c/(R_0(R_0-1))\}$: the
thermal term cancels the first variation of $\alpha K(\log K-1)$ to error
$O(K_0/R_0^3)$, and division by the slope $\sim\alpha R_0$ gives the relative
error $O(R_0^{-4})$.
\end{proof}
\mergenotec{The resummed forms have the same order of accuracy as the
two-term truncations of Theorem~\ref{dpn:thm:log} and \eqref{dpn:eq:inverselead}:
the term $\alpha cK/r^3$ hidden in \eqref{dpn:fp:eq:thermal} is not $\alpha
P_3(c)K/r^3$.  They are compact displays, not sharper estimates.  Source 77-51
calls \eqref{dpn:fp:eq:thermal} ``a sharper thermal correction'' relative to its
first display, $\log a_n=m(L-1)+O(m/L)$.  As in Section~\ref{dpn:sec:inverse},
the core $K(\log K-1)=Y/\alpha$ is the case $a=b=1$ of the Lambert-core
theorem \texttt{p0:thm:lambert-core} of \cite{tai}, after taking logarithms
with $w=\log K-1$.}

\paragraph{An elementary upper bound by a cutoff sum \src{77-51}.}
For $\alpha=1$ choose $t=r/K$; positivity gives $\log a_1(n)\le nt+F(t)$.  With
$g(x)=\log x-tx$ and $K'=\lfloor K\rfloor$, source 77-51 shows
$F(t)=\sum_{k=1}^{K'}g(k)+O(K/r)$.  For large $K$, concavity gives $g(k)\ge0$
on $2\le k\le K'$, and the $k=1$ correction is bounded.  On $2\le k\le K/2$,
$\log(1+e^{-g(k)})\le e^{tk}/k\le\sqrt K/k$, a total $O(\sqrt K\log K)$.  On
$K/2<k\le K$, $\log x\ge-2(1-x)$ for $1/2\le x\le1$ gives $g(k)\ge(r-2)(1-k/K)$,
and a geometric sum gives $O(K/r)$.  For $k>K$,
$\log(1+e^{g(k)})\le(k/K)e^{-r(k-K)/K}$, with the same bound.  Finally
$nt+\sum_{k\le K'}g(k)=\log K'!+t\{n-K'(K'+1)/2\}=K(r-1)+O(r)$.  This is a
discrete form of the positive-part argument of
Section~\ref{dpn:48:sec:elementary}; source 77-51 adds that solving
$K(\log K-1)=Y$ exactly, with error $O(K/\log K)$ and slope $\log K$, proves
the first inverse form ``without saddle analysis''.

\paragraph{Coefficient generation in source 77-51's variables \src{77-51}.}
Source 77-51 generates $d_j$ and $b_j$ by the same two steps as
Sections~\ref{dpn:sec:sommerfeld} and~\ref{dpn:sec:eliminate}, at $\alpha=1$.
Its rational functions $R_1(x)=1/(x-1)$,
$R_{j+1}(x)=(R_j(x)+xR_j'(x))/(x-1)$ are the derivatives $U_j$ of
\eqref{dpn:eq:Ulagrange} ($x^{(j)}(0)=MR_j(s)$); its series
$T(L)=\sum_{j\ge0}2\eta(2j+2)R_{2j+1}(L)$ is the smoothing series of
Lemma~\ref{dpn:lem:smoothing}; and its stationarity equation for
$\rho=M/K$ in powers of $h=1/r$ is the elimination of the saddle.  With
$P=\pi^2$ it records
\[
 T(L)=\frac{P}{6(L-1)}+\frac{7P^2(2L+1)}{360(L-1)^5}+O(L^{-6}),\qquad
 \rho=1-\frac{Ph^2}6-\frac{Ph^3}6+\frac{P^2h^4}{24}+O(h^5),
\]
and its inverse equation
$\tau[h^{-1}+\log\tau-1+D((h^{-1}+\log\tau)^{-1})]=h^{-1}-1$,
$D(h)=\sum_jd_jh^j$, $\tau^2=1+\sum_{j\ge2}b_jh^j$, is \eqref{dpn:eq:Einverse}
with $E=\tau$, $h=1/R_0$.  A separately written rational-series checker agrees
through $d_6$ and $b_7$; the formal series are justified, as here, by
truncation and the implicit function theorem, ``without interchanging
infinite asymptotic series''.  No convergence of either formal hierarchy is
asserted.

\paragraph{Inverses of the exact-saddle model \src{77-51}.}
Source 77-51 inverts $A_R(x)=e^{F(t_x)+xt_x}(2\pi B(t_x))^{-1/2}
(1+\sum_{\ell\le R}\mathcal E_\ell(t_x))$, whose logarithmic slope is
asymptotic to $t_x$, and places the threshold between the roundings of
$x_R(Y)\mp C_Rw^{-R}$, $w=M/s$.  It ``does not prescribe an exact rounding
choice when $x_R(y)$ is very close to an integer'', and ``the asymptotic
constants are not supplied as effective numerical interval bounds''.
Theorem~\ref{dpn:jh:thm:threshold} \src{75-01} is the sharper statement; the
two-ceiling step is the staircase theorem \texttt{p0:thm:staircase} of
\cite{tai}.

\subsection{Slot multiplicities \texorpdfstring{$k^\beta$}{k\textasciicircum beta}}\label{dpn:fp:sec:family}
This subsection is source 77-51's Section~8, translated (its $a^{(\alpha,\beta)}_n$,
$F_{\alpha,\beta}$, $d$, $m_0$, $L_0$, $\ell$, $\mathcal W$, $u_*$, $m_*$,
$L_*$ are written as below).  Fix $\alpha>0$ and an integer $\beta\ge0$, and let
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:fp:eq:family}
 A^{[\beta]}_\alpha(q)=\prod_{k\ge1}(1+k^\alpha q^k)^{k^\beta}
 =\sum_{n\ge0}a^{[\beta]}_\alpha(n)q^n .
\end{equation}
Here $k^\beta$ is the number of labelled slots of size $k$, each used at most
once, and a used slot of size $k$ has weight $k^\alpha$.  The coefficients are
nonnegative real weights; for integer $\alpha$ they are coloured counts.
Fractional slot multiplicities are outside the theorem.
\mergenotec{For $\beta=0$ this is $A_\alpha$.  For $\beta\ge1$ it lies
between the two products that Section~1.1 distinguishes: several parts of
one size may occur, as in $\prod(1+q^k)^{k^\alpha}$, but each carries the
internal weight $k^\alpha$, as in $A_\alpha$.  It is also not the convolution
power $A_\alpha^\mu$ of Section~\ref{dpn:cp:sec:powers}, which has a constant
number $\mu$ of slots at every size.  The notation $a^{[\beta]}_\alpha$ is
chosen so as not to collide with $a_{\alpha,\mu}$.}

\begin{theorem}[Slot multiplicities; source 77-51]\label{dpn:fp:thm:family}
Put $K^{[\beta]}=((\beta+2)n)^{1/(\beta+2)}$ and $r^{[\beta]}=\log K^{[\beta]}$.  Then
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:fp:eq:familythermal}
 \log a^{[\beta]}_\alpha(n)
 =\alpha (K^{[\beta]})^{\beta+1}\left(\frac{r^{[\beta]}}{\beta+1}-\frac1{(\beta+1)^2}\right)
  +\frac{\pi^2(K^{[\beta]})^{\beta+1}}{6\alpha(r^{[\beta]}-1)}
  +O_{\alpha,\beta}\bigl((K^{[\beta]})^{\beta+1}/(r^{[\beta]})^3\bigr).
\end{equation}
Define the exact saddle $t_n$ and the cumulants $\kappa^{[\beta]}_j$,
$B^{[\beta]}=\kappa^{[\beta]}_2$ from $F^{[\beta]}(t)=\log A^{[\beta]}_\alpha(e^{-t})$
as in Section~\ref{dpn:sec:saddle}, and $s$, $M=e^s$ by $t=\alpha s/M$ as in
\eqref{dpn:eq:phase}.  With $\mathcal W=M^{\beta+1}/s$, for every fixed
$R\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:fp:eq:familysaddle}
 a^{[\beta]}_\alpha(n)=\frac{e^{F^{[\beta]}(t_n)+nt_n}}{\sqrt{2\pi B^{[\beta]}}}
 \left(1+\sum_{\ell=1}^R\mathcal E^{[\beta]}_\ell+O_{\alpha,\beta,R}(\mathcal W^{-R-1})\right),
\end{equation}
where $\mathcal E^{[\beta]}_\ell$ is \eqref{dpn:eq:edgepoly} with $\kappa_j$
replaced by $\kappa^{[\beta]}_j$.  For $Y=\log y$ set
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:fp:eq:familyinvvars}
 u_*=\frac{(\beta+1)^2Y/\alpha}{W_0\bigl((\beta+1)^2Y/(\alpha e)\bigr)},\qquad
 K^{[\beta]}_0=u_*^{1/(\beta+1)},\qquad R^{[\beta]}_0=\log K^{[\beta]}_0.
\end{equation}
The least index $N^{[\beta]}_\alpha(Y)$ with $a^{[\beta]}_\alpha(n)\ge e^Y$ obeys
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:fp:eq:familyinverse}
 N^{[\beta]}_\alpha(Y)=\frac{(K^{[\beta]}_0)^{\beta+2}}{\beta+2}
 \left[1-\frac{(\beta+2)\pi^2}{6\alpha^2R^{[\beta]}_0(R^{[\beta]}_0-1)}
 +O_{\alpha,\beta}((R^{[\beta]}_0)^{-4})\right].
\end{equation}
The exact-saddle inverse has pre-rounding uncertainty
$O_{\alpha,\beta,R}(\mathcal W^{-R-1}/t)$.
\end{theorem}
At $\beta=0$, $K^{[0]}=K$, $r^{[0]}=r$, $\mathcal W=M/s=1/\eps$, and the theorem is
Proposition~\ref{dpn:fp:prop:thermal} with Theorem~\ref{dpn:thm:saddle}.

\paragraph{Monotonicity \src{77-51}.}
Choose the least-labelled selected slot at the largest occupied size $k$, and
move it to size $k+1$ with the same label.  The label exists because
$(k+1)^\beta\ge k^\beta$, and no slot at the new largest size was occupied.
The unique new largest slot records the inverse operation.  The map is
injective and multiplies its weight by $((k+1)/k)^\alpha>1$.  Thus the
coefficient sequence is strictly increasing for $n\ge1$, including
noninteger $\alpha$.

\paragraph{Uniform estimates for a fixed pair \src{77-51}.}
To prove the estimates uniformly for a \emph{fixed} pair $(\alpha,\beta)$, take
$0<\delta<\min(1/2,(\beta+1)/(2\alpha))$, and let $p_k$ be the Bernoulli
parameter of a slot of size $k$, as in \eqref{dpn:eq:pk}.  For $k\le\delta M$
and fixed $j\ge0$,
\[
 k^{\beta+j}p_k(1-p_k)\le M^{\alpha\delta}k^{\beta+j-\alpha}.
\]
Its sum is $O(M^{\max(\alpha\delta,\beta+j+1-\alpha+\alpha\delta)}\log M)$, and
that exponent is less than $\beta+j+1$.  This handles large $\alpha$, for
which a naive split at $M/2$ is insufficient.  On $\delta M\le k\le M$,
\[
 \alpha\log k-tk\ge\alpha(s-1/\delta)(1-k/M),
\]
so a geometric sum applies.  Above $M$, use
$p_k\le(k/M)^\alpha e^{-\alpha s(k-M)/M}$.  It follows that
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:fp:eq:familybounds}
 \sum_{k\ge1}k^{\beta+j}p_k(1-p_k)
 =O_{\alpha,\beta,j}(M^{\beta+j+1}/s).
\end{equation}
The active interval still has consecutive length comparable to $M/s$, but
each size now has $k^\beta\asymp M^\beta$ independent slots.  Thus
$B^{[\beta]}\asymp M^2\mathcal W$ and $\kappa^{[\beta]}_j=O_j(M^j\mathcal W)$.
The analytic-disk argument remains valid; in its upper tail the small quantity
is $O(M^{-\alpha})$.  The trigonometric exponent of Lemma~\ref{dpn:lem:block}
is multiplied by $M^\beta$, giving noncentral suppression
$e^{-cM^{\beta+1}/s^3}$ (with an absolute constant $c$, not this report's
$c=\pi^2/(6\alpha^2)$).  The proof of the exact-saddle expansion then applies
with $1/\eps$ replaced by $\mathcal W$.

\paragraph{Thermal and inverse constants \src{77-51}.}
Sum--integral errors are $O(M^\beta s)$ for $x^\beta\log(1+x^\alpha e^{-tx})$
and $O(M^{\beta+1})$ for the mean integrand.  These follow by integrating
absolute derivatives, using \eqref{dpn:fp:eq:familybounds}.  The hard
integrals of the log generating function and of the mean are
\[
 F_{\rm hard}=\alpha M^{\beta+1}\left(\frac{s}{(\beta+1)(\beta+2)}-\frac1{(\beta+1)^2}\right),
 \qquad \frac{M^{\beta+2}}{\beta+2}.
\]
Small-size and distant-tail contributions are smaller than every
inverse-logarithmic order, by the $\delta$ split just proved.  On the central
branch $v=tx-\alpha\log x$,
\[
 x'(0)=\frac M{\alpha(s-1)},\quad
 x''(0)=-\frac M{\alpha^2(s-1)^3},\quad
 x'''(0)=\frac{M(2s+1)}{\alpha^3(s-1)^5}.
\]
The even soft-cutoff kernel multiplies $x(v)^\beta x'(v)$, whose second
derivative is $O(M^{\beta+1}/s^3)$.  Its constant term gives
\[
 F^{[\beta]}(t)=F_{\rm hard}+\frac{\pi^2M^{\beta+1}}{6\alpha(s-1)}+O(M^{\beta+1}/s^3).
\]
For the mean, the odd kernel multiplies $x(v)^{\beta+1}x'(v)$, whose first
derivative is $O(M^{\beta+2}/s^2)$.  Hence $M=K^{[\beta]}(1+O((r^{[\beta]})^{-2}))$.  The
hard exponent has zero derivative at $K^{[\beta]}$ and second derivative
$\alpha(r^{[\beta]}-1)(K^{[\beta]})^{\beta-1}$, so the displacement costs
$O((K^{[\beta]})^{\beta+1}/(r^{[\beta]})^3)$.  This proves \eqref{dpn:fp:eq:familythermal}.

The main entropy $H(m)=\alpha m^{\beta+1}(\log m/(\beta+1)-1/(\beta+1)^2)$ has
derivative $\alpha m^\beta\log m$.  With $u=m^{\beta+1}$, the equation $H(m)=Y$
is $u(\log u-1)=(\beta+1)^2Y/\alpha$, giving \eqref{dpn:fp:eq:familyinvvars}.
The thermal term changes $m$ by the relative amount
$-\pi^2/[6\alpha^2R^{[\beta]}_0(R^{[\beta]}_0-1)]+O((R^{[\beta]}_0)^{-4})$, proving
\eqref{dpn:fp:eq:familyinverse}.  The exact-saddle inverse follows as before
from a logarithmic slope asymptotic to $t$.
\mergenotec{The core $u(\log u-1)=(\beta+1)^2Y/\alpha$ is, with
$w=\log u-1$, the equation $w+\log w=\log((\beta+1)^2Y/(\alpha e))$: the case
$a=b=1$ of \texttt{p0:thm:lambert-core} of \cite{tai}, on the principal
branch, as for $\beta=0$.  No novelty is claimed for the inversion.  Source
77-51 gives every inverse-logarithmic order only for A022629 ($\alpha=1$,
$\beta=0$); for $\beta\ge1$ only the two orders of
\eqref{dpn:fp:eq:familythermal} and \eqref{dpn:fp:eq:familyinverse} are proved.}

\subsection{The OEIS instances, and A266891}\label{dpn:fp:sec:oeis}
For $(\alpha,\beta)=(1,0)$ the theorem gives A022629, and for $(2,0)$ A092484,
whose entry records the conjecture $\log a_n\sim\sqrt{2n}\{\log(2n)-2\}$
\cite{oeis2}; both are proved above by Theorem~\ref{dpn:thm:log}.
\begin{corollary}[OEIS A266891; source 77-51]\label{dpn:fp:cor:a266891}
For $(\alpha,\beta)=(1,1)$, that is for $\prod_{k\ge1}(1+kq^k)^k$
(\OEIS{A266891}), with $K^{[1]}=(3n)^{1/3}$ and $r^{[1]}=\log K^{[1]}$,
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:fp:eq:a266891}
 \log a^{[1]}_1(n)=(K^{[1]})^2\left(\frac{r^{[1]}}2-\frac14\right)
 +\frac{\pi^2(K^{[1]})^2}{6(r^{[1]}-1)}+O\bigl((K^{[1]})^2/(r^{[1]})^3\bigr).
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}
Its leading term is exactly the A266891 conjecture
$n^{2/3}(2\log(3n)-3)/(4\cdot3^{1/3})$, credited to Kote\v{s}ovec on
8~May 2018 \cite{oeis266891}.  These are specializations of one family
theorem.  Its constants are not asserted to be uniform as $\alpha\downarrow0$
or $\beta\to\infty$ \src{77-51}.
\mergenotec{The intake computed the coefficients of $\prod_{k\ge1}(1+kq^k)^k$
by the finite product: $1,1,4,13,29,81,188,456,1030,2405,5295,11611,25246$.
The first ten equal the A266891 prefix recorded in source 77-51's
\file{77-51-fp-ind-SOURCE_CHECK.md}; the intake did not re-fetch the OEIS
entry.  No other ProveIt file mentions A266891.}

\subsection{Related work and computational checks \src{77-51}}\label{dpn:fp:sec:checks}
Naranjo and Ram\'irez \cite{NR} discuss A022629 explicitly in their
combinatorial treatment of coloured partitions; source 77-51's proof ``does
not depend on an absence-of-literature assertion''.  Its comparisons with
Gikunda's thesis \cite{gikunda} (Proposition~4.2.5 assumes a shrinking tilt
$a=O(r^\delta)$, which a fixed $\alpha>0$ does not satisfy) and with
Granovsky and Stark \cite{GS} (their framework assumes internal weights at
most one, so it cannot be applied directly with $k^\alpha>1$) are those of
Section~1.2.
\mergenotec{Source 77-51 cites the thesis at a ResearchGate address
(publication 402798449) and its supplementary source check at another
(publication 402798310); this report keeps the DOI of \cite{gikunda}.}

Source 77-51's package contains proof-source snapshots, independent
mathematical audits, and separate exact and numerical programs.  One exact
recurrence multiplies the finite product through the requested degree; an
independent one uses logarithmic differentiation.  The A022629 producer check
verifies 2,001 exact coefficients, the first 43 listed terms, 1,999 strict
inequalities and 2,000 factorial lower bounds.  The independent core checker
compares 251 coefficients by two methods and checks Gaussian corrections
through $R=4$ and 48 inverse-derivative identities; independent
rational-series calculations reproduce $d_1,\dots,d_6$ and $b_2,\dots,b_7$.
Table~\ref{dpn:fp:tab:numerics} gives its high-precision comparisons for
A022629.  They are not interval certificates or proof dependencies.  The
signed relative error is the exact coefficient divided by the approximant,
minus one.
\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering\small
\begin{tabular}{r rr}
\toprule
$n$ & Leading saddle & With $\mathcal E_1$\\
\midrule
50   & $-1.75854\cdot10^{-2}$ & $-8.75761\cdot10^{-4}$\\
200  & $-6.85783\cdot10^{-3}$ & $-2.59784\cdot10^{-5}$\\
1000 & $-2.47368\cdot10^{-3}$ & $-4.50475\cdot10^{-6}$\\
2000 & $-1.60739\cdot10^{-3}$ & $-1.92291\cdot10^{-6}$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Relative errors $a_1(n)/G_J-1$ for A022629, $J=0,1$, source
77-51.}\label{dpn:fp:tab:numerics}
\end{table}
The exact-saddle approximation is already useful in this range; the
inverse-logarithmic hierarchy serves a different, much coarser scale.  No
finite truncation is guaranteed to improve every small-$n$ value
\src{77-51}.

\subsection{Research questions of source 77-51}\label{dpn:fp:sec:questions}
Source 77-51 closes with four problems, kept with its own numbers; overlaps
with Sections~\ref{dpn:sec:future} and~\ref{dpn:sec:b75questions} are marked.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textbf{77-51.\arabic*}, leftmargin=*]
\item \textbf{Effective constants} for the Fourier remainder and the inverse
  brackets, so that a given large threshold can be located with certified
  numerical error (overlaps project~8, 48.7, 75-01.8 and 75-06.8).
\item \textbf{The beyond-all-inverse-logarithmic contributions} from discrete
  sum--integral corrections and noncentral Fourier arcs: the argument
  suppresses them in bounds and does not identify a complete exponential
  transseries (overlaps projects~1 and~2, 48.1, 75-01.5 and 75-06.1).
  \writenotec{Theorem~\ref{dpn:thm:resonance} \src{40} gives the action of
  each fixed resonance; the full contribution stays open.}
  \textit{[Batch 100, 5 October 2026.]} The sum--integral corrections are
  identified by Theorem~\ref{dpn:rb:thm:smooth} \src{100-89}; the noncentral
  arcs only in the critical window (Section~\ref{dpn:ct:sec:theta}).
\item \textbf{The transition as the positive tilt tends to zero with $n$},
  connecting the fixed-tilt cutoff to the shrinking-tilt regime of
  \cite{gikunda}; the constants of the present proof are not uniform there
  (overlaps project~3, 48.2, 75-01.3 and 75-06.3).
\item \textbf{Slot multiplicities beyond exact integer powers}, under explicit
  regularity and lattice conditions.  Arbitrary fractional exponents in
  \eqref{dpn:fp:eq:family} need not retain coefficient positivity and are not
  covered by the Bernoulli interpretation (related to project~7, 48.5,
  75-01.6 and 75-06.5, which vary the weight rather than the number of slots).
\end{enumerate}
Source 77-51 establishes its displayed statements by ordinary analysis.
Finite computations corroborate the formulas, but ``no numerical fit,
exhaustive test or proof-assistant verification is used to infer the
asymptotic theorems''.

\section{Addendum (batch 100): a smooth boundary sector and exact-range inversion}\label{dpn:rb:sec:b100}
Batch 100 of ProveIt's incoming-reports intake delivered three more
manuscripts on this family.  They are Research Reports 82, 89 and 93 of the
session bundle that arrived in commit \texttt{60f54ea06} and was placed in
\texttt{36571ae0e}, and they form one author series: all three carry the
author line ``Research note prepared for Vladimir Reshetnikov with OpenAI''
(also in their PDF metadata), and each builds on the one before.
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Source 100-82} (Report 82), \emph{Asymptotics and Inverse
  Growth for Weighted Distinct Partitions} (subtitle: the sequence OEIS
  A022629), 1 October 2026, 7 pages: $\alpha=1$ only; a relative equivalent
  at the saddle of the \emph{integral} with the explicit constant
  $\tfrac12\log(2\pi)-1$, all logarithmic orders, and the Lambert-$W$
  inverse.  Its JSON record carries ten forward and nine inverse
  coefficients (inverse orders 2--10).
\item \textbf{Source 100-89} (Report 89), \emph{Relative Expansions and Exact
  Range Inversion for Power Weighted Distinct Partitions}, 1 October 2026,
  9 pages: every real $\alpha>0$, uniformly on compact sets; the
  lattice-minus-integral difference is smooth at $t=0$, with explicit
  Taylor coefficients; an all-orders relative expansion with the exact
  integral in the exponent; an all-orders exact-range inverse.  Its README
  calls source 100-82 ``the earlier A022629 report''.
\item \textbf{Source 100-93} (Report 93), \emph{Critical Theta Asymptotics and
  Inversion for Power Weighted Distinct Partitions}, 2 October 2026,
  13 pages: $\alpha$ growing with $n$ in a critical window.  It calls source
  100-89 its ``fixed-power companion'' and pins it by the SHA-256 of its
  archive (\texttt{225b2070\ldots}, which matches the delivered archive).
\end{itemize}
Sources 100-82 and 100-89 name no ProveIt commit and record no repository
search.  Source 100-93 names the ProveIt commit \texttt{6591a56b3} (1 October
2026, 18:55 PDT), which predates the placement \texttt{9df4ba51a} (19:13 PDT)
of this report's first two sources; none of the three saw this report.  They
are called by their bundle report numbers, because the cluster-local numbers
01--03 of batch 100 collide with those of other batch-100 clusters.

This section merges sources 100-89 and 100-82.  Source 100-89 is the base:
it has the weaker hypotheses (every real $\alpha>0$), and source 100-82 is its
case $\alpha=1$.  Section~\ref{dpn:ct:sec:theta} is source 100-93.  For this
report, sources 100-82 and 100-89 are the sixth and seventh proofs of the
A022629 conjecture, and source 100-89 is the sixth for A092484 and
A265840--A265842 (sources 40, 48, 75-01, 75-06 and 77-51 treat every real
$\alpha>0$).  Their shared results are recorded once, in
Table~\ref{dpn:rb:tab:dup}, and not reprinted.  Their new material is: the two
coefficients $P_9,P_{10}$ and the two inverse coefficients $\Delta_9,\Delta_{10}$
(Proposition~\ref{dpn:rb:prop:P910}); the smooth boundary sector and its
constants (Section~\ref{dpn:rb:sec:boundary}); the all-orders relative
expansion with the exact integral and its first-order form at the integral
saddle (Section~\ref{dpn:rb:sec:relative}); and an all-orders exact-range
inverse (Section~\ref{dpn:rb:sec:inverse}).  Material from these sources is
marked \src{100-82}, \src{100-89} or \src{100-93}; text added in this merge
is marked \textbf{[Merge note, batch 100.]}; dated notes on earlier text read
\textit{[Batch 100, 5 October 2026.]}; proofs and statements supplied by this
write rather than by a source are marked \wrt.  Their notation is translated
by the fourth table of Section~\ref{dpn:sec:notation}.

\mergenoted{The intake compared the coefficients in exact rational
arithmetic.  Source 100-82's JSON record
\file{data/100-82-integral-check_formal_series.json} gives $s_j$ and $v_j$ as
polynomials in $P=\pi^2$ at $\alpha=1$.  With $P=6c$ they equal
$P_1,\dots,P_8$ of \eqref{dpn:eq:P} and \eqref{dpn:eq:P8} and
$\Delta_2,\dots,\Delta_8$ of Table~\ref{dpn:cp:tab:inverse}; source 100-89's
four printed terms are $P_1,\dots,P_4$.  The substitution is legitimate for
every $\alpha$: the generation is algebraic in the factors

100-89's README makes the same point, ``$\pi^2\to\pi^2/a^2$'').}

\begin{proposition}[Two further forward and inverse coefficients; source 100-82, checked here]\label{dpn:rb:prop:P910}
The next coefficients of \eqref{dpn:eq:logall} and of
\eqref{dpn:eq:inverseE2} (equivalently of \eqref{dpn:cp:eq:inverse}) are
\begin{align}
 P_9(c)&=c+\tfrac{1162}5c^2+\tfrac{9305}3c^3+\tfrac{3668017}{1050}c^4
        +\tfrac78c^5,\label{dpn:rb:eq:P9}\\
 P_{10}(c)&=c+\tfrac{1926}5c^2+\tfrac{51747}5c^3+\tfrac{11264369}{350}c^4
        +\tfrac{2861817}{440}c^5,\label{dpn:rb:eq:P10}\\
 \Delta_9(c)&=-2c-276c^2-1344c^3-\tfrac{37148}{105}c^4,\label{dpn:rb:eq:D9}\\
 \Delta_{10}(c)&=-2c-\tfrac{2459}5c^2-\tfrac{18455}3c^3-\tfrac{227819}{35}c^4

\begin{proof}
These are exact computations from the all-orders algorithm of

prints only $s_1,\dots,s_4$ and $v_2,\dots,v_4$ in its article; the values
above are its JSON record's $s_9,s_{10},v_9,v_{10}$ (``ten completed forward
and inverse orders'', with saddle and reversion residuals checked through
