\paragraph{Stale statements and priority caveats.}
Source 100-82's abstract says its logarithmic expansion ``proves and
strengthens the conjecture currently listed in OEIS A022629'', and source
100-89's that its corollary ``settles the current conjectures in OEIS A092484
and A265840--A265842''.  Both were true of the entries as consulted (the
intake did not re-fetch them) and blind to this report; here they are further
proofs, and only the material listed above is new.  Source 100-89's ``The
closest located source is Gikunda's 2026 thesis'' likewise predates this
report.  Both keep explicit caveats, kept here: source 100-82, ``The bounded
literature check is not an exhaustive priority determination'', ``No
submission or publication has been made''; source 100-89, ``The literature
comparison below is bounded and makes no exhaustive priority claim'', and
``These specific comparisons are not an exhaustive priority search''.  Both
call themselves ``unrefereed ordinary mathematics, not a Lean
formalization'' and credit Euler--Maclaurin summation, cumulant expansions,
saddle inversion, and the probabilistic local-limit architecture as classical.

%% ---------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{The smooth boundary sector}\label{dpn:rb:sec:boundary}
This subsection is source 100-89's Sections~1--3, translated.  Throughout,
$\mathfrak A\subset(0,\infty)$ is an arbitrary fixed compact set, and uniform
assertions in $\alpha$ refer to $\alpha\in\mathfrak A$.  For $t>0$ let
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:rb:eq:Xi}
 I_\alpha(t)=\int_0^\infty\log(1+x^\alpha e^{-tx})\dd x,\qquad
 \Xi_\alpha(t)=F(t)-I_\alpha(t),
\end{equation}
so $\Xi_\alpha$ is the lattice-minus-integral difference, and write
$\varpi(x)=\alpha\log x-tx$ for the log odds, so that $p(x)=1/(1+e^{-\varpi(x)})$.
Section~\ref{dpn:sec:sommerfeld} bounds it by $O_\alpha(s)$ and stresses that
it ``must not be discarded'' when a multiplicative equivalent is wanted;
source 100-89 identifies it.

\begin{theorem}[Smooth matched boundary; source 100-89]\label{dpn:rb:thm:smooth}
$\Xi_\alpha$ has a $C^\infty$ extension from $t>0$ to $t=0$, uniformly with
every fixed derivative for $\alpha\in\mathfrak A$.  Its Taylor coefficients
$\xi_r(\alpha)=\Xi_\alpha^{(r)}(0)/r!$ are real analytic on $(0,\infty)$ and
extend holomorphically to $\Re\alpha>0$.  For every fixed $D\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:rb:eq:Xiexp}
 \Xi_\alpha(t)=\sum_{r=0}^D\xi_r(\alpha)t^r+O_{\mathfrak A,D}(t^{D+1}).
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
Smoothness does not imply that the Taylor series $\sum_r\xi_r(\alpha)t^r$
converges, and no convergence is asserted \src{100-89}.  The holomorphy is
asserted by source 100-89 with a sketch; Lemma~\ref{dpn:rb:lem:holo} proves it.

\begin{proof}[Proof of the smoothness \src{100-89}]
Put $f(x;t)=\log(1+x^\alpha e^{-tx})$ and $\chi_r(x;t)=\partial_t^rf(x;t)$.
Choose an even integer $m_{\rm E}\ge2$ with $m_{\rm E}>r+1$.  For $t>0$,
Euler--Maclaurin summation from $x=1$ gives
\begin{align}\label{dpn:rb:eq:EM}
 \Xi_\alpha^{(r)}(t)
 ={}&\frac{\chi_r(1;t)}2-\int_0^1\chi_r(x;t)\dd x
 -\sum_{j=1}^{m_{\rm E}/2}\frac{B_{2j}}{(2j)!}\partial_x^{2j-1}\chi_r(1;t)\\
 &-\frac1{m_{\rm E}!}\int_1^\infty\widetilde B_{m_{\rm E}}(x)
                          \partial_x^{m_{\rm E}}\chi_r(x;t)\dd x,\notag
\end{align}
with the Bernoulli numbers $B_{2j}$ and the periodic Bernoulli polynomial
$\widetilde B_{m_{\rm E}}$.  All boundary terms at infinity vanish for $t>0$,
and differentiation in $t$ is legitimate by exponential decay.  With the
Bernoulli cumulant polynomials $b_r$ of \eqref{dpn:eq:cumulants} and
$p=p(x)$,
\[
 \chi_r(x;t)=(-x)^rb_r(p),\qquad r\ge1.
\]
For $r\ge2$, $b_r$ is divisible by $p(1-p)$; for $r=1$, $b_1=p$, but every
positive spatial derivative of $p$ has that factor, and since $m_{\rm E}>r$
every Leibniz term differentiates the cumulant at least once.  Using
$\varpi'(x)=\alpha/x-t$ and its derivatives,
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:rb:eq:derivbound}
 |\partial_x^{m_{\rm E}}\chi_r(x;t)|
 \le C\,x^{r-m_{\rm E}}(1+tx)^{m_{\rm E}}e^{-|\varpi(x)|},\qquad
 x\ge1,\ r\ge1,
\end{equation}
with $C$ uniform on $\mathfrak A$: the dominant $-x$ of $\chi_1$ is killed by
$m_{\rm E}\ge2$.  For $r=0$ the term with no derivative on $p$ is
$p\,\partial_x^{m_{\rm E}}\varpi=O(x^{-m_{\rm E}})$ and the others carry
$p(1-p)$, so
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:rb:eq:r0}
 |\partial_x^{m_{\rm E}}f(x;t)|\le Cx^{-m_{\rm E}}+Ct^{m_{\rm E}}e^{-|\varpi(x)|};
\end{equation}
the limiting logarithmic growth has not been discarded.  On $1\le x\le1/t$
the right side of \eqref{dpn:rb:eq:derivbound} is at most $Cx^{r-m_{\rm E}}$,
integrable since $m_{\rm E}>r+1$, and the derivative converges pointwise to
its value at $t=0$.  On the rest, uniformly on $\mathfrak A$,
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:rb:eq:tail}
 \int_{1/t}^\infty x^re^{-|\varpi(x)|}\dd x
 =O\bigl((t^{-1}\log(1/t))^{r+1}\bigr):
\end{equation}
use $e^{-|\varpi|}\le1$ up to $T=C_{\mathfrak A}t^{-1}\log(1/t)$, with
the constant chosen so that $x^\alpha\le e^{tx/2}$ for $x\ge T$, and
$e^{-|\varpi(x)|}\le e^{-tx/2}$ beyond.  Multiplied by $t^{m_{\rm E}}$ this is
$o(1)$.  This proves uniform $L^1$ convergence of the derivative in
\eqref{dpn:rb:eq:EM} for $r\ge1$, and \eqref{dpn:rb:eq:r0} does so for $r=0$.
The local terms and the integral over $[0,1]$ converge uniformly because
$\alpha$ stays away from zero.  Hence every $\Xi_\alpha^{(r)}(t)$ has a finite
uniform limit as $t\downarrow0$; the fundamental theorem of calculus, applied
successively, gives the smooth extension, and boundedness of the next
derivative gives \eqref{dpn:rb:eq:Xiexp}.
\end{proof}

Let $\mathfrak L_{m_{\rm E}}[h]$ denote the right side of \eqref{dpn:rb:eq:EM}
with $\chi_r(\cdot;t)$ replaced by a function $h$.  Then
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:rb:eq:functional}
 \xi_r(\alpha)=\frac{\mathfrak L_{m_{\rm E}}[\chi_r(\cdot\,;0)]}{r!},\qquad
 m_{\rm E}>r+1,
\end{equation}
a convergent formula, independent of $m_{\rm E}$ \src{100-89}.  For complex
$\alpha$ with $\Re\alpha>0$, source 100-89 uses the determinations
$\alpha\log x+\log(1+x^{-\alpha})$ on $x>1$ and $\log(1+x^\alpha)$ on $x<1$,
which match at $x=1$, and states that the spatial derivatives in
\eqref{dpn:rb:eq:functional} have locally uniform integrable bounds, so that
the coefficients are holomorphic on $\Re\alpha>0$.  ``This statement
concerns the limiting coefficients; it does not assume that the
positive-$t$ product is zero-free in a fixed complex neighborhood of $a$.''
The bounds are asserted, not proved; the following lemma supplies them.

\begin{lemma}[Holomorphy of the boundary coefficients; \wrt]\label{dpn:rb:lem:holo}
For every $r\ge0$, the right side of \eqref{dpn:rb:eq:functional} is
holomorphic in $\alpha$ on $\Re\alpha>0$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Fix a compact set $\mathfrak K\subset\{\Re\alpha>0\}$ and put
$\sigma_{\mathfrak K}=\min_{\mathfrak K}\Re\alpha>0$.  For $x>0$, $|x^\alpha|=x^{\Re\alpha}$
and $\arg x^\alpha=\Im\alpha\log x$, so $x^\alpha=-1$ would need $x=1$, where
$x^\alpha=1$; hence $1+x^{\pm\alpha}\ne0$.  On $x\ge1$ write $y=x^{-\alpha}$,
so $|y|\le1$ with equality only at $x=1$, where $y=1$.  The continuous
function $|1+y|$ on $[1,X]\times\mathfrak K$ is positive and tends to $1$ as
$x\to\infty$ uniformly on $\mathfrak K$; hence $|1+y|\ge\rho_{\mathfrak K}>0$
on $[1,\infty)\times\mathfrak K$.  Likewise $|1+x^\alpha|\ge\rho_{\mathfrak K}$
on $(0,1]\times\mathfrak K$, after decreasing $\rho_{\mathfrak K}$.

For $r\ge1$, $\chi_r(x;0)=(-x)^rb_r(p)$ with $p=1/(1+y)$, and for $r=0$ the
determination above is $\alpha\log x+\log(1+y)$ on $x\ge1$.  Use the Euler
operator $x\partial_x$.  Then $(x\partial_x)y=-\alpha y$, so for a function
$G$ holomorphic on a neighbourhood of
$\{|1+y|\ge\rho_{\mathfrak K},\ |y|\le1\}$,
$(x\partial_x)^iG(y)=(-\alpha)^i(y\partial_y)^iG(y)$, and
$\partial_x^{m}=x^{-m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sigma_{m,i}(x\partial_x)^i$ for $m\ge1$,
with integer coefficients $\sigma_{m,i}$ (signed Stirling numbers of the first
kind; there is no $i=0$ term).  Every function $(y\partial_y)^iG$ with $i\ge1$
is $y$ times a function bounded on that region, so it is
$O(|y|)=O(x^{-\sigma_{\mathfrak K}})$.  By Leibniz's rule applied to
$x^r\cdot b_r(1/(1+y))$, every term of $\partial_x^{m_{\rm E}}\chi_r(x;0)$ is
either $x^{r-m_{\rm E}}$ times some $(y\partial_y)^iG$ with $i\ge1$, or, from
the terms in which all derivatives fall on $x^r$, a multiple of
$x^{r-m_{\rm E}}b_r(p)$ with $m_{\rm E}\le r$, which does not occur because
$m_{\rm E}>r+1$.  Hence
\[
 |\partial_x^{m_{\rm E}}\chi_r(x;0)|\le C_{\mathfrak K}\,x^{r-m_{\rm E}-\sigma_{\mathfrak K}},
 \qquad x\ge1,\ \alpha\in\mathfrak K,
\]
and for $r=0$ the extra term $\partial_x^{m_{\rm E}}(\alpha\log x)
=\alpha(-1)^{m_{\rm E}-1}(m_{\rm E}-1)!\,x^{-m_{\rm E}}$ is integrable because
$m_{\rm E}\ge2$.  The integrand of the last term of \eqref{dpn:rb:eq:EM} is
therefore dominated, uniformly on $\mathfrak K$, by an integrable function,
and it is holomorphic in $\alpha$ for each $x$; by dominated convergence and
Morera's theorem the integral is holomorphic.  The boundary terms at $x=1$
are finitely many derivatives of functions holomorphic in $(x,\alpha)$ near
$\{1\}\times\mathfrak K$.  On $(0,1]$ the integrand $\chi_r(x;0)$ is
continuous, bounded on $(0,1]\times\mathfrak K$ (since $|x^\alpha|\le1$ and
$|1+x^\alpha|\ge\rho_{\mathfrak K}$; for $r=0$ the principal logarithm of $1+x^\alpha$ is
holomorphic there), and holomorphic in $\alpha$, so $\int_0^1$ is
holomorphic as well.  For real $\alpha$ both determinations are the real
logarithm, so the holomorphic function restricts to $r!\,\xi_r(\alpha)$, which
is therefore real analytic.
\end{proof}

\paragraph{Closed forms \src{100-89}.}
For $\alpha>1$, separating $\alpha\log x$ from $\log(1+x^{-\alpha})$, applying
Stirling's formula and $\int_0^\infty\log(1+x^{-\alpha})\dd x=\pi\csc(\pi/\alpha)$
(integration by parts) gives
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:rb:eq:xi0}
 \xi_0(\alpha)=\frac\alpha2\log(2\pi)+\sum_{k\ge1}\log(1+k^{-\alpha})
                         -\pi\csc(\pi/\alpha).
\end{equation}
At $\alpha=2$, Euler's product for $\sinh$ gives $\xi_0(2)=\log(1-e^{-2\pi})$;
and $\xi_0(1)=\frac12\log(2\pi)-1$, the continued value at the removable
resonance, or directly from Stirling's formula for $\log(1+x)$.  For
$\alpha>2$, $\chi_1(x;0)=-x+x/(1+x^\alpha)$ and $\mathfrak L[-x]=\frac1{12}$, so
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:rb:eq:xi1}
 \xi_1(\alpha)=\frac1{12}+\sum_{k\ge1}\frac{k}{1+k^\alpha}
                       -\frac\pi\alpha\csc(2\pi/\alpha),\qquad
 \xi_1(1)=\frac7{12}-\gamma_{\rm E},\quad
 \xi_1(2)=\frac1{12}-\Re\psi(1+\ii),
\end{equation}
with Euler's constant $\gamma_{\rm E}$ and $\psi=\Gamma'/\Gamma$; the two
separately divergent pieces of \eqref{dpn:rb:eq:xi1} are not evaluated
individually at $\alpha=2$.  For $r\ge2$ and $\alpha>r+1$,
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:rb:eq:xir}
 \xi_r(\alpha)=\frac1{r!}\left\{
 \sum_{k\ge1}(-k)^rb_r\Bigl(\frac{k^\alpha}{1+k^\alpha}\Bigr)
 -\frac\pi\alpha\Bigl(\frac{r+1}{\alpha}\Bigr)^{r-1}
       \csc\Bigl(\frac{\pi(r+1)}\alpha\Bigr)\right\}:
\end{equation}
with $v=(r+1)/\alpha\in(0,1)$ and $y=x^\alpha$, integrating by parts $r-2$
times reduces $b_r$ to $b_2=y/(1+y)^2$ and gives
$\int_0^\infty x^rb_r(x^\alpha/(1+x^\alpha))\dd x
=\frac{(-v)^{r-2}}\alpha\frac{\pi v}{\sin\pi v}$; multiplication by $(-1)^r$
gives \eqref{dpn:rb:eq:xir}.  Formula \eqref{dpn:rb:eq:functional} continues it
through all apparent positive-$\alpha$ resonances, so these poles create no
fractional or logarithmic powers in $\Xi_\alpha(t)$.

\paragraph{A finite algorithm for integer powers \src{100-89}.}
For $\alpha=p\in\N$ and $r\ge1$, $\chi_r(x;0)=(-x)^rb_r(x^p/(1+x^p))$ is
rational, with poles among $z^p=-1$.  Polynomial division and partial
fractions, followed by
\begin{gather}
 \mathfrak L[x^\ell]=\zeta(-\ell)\ \ (\ell\ge0),\qquad
 \mathfrak L[(x-z)^{-1}]=\log(-z)-\psi(1-z),\notag\\
 \mathfrak L[(x-z)^{-j}]=\zeta(j,1-z)-\frac{(-z)^{1-j}}{j-1}\ \ (j\ge2),\label{dpn:rb:eq:rule}
\end{gather}
compute $r!\,\xi_r(p)$ exactly.  The first is Faulhaber's formula inside the
Euler--Maclaurin functional; the others follow from finite digamma sums and
convergent Hurwitz zeta sums.  Principal logarithms are used and conjugate
roots are paired; no root is positive real, and $\Re(1-z)>0$.  A finite
constant formula, valid also for $p=1$, is
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:rb:eq:gammaroot}
 \xi_0(p)=\frac p2\log(2\pi)-\sum_{z^p=-1}\log\Gamma(1-z)
                         +\sum_{z^p=-1}z\{1-\log(-z)\},
\end{equation}
from Stirling's formula for each $\log(x-z)$ in $\log(1+x^p)$, whose
functional is $\frac12\log(2\pi)-\log\Gamma(1-z)+z-z\log(-z)$.  For example,
\[
 \xi_2(1)=-\frac1{24}+\frac{3\gamma_{\rm E}}2-\frac{\pi^2}{12},\qquad
 \xi_3(1)=-\frac{19}{36}-\frac{8\gamma_{\rm E}}3+\frac{\pi^2}4-\frac{\zeta(3)}3.
\]
The delivered symbolic checkers implement this functional through order six
for $p=1$ and reconstruct four square-weight rational cumulants at the poles
$\pm\ii$.

\mergenoted{The intake confirmed the six printed constants independently, by
fitting a polynomial in $t$ to $\Xi_\alpha(t)$ computed directly (40-digit
mpmath sums and quadrature) at $t=0.0075,\dots,0.04$: $\xi_0(1)$, $\xi_1(1)$,
$\xi_0(2)$, $\xi_1(2)$ agree to 10--12 significant digits, $\xi_2(1)$ to 9 and
$\xi_3(1)$ to 6 (the fit's own truncation error).  \wrt\ The write repeated this and also
compared $\Xi_\alpha(t)$ at $t=0.0025$ and $0.00125$ with
\eqref{dpn:rb:eq:xi0}--\eqref{dpn:rb:eq:xi1} at $\alpha=1.5,2.5,3$, evaluating the
slowly convergent sums with Hurwitz zeta tails: $(\Xi_\alpha(t)-\xi_0)/t$
settles (at $\alpha=1.5$ near $-0.00477$) and $(\Xi_\alpha(t)-\xi_0-\xi_1t)/t^2$
settles at $\alpha=2.5,3$.  Plain \texttt{mpmath.nsum} is not accurate enough
for these sums near $\alpha=2$ (it was off by about $0.01$ in $\xi_1(2.5)$ and
$\xi_0(1.5)$); the delivered programs use it only at $\alpha=3$, where it is
accurate.  These are numerical confirmations, not certificates.}

%% ---------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Relative expansions at the integral saddle}\label{dpn:rb:sec:relative}
This subsection is source 100-89's Theorem~1.2, Corollary~1.3 and
Sections~4--5, with source 100-82's Theorem~1.1 as the case $\alpha=1$.  Fix
$J\ge1$ and put $D=2J+2$ (source 100-89 writes $M=J-1$ and $D=2M+4$).  Let
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:rb:eq:FD}
 F_D(t)=I_\alpha(t)+\sum_{r=0}^D\xi_r(\alpha)t^r,
\end{equation}
so $F(t)-F_D(t)=O(t^{D+1})$ with every fixed derivative.  For all large $n$
let $t_D$ be the unique small positive solution of $-F_D'(t_D)=n$, and put
$B_D=F_D''(t_D)$, $\kappa_{j,D}=(-1)^jF_D^{(j)}(t_D)$, and
$\mathcal E_\ell[D]$ for $\mathcal E_\ell$ of \eqref{dpn:eq:edgepoly} with
$\kappa_j,B$ replaced by $\kappa_{j,D},B_D$.

\begin{theorem}[All-orders relative expansion with the exact integral; source 100-89]\label{dpn:rb:thm:allorders}
Uniformly for $\alpha\in\mathfrak A$, as $n\to\infty$,
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:rb:eq:allorders}
 a_\alpha(n)=\frac{\exp\{F_D(t_D)+nt_D\}}{\sqrt{2\pi B_D}}
 \Bigl\{1+\sum_{\ell=1}^{J-1}\mathcal E_\ell[D]+O_{\mathfrak A,J}(t_D^{J})\Bigr\},
 \qquad t_D\sim\frac{\alpha\log\sqrt{2n}}{\sqrt{2n}}.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
The exact integral stays in the exponent; only finitely many boundary
coefficients enter.  A fixed expansion in inverse powers of $\log n$ would
not give the same relative accuracy \src{100-89}.

\begin{proof}[Proof \src{100-89}]
At the lattice saddle, that is, with $F$, $t_n$, $B$ and $\kappa_j$ in
place of $F_D$, $t_D$, $B_D$ and $\kappa_{j,D}$, \eqref{dpn:rb:eq:allorders} is
Theorem~\ref{dpn:thm:saddle} in the cumulative form \eqref{dpn:48:eq:edgeworth}, since $t=\alpha\eps$; source
100-89's Section~4 re-proves it (Table~\ref{dpn:rb:tab:dup}).  Replace the
lattice by the model: Theorem~\ref{dpn:rb:thm:smooth} with derivatives gives
$F^{(j)}(t)-F_D^{(j)}(t)=O(t^{D+1-j})$ for $0\le j\le D$.  The integral
variance is positive and of the size \eqref{dpn:eq:varianceleading}, so the
small saddle of $F_D$ is unique, and its displacement from the lattice saddle
is $O(t_D^D/B_D)$.  The phase difference is $O(t_D^{D+1})$ plus a smaller
quadratic displacement term.  Substituting the $F_D$ derivatives in each of
the finitely many normalized cumulant terms changes them by $o(t_D^{J})$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[The remainder scale; \wrt]\label{dpn:rb:rem:sharp}
The remainder $O(t^J)=O((\alpha s/M)^J)$ is the conservative scale of
Theorem~\ref{dpn:thm:saddle}, not the sharp $O(\epsh^J)$, $\epsh=1/(\alpha Ms)$,
of Theorem~\ref{dpn:jh:thm:saddle}; source 100-89 claims nothing sharper, and
its expansion is not sharper than Section~\ref{dpn:jh:sec:sharp}.  The
replacement step transfers the sharp scale: with $D=2J+2$,
\[
 a_\alpha(n)=\frac{\exp\{F_D(t_D)+nt_D\}}{\sqrt{2\pi B_D}}
 \Bigl\{1+\sum_{\ell=1}^{J-1}\mathcal E_\ell[D]+O_{\alpha,J}(\epsh^{J})\Bigr\}.
\]
Indeed the changes in the proof above are: the phase, by relative
$O(t^{D+1})$; the saddle, displaced by $\delta=O(t^{D}/B)$, which changes the
variance by relative $O(|\kappa_3|\delta/B)=O(|\kappa_3|t^D/B^2)=O(t^Ds/M^2)$;
and each normalized cumulant with $j\le2J$, by $O(t^{D+1-j}B^{-j/2})$ from the
substitution and $O(|\kappa_{j+1}|\delta B^{-j/2})$ from the displacement.
Since $t^{D+1}/\epsh^J=\alpha^{D+1+J}s^{D+1+J}M^{J-D-1}\to0$ and
$t^Ds/(M^2\epsh^J)=\alpha^{D+J}s^{D+J+1}M^{J-D-2}\to0$, all of them are
$o(\epsh^J)$, so Theorem~\ref{dpn:jh:thm:saddle} (with its $J-1$) at the
lattice saddle gives the display.  This is a bookkeeping consequence of two
proved theorems, not a new estimate.  (Dated note, 5~October 2026: the first
version listed the saddle change as relative $O(t^{D}/B)$ and omitted the
displacement terms; an independent check made by the intake after the write
supplied them and found the conclusion unchanged.)
\end{remark}

For direct use, let $t^{\rm int}$ solve $-I_\alpha'(t^{\rm int})=n$ and put
$B^{\rm int}=I_\alpha''(t^{\rm int})$, $I^{(j)}_\alpha=I_\alpha^{(j)}(t^{\rm int})$.
\begin{corollary}[First order at the integral saddle; source 100-89]\label{dpn:rb:cor:first}
Uniformly for $\alpha\in\mathfrak A$,
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:rb:eq:first}
 a_\alpha(n)=\frac{\exp\{I_\alpha(t^{\rm int})+nt^{\rm int}+\xi_0(\alpha)\}}{\sqrt{2\pi B^{\rm int}}}
 \biggl\{1+\xi_1(\alpha)t^{\rm int}+\frac{I^{(4)}_\alpha}{8(B^{\rm int})^2}
 -\frac{5(I^{(3)}_\alpha)^2}{24(B^{\rm int})^3}+O_{\mathfrak A}\bigl((t^{\rm int})^2\bigr)\biggr\}.
\end{equation}
In particular, at $\alpha=1$, where $\xi_0(1)=\frac12\log(2\pi)-1$,
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:rb:eq:a1}
 a_1(n)\sim\frac{\exp\{I_1(t^{\rm int})+nt^{\rm int}-1\}}{\sqrt{B^{\rm int}}}
 \qquad\text{(source 100-82's Theorem~1.1)}.
\end{equation}
\end{corollary}
The integral cumulant block in \eqref{dpn:rb:eq:first} is kept exactly, rather
than further truncated on a different asymptotic scale \src{100-89}.  The two
factors $\sqrt{2\pi}$ in \eqref{dpn:rb:eq:a1} cancel \src{100-82}.
\begin{proof}[Proof \src{100-89}]
Start at $t^{\rm int}$.  Smoothness gives $F'=I_\alpha'+\xi_1+O(t)$, hence
$t_n-t^{\rm int}=-\xi_1/B^{\rm int}+O(t^{\rm int}/B^{\rm int})
+O(|I^{(3)}_\alpha|/(B^{\rm int})^3)$.  The change in the stationary phase is
$O(1/B^{\rm int})$; the relative variance change is
$O(1/B^{\rm int})+O(|I^{(3)}_\alpha|/(B^{\rm int})^2)=O(s/M^2)=O(t^2)$, and
$1/B^{\rm int}=O(s/M^3)=o(t^2)$.  The lattice--integral differences in fixed
cumulants are bounded, hence smaller still after normalization.  Expanding
$\exp(\xi_1t^{\rm int}+O(t^2))$ and using $1+\mathcal E_1$ gives
\eqref{dpn:rb:eq:first}, with all saddle-shift errors at the claimed order.
Source 100-82's proof of \eqref{dpn:rb:eq:a1} is different; it is the
second route of Section~\ref{dpn:rb:sec:routes}.
\end{proof}
\mergenoted{Comparison with the sharp first correction: here $\xi_1t$ is of
order $s/M$, larger than $\mathcal E_1\sim-3/(8\alpha Ms)$
(Theorem~\ref{dpn:jh:thm:saddle}) by a factor of order $s^2$; at the integral
saddle the leading correction is the boundary term, not the Edgeworth term.
The $O(t^2)$ remainder is again the conservative scale.}

%% ---------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Exact-range inversion}\label{dpn:rb:sec:inverse}
This subsection is source 100-89's Section~6.  Fix $J\ge1$, $D=2J+2$, continue
$t_D$, $B_D$ and $\mathcal E_\ell[D]$ smoothly to large real $x$ in place of
$n$, and define
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:rb:eq:Psiint}
 \Psi^{\rm int}_J(x)=F_D(t_D)+xt_D-\tfrac12\log(2\pi B_D)
                         +\log\Bigl(1+\sum_{\ell=1}^{J-1}\mathcal E_\ell[D]\Bigr),
\end{equation}
the logarithm being defined eventually because the sum is $O(t_D)$.  For
integer $n$, Theorem~\ref{dpn:rb:thm:allorders} gives
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:rb:eq:logerror}
 \log a_\alpha(n)=\Psi^{\rm int}_J(n)+O(t_D^{J}).
\end{equation}
Write $P(t)$ for $1+\sum_{\ell<J}\mathcal E_\ell[D]$ as a function of the saddle
variable.  Implicit differentiation gives $t_D'(x)=-1/B_D$ and
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:rb:eq:Psiprime}
 (\Psi^{\rm int}_J)'(x)=t_D+\frac{\partial_tB_D}{2B_D^2}-\frac{\partial_tP}{P\,B_D},
\end{equation}
with $\partial_t$ the derivative in the saddle variable.

\begin{proposition}[Slope of the inverse model; source 100-89, completed \wrt]\label{dpn:rb:prop:slope}
$(\Psi^{\rm int}_J)'(x)=t_D+O(s/M^2)+O(s^2/M^3)\sim t_D>0$, uniformly on
$\mathfrak A$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Source 100-89 states the two bounds $\partial_tB_D=O(M^4/s)$ and
$\partial_tP=O(M\eps)=O(s)$, the latter from ``fixed-order moment bounds one
order higher'' and the fact that every nonconstant term of $P$ has weight at
least two.  \wrt\ The details: the integral analogue of
\eqref{dpn:eq:mombound}, $\int_0^\infty x^jp(1-p)\dd x=O(M^{j+1}/s)$, holds by
the same proof (or by the sum--integral comparison of
Section~\ref{dpn:48:sec:operator}), and the boundary polynomial in
\eqref{dpn:rb:eq:FD} changes each derivative of order at most $D$ by $O(1)$; so
$|\kappa_{j,D}|=O(M^{j+1}/s)$ for $2\le j\le D$, and $B_D\asymp M^3/s$.  Then
$\partial_tB_D=-\kappa_{3,D}$ gives the first bound, and
$\partial_tB_D/(2B_D^2)=O((M^4/s)(s/M^3)^2)=O(s/M^2)$.  Next,
$\partial_t\kappa_{j,D}=-\kappa_{j+1,D}$, so for the normalized cumulants
$x_j=\kappa_{j,D}B_D^{-j/2}$,
\[
 \partial_tx_j=-\frac{\kappa_{j+1,D}}{B_D^{j/2}}
 +\frac j2\,\frac{\kappa_{j,D}\kappa_{3,D}}{B_D^{j/2+1}}
 =O\Bigl(\frac{M^{j+2}}s\Bigl(\frac s{M^3}\Bigr)^{j/2}\Bigr)
 =O\bigl(M\eps^{(j-2)/2}\bigr),
\]
both terms having this size; only $j+1\le2J+1\le D$ is used, the ``one order
higher''.  Each monomial $\prod_jx_j^{m_j}$ of $P$ has weight
$w=\sum_j(j-2)m_j\ge2$, because the only weight-one monomial, $x_3$, has odd
degree and does not occur; by the product rule its derivative is
$O(M\eps^{w/2})=O(M\eps)$.  Hence $\partial_tP=O(M\eps)=O(s)$, while
$P=1+O(\eps)$, so $\partial_tP/(PB_D)=O(s\cdot s/M^3)=O(s^2/M^3)$.  Both
error terms are $o(t_D)$ since $t_D\asymp s/M$.
\end{proof}

Let $n^{\rm int}_{*,J}(Y)$ denote the eventual real inverse of
$\Psi^{\rm int}_J$, and, as in Theorem~\ref{dpn:thm:inverse}, $N_\alpha(Y)$ the
least $n$ with $\log a_\alpha(n)\ge Y$.

\begin{theorem}[Exact-range inverse; source 100-89]\label{dpn:rb:thm:inverse}
For every fixed $J\ge1$,
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:rb:eq:exactinverse}
 n^{\rm int}_{*,J}\bigl(\log a_\alpha(n)\bigr)=n+O\bigl(t_D(n)^{J-1}\bigr).
\end{equation}
For $J\ge2$, sequence indices are therefore recovered eventually by
$n=\lfloor n^{\rm int}_{*,J}(\log a_\alpha(n))+\frac12\rfloor$.  For arbitrary
large $Y$, put $x=n^{\rm int}_{*,J}(Y)$ and $t_x=t_D(x)$.  There is a
constant $C$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:rb:eq:threshold}
 \bigl\lceil x-Ct_x^{J-1}\bigr\rceil\le N_\alpha(Y)\le\bigl\lceil x+Ct_x^{J-1}\bigr\rceil.
\end{equation}
If $J\ge2$ and $\operatorname{dist}(x,\mathbb Z)>Ct_x^{J-1}$, then
$N_\alpha(Y)=\lceil x\rceil$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}[Proof \src{100-89}]
Proposition~\ref{dpn:rb:prop:slope} and \eqref{dpn:rb:eq:logerror} give
\eqref{dpn:rb:eq:exactinverse} by the mean value theorem; $t_D$ varies by a
factor $1+o(1)$ on bounded $x$-intervals.  For thresholds, the coefficients
increase strictly for $n\ge1$ by the injection of
Section~\ref{dpn:sec:inverse}, also for nonintegral $\alpha$.  By
\eqref{dpn:rb:eq:logerror} and the positive slope, integers at least $Ct_x^{J-1}$
below $x$ lie below the threshold and those at least that far above lie
above it, which is \eqref{dpn:rb:eq:threshold}.  The nearest-integer claim uses
$t_D^{J-1}\to0$ for $J\ge2$.
\end{proof}
There is no unconditional ceiling rule for arbitrary thresholds, and no
assertion about how often thresholds fall into the exceptional
neighbourhoods of integers.  The exact-range error is
$O((\log n/\sqrt n)^{J-1})$ for every fixed $J$ \src{100-89}.

\mergenoted{Relation to Theorem~\ref{dpn:jh:thm:threshold} \src{75-01}.  Exact
index recovery at sequence values already follows from
\eqref{dpn:jh:eq:atvalue}: the displacement $-3/(8\alpha^2s^2)$ of the
Gaussian inverse tends to zero, and with $\Psi_1$ it is
$O(M^{-1}s^{-3})$.  Source 100-89's theorem gives the same conclusion for
every truncation order, at the conservative scale: for $J=2$ its error
$O(t)=O(\alpha s/M)$ is weaker than the $\Psi_1$ bound by a factor of order
$s^4$, and for large $J$ it is the only all-orders statement here.  By
Remark~\ref{dpn:rb:rem:sharp} the error in \eqref{dpn:rb:eq:exactinverse}
improves to $O(\epsh^J/t)$.  Its conditional ceiling rule is consistent with
the sentence after Theorem~\ref{dpn:jh:thm:threshold}: no universal rounding
rule exists for arbitrary thresholds; source 100-89 says the same.  The
threshold step is the staircase theorem \texttt{p0:thm:staircase} of
\cite{tai}.}

\begin{remark}[A resummed inverse at $\alpha=1$; source 100-82]\label{dpn:rb:rem:resummed}
Source 100-82 inverts $\Psi^{\rm int}(x)=I_1(t^{\rm int})+xt^{\rm int}-1
-\frac12\log B^{\rm int}(t^{\rm int})$, the logarithm of \eqref{dpn:rb:eq:a1}.
Exact differentiation gives $(\Psi^{\rm int})'(x)=t^{\rm int}
+\partial_tB^{\rm int}/(2(B^{\rm int})^2)$, and $|\partial_tB^{\rm int}|\le\int
x^3p(1-p)\dd x=O(M^4/s)$, so $(\Psi^{\rm int})'=t^{\rm int}+O(s/M^2)\sim
t^{\rm int}$.  If $\nu(y)$ is the eventual inverse of $e^{\Psi^{\rm int}}$,
monotone bracketing gives $N_1(\log y)=\nu(y)+o(1/t^{\rm int}_{\nu(y)})+O(1)$;
the saddle scale varies by $1+o(1)$ on this window since
$(t^{\rm int})'=-1/B^{\rm int}$.  ``A relative forward equivalent does not
justify an exact rounding rule.''  This is weaker than both
Theorem~\ref{dpn:jh:thm:threshold} and Theorem~\ref{dpn:rb:thm:inverse}.
\end{remark}

%% ---------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Second routes and forms that differ}\label{dpn:rb:sec:routes}
\paragraph{The constant $\frac12\log(2\pi)-1$ by an $L^1$ limit \src{100-82}.}
At $\alpha=1$ write $f_t(x)=\log(1+xe^{-tx})$, $t=se^{-s}$, $M=e^s$.  Then
$f_t(0)=0$, $f_t'(0)=1$ and
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:rb:eq:fsecond}
 f_t''=-\frac{p^2}{x^2}-\frac{2tp(1-p)}x+t^2p(1-p),\qquad p=p(x),
\end{equation}
with continuous limits at zero.  The first term converges in $L^1(0,\infty)$
to $-(1+x)^{-2}$, since $p(x)\le x/(1+x)$; and
$\int_0^\infty p\,x^{-1}\dd x=O(s)$, $\int_0^\infty p\dd x=O(M)$, so the other two
terms have $L^1$ norms $O(ts)$ and $O(t^2M)$, which tend to zero.
Second-order Euler--Maclaurin summation gives
$F(t)-I_1(t)=-\frac1{12}-\frac12\int_0^\infty\widetilde B_2(x)f_t''(x)\dd x$,
and the $L^1$ convergence allows the limit inside the remainder.  The
limiting functional is evaluated by the finite formula for $\log(1+x)$ on
$[0,X]$: by Stirling's formula
$\sum_{k\le X}\log(1+k)-\int_0^X\log(1+x)\dd x-\frac12\log(X+1)\to
\frac12\log(2\pi)-1$, with the same Bernoulli remainder.  Hence
$F(t)=I_1(t)+\frac12\log(2\pi)-1+o(1)$: the small-part boundary is essential
for this constant.  This is the case $r=0$, $\alpha=1$ of
Theorem~\ref{dpn:rb:thm:smooth}, proved by a second-order expansion only.

\paragraph{The local limit at the integral saddle \src{100-82}.}
For $h=xp$ and $h=x^2p(1-p)$ the bounded-variation inequality
$|\sum_kh(k)-\int_0^\infty h|\le\int_0^\infty|h'|$ (as in
Section~\ref{dpn:48:sec:operator}) gives, at $\alpha=1$,
$\E S=-I_1'(t)+O(sM)$ and $\Var S=I_1''(t)+O(sM^2)=I_1''(t)(1+o(1))$; the
third absolute centred moment sum is $O(M^4/s)+O(sM^3)=o((I_1'')^{3/2})$.  At
$t=t^{\rm int}$ the target is therefore within $o(\sqrt{\Var S})$ of the mean,
and the block bounds give
$\Prob(S=n)\sim(2\pi B^{\rm int})^{-1/2}$, the uniform local limit
\eqref{dpn:eq:uniformllt} evaluated at the integral saddle.  With the constant
above this proves \eqref{dpn:rb:eq:a1} without Theorem~\ref{dpn:rb:thm:smooth}.

\paragraph{A third proof of the block inequality \src{100-82 and 100-89}.}
For a block of $\ell$ consecutive integers, the sum in
Lemma~\ref{dpn:lem:block} is at least $(\ell-|Z_\ell|)/2$ with
$Z_\ell=\sum_{j<\ell}e^{\ii j\theta}$.  If $|\theta|\ge2\pi/\ell$, then
$|Z_\ell|\le\pi/|\theta|\le\ell/2$.  At smaller angles both sources take a
sub-block of length $m=\lfloor\ell/8\rfloor$ and use the identity
\[
 m^2-|Z_m|^2=\sum_{i,j=0}^{m-1}\bigl(1-\cos((i-j)\theta)\bigr)\ge c\,m^4\theta^2,
\]
from $1-\cos u\ge cu^2$ on a fixed small interval; hence
$m-|Z_m|\ge c'm^3\theta^2$, independently of the starting point.  This
replaces the uniform-convergence step of the first two proofs.

\paragraph{The Sommerfeld expansion with the operator $\Top$ \src{100-82 and 100-89}.}
Source 100-89's (31) is, at $t=\alpha se^{-s}$,
\[
 I_\alpha(t)=\alpha M\Bigl\{\frac s2-1+\sum_{j=1}^J\frac{2\eta(2j)}{\alpha^{2j}}\,
 \frac{\Top^{2j-1}s}{s}\Bigr\}+O_{\mathfrak A,J}(Ms^{-2J-2}),
\]
with $\Top=\frac s{s-1}\frac{\dd}{\dd s}$, and source 100-82's Lemma~4.1 is its
case $\alpha=1$.  This is Lemma~\ref{dpn:lem:smoothing}: $s^{-1}\Top^{2j-1}s
=U_{2j-1}(s)$ (for instance $s^{-1}\Top s=1/(s-1)=U_1$ and
$s^{-1}\Top^3s=(2s+1)/(s-1)^5=U_3$), and, as $\Top s=f(s)$, it is also source
48's \eqref{dpn:48:eq:Sseries}.  The kernel moments are written
$\int_\R u^{2j-2}\log(1+e^{-|u|})\dd u=2(2j-2)!\,\eta(2j)$ (sources 100-82 and
100-89 write $2(1-2^{1-2j})\zeta(2j)$ for $2\eta(2j)$, and $D_b$ for $\Top$).
Both prove it by inverting $u=tx-\alpha\log x$ near $M$ on $|u|\le cs$ and
Taylor-expanding the Jacobian, with $\Top^r$ of the Jacobian
$O_r(s^{-r-1})$ by induction from $s/(s-1)=1+1/(s-1)$.  Source 100-82 adds the
lower edge explicitly: $\int_0^\infty\max(\log x-tx,0)\dd x
=M(s/2-1)+x_--tx_-^2/2$, with the lower zero $x_-\to1$.

\paragraph{Eliminating the saddle as a minimized phase \src{100-82 and 100-89}.}
At $\alpha=1$ write $\mathcal A_J(s)$ for the braces above, so that
$I_1(se^{-s})=e^s\mathcal A_J(s)+O(e^ss^{-2J-2})$.  Both sources solve
formally, for $\delta=s-r=O(r^{-2})$,
\[
 e^{2\delta}\,\frac{2[\mathcal A_J(r+\delta)+\mathcal A_J'(r+\delta)]}{r+\delta-1}=1,
\]
and expand $e^\delta\mathcal A_J(r+\delta)+\frac12(r+\delta)e^{-\delta}$, whose
coefficients through $r^{-R}$ are $r-1+\sum_{j\le R}P_j(\pi^2/6)r^{-j}$ for
every sufficiently large $J$; source 100-89 multiplies by $\alpha$ for general
$\alpha$.  With $\mathcal A_J=s/2-1+d_1(s)$ this is \eqref{dpn:eq:rfroms} and
\eqref{dpn:eq:formalH}: $2(\mathcal A_J+\mathcal A_J')/(s-1)=Q_1(s)$, so the
stationary equation is $r=s+\frac12\log Q_1(s)$, and $e^{\delta}=Q_1^{-1/2}$
turns the expanded value into $(s(Q_1+1)/2-1+d_1)/\sqrt{Q_1}$.  The
justification differs from the first two: the exact integral saddle
minimizes $I_1(t)+nt$; on $|s-r|\le1$ the approximate phase
$e^s\mathcal A_J(s)+nse^{-s}$ is uniformly within $O(Kr^{-2J-2})$ of it and
strictly convex, with second derivative comparable to $Kr$; so the two minima
differ by the same amount, and the implicit function theorem in $z=1/r$
gives the unique branch $\delta=O(z^2)$.  The first terms are
$\delta=-cr^{-2}-cr^{-3}+c^2r^{-4}+O(r^{-5})$, which is \eqref{dpn:48:eq:sfirst},
and $s_4=c-c^2/2+2\cdot\frac{7\pi^4}{360}=P_4(\pi^2/6)$ \src{100-82}.

\paragraph{The inverse equation \src{100-82}.}
With $X_0=Y/w=K_0$ and $C=w+1=R_0$, source 100-82's equation
$e^{\varepsilon}(C+\varepsilon-1+\sum_js_j(C+\varepsilon)^{-j})=C-1$ for the
inverse correction is \eqref{dpn:eq:Einverse} with $E=e^\varepsilon$, as for
source 75-06; its reversion $\varepsilon=-cC^{-2}-cC^{-3}+(c^2-c)C^{-4}+O(C^{-5})$
gives the coefficients $-c$, $-c$, $\frac32c^2-c$ of \eqref{dpn:eq:inverseE}.
The remainder is transported by the slope $\log X$ of the leading phase in
$X=\sqrt{2n}$, as in Section~\ref{dpn:sec:inverse}.

%% ---------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Literature, scope and non-claims of sources 100-82 and 100-89}\label{dpn:rb:sec:scope}
Source 100-89 compares four general theories, none of which supplies these
boundary coefficients: Granovsky--Stark \cite{GS} allow inside weights at most
one, and their decreasing-weight examples differ from $k^\alpha$;
Bridges, Brindle, Bringmann and Franke \cite{BBBF} study nonnegative
multiplicities in products $\prod(1-q^k)^{-f(k)}$; Ahmadi, G\'omez-A\'iza and
Ward \cite{AGW} place their weights outside the distinct-part factors; and the
exponential Hayman criterion of Cant\'on, Fern\'andez, Fern\'andez and Maci\'a
\cite{Canton} assumes nonnegative coefficients of the logarithm, unavailable
here.  ``General saddle methods remain central to the proof.''  It reads
Gikunda's thesis \cite{gikunda} as Section~1.2 does: its Lemma~4.2.2 and
Proposition~4.2.5 impose a shrinking tilt $a=O(t^\delta)\to0$, which does not
cover fixed positive powers.  Source 100-82 cites Naranjo and Ram\'irez
\cite{NR}, ``printed page~12'', who identify this sequence and product but
``do not derive its asymptotics'', and Granovsky--Stark as above.
\mergenoted{Source 100-89 cites the thesis at the ResearchGate address
402798310 (as source 77-51's source check did); this report keeps the DOI of
\cite{gikunda}.}

\paragraph{Non-claims.}
Source 100-89: the results are uniform only for $\alpha$ in a fixed compact
positive interval; they do not address $\alpha$ growing with $n$, convergence
of the boundary Taylor series, optimal truncation, or a complete exponentially
small lattice expansion; no effective onset is known; exact-range rounding and
arbitrary-threshold rounding have different, explicitly stated scopes; ``The
symbolic checks verify finite coefficient identities; they do not replace the
analytic all-orders and minor-arc proofs''; and a uniform theory for growing
$\alpha$, ``possibly involving a theta factor, is a separate research problem
and is not a consequence claimed here''.  Source 100-82: no convergent full
transseries, no remainder uniform as the truncation order grows, no exact
threshold rounding rule, no exhaustive novelty; ``Finite logarithmic
truncations are not confused with relative coefficient asymptotics'';
quadratures are orientation, not proof premises; the lower boundary ``is
negligible at every inverse-logarithmic scale but cannot be omitted from the
relative equivalent''.  Neither claims Lean or Rocq verification, and neither
uses a repository theorem.

%% ---------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Further questions and research (sources 100-82 and 100-89)}\label{dpn:rb:sec:questions}
Every claim of the two sources is proved above or listed here (Vladimir's
standing rule of 4 October 2026).  Source 100-89's two sketched steps, the
holomorphy of $\xi_r$ and the slope bound of its inverse model, are completed
by Lemma~\ref{dpn:rb:lem:holo} and Proposition~\ref{dpn:rb:prop:slope}; no
claim of either source was found to be wrong.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textbf{100.\arabic*}, leftmargin=*]
\item \textbf{The size of the boundary coefficients.}  Source: source 100-89,
  after Theorem~1.1 and Section~7.2 (convergence and optimal truncation are
  ``not addressed'').  Open: the growth of $\xi_r(\alpha)$ in $r$; whether
  $\sum_r\xi_r(\alpha)t^r$ converges near $t=0$ or is Gevrey, and what
  optimal truncation of \eqref{dpn:rb:eq:Xiexp} gives.  Sketch: none; the
  functional \eqref{dpn:rb:eq:functional} and the Hurwitz rule
  \eqref{dpn:rb:eq:rule} give every coefficient at integer $\alpha$.  Overlaps
  project~2 and question 75-06.2.
\item \textbf{Effective onset.}  Source: both sources (``No effective onset
  is claimed'').  Open: explicit constants in
  Theorems~\ref{dpn:rb:thm:smooth}, \ref{dpn:rb:thm:allorders}
  and~\ref{dpn:rb:thm:inverse}, so that the threshold bracket
  \eqref{dpn:rb:eq:threshold} can be certified at a stated $Y$.  Overlaps
  project~8, 48.7, 75-01.8 and 77-51.1.
\item \textbf{An exponentially small lattice correction.}  Source: both
  sources (no complete exponentially small expansion, no convergent
  transseries).  Open, overlapping projects~1--2 and 48.1; the critical
  window of Section~\ref{dpn:ct:sec:theta} is the only regime in which the
  noncentral contributions are computed.
\item \textbf{Uniformity in the truncation order.}  Source: source 100-82
  (``uniform remainder as truncation order grows'' not claimed).  Open: a
  remainder in Theorem~\ref{dpn:thm:log} uniform in $R$, or a proof that none
  exists.
\item \textbf{Rounding at arbitrary thresholds.}  Source: source 100-82 (``No
  exact ceiling rule is asserted'') and source 100-89 (``no assertion about
  the frequency of their near-integer exceptions'').  Re-scoped:
  Theorem~\ref{dpn:rb:thm:inverse} gives the ceiling rule off an
  $O(t^{J-1})$-neighbourhood of the integers.  Open: how often
  $n^{\rm int}_{*,J}(Y)$ falls into that neighbourhood as $Y$ varies, for
  instance for $Y=\log10^m$.
\item \textbf{The finite-size residuals at $\alpha=3$.}  Source: source
  100-89, Section~7.1 (``does not identify their asymptotic form'').
  Re-scoped: the numerical decomposition in the batch-100 note of
  Section~\ref{dpn:jh:sec:numerics} attributes the residual at $n=5000$ to the
  Fourier cells around $\pm2\pi/M$.  Open: a \emph{frozen-exact satellite
  formula at fixed $\alpha$}, an analogue of Lemma~\ref{dpn:ct:lem:local} in
  which the satellite phase and damping use the exact Bernoulli
  probabilities of the lower bulk, which is not frozen at fixed $\alpha$, with
  a proof that it gives the cell contributions to relative error $o(1)$.
  Missing: everything beyond Theorem~\ref{dpn:thm:resonance}, which gives the
  modulus at one point.
\item \textbf{Growing $\alpha$.}  Source: source 100-89, Section~7.1.
  Answered in the critical window $\alpha\asymp M^{1/3}/\log M$ by
  Section~\ref{dpn:ct:sec:theta}; the adjacent regimes are its questions
  100-93.4 and 100-93.5.
\end{enumerate}

%% ---------------------------------------------------------------------
\section{The critical theta window for growing powers}\label{dpn:ct:sec:theta}
This section is source 100-93 (Section~\ref{dpn:rb:sec:b100}), translated:
its $a$, $K$, $b$, $W$, $\lambda$, $L$, $R$, $\mu$, $U$, $C$, $V$, $Q$, $T_r$
and $z$ are written $\alpha$, $M$, $s$, $H$, $\Lambda$, $F(t)$, $R_{\ge2}$,
$\mu_R$, $B_{RR}$, $B_{RS}$, $B$, $Q_\perp$, $\mathsf T_r$ and $K$ (fourth
table of Section~\ref{dpn:sec:notation}).  Every other section of this report
keeps $\alpha$ fixed; here $\alpha$ grows with $n$.  Source 100-93's own scope
paragraph is kept: it is ``an unrefereed mathematical research note, not a
formal proof-assistant development''; its critical-window theorem is
self-contained; source 100-89 is ``its companion and background dependency'',
but ``None of those compact-$a$ error estimates is extended to growing $a$
here'', and ``The present results have a different uniformity domain and a
weaker inverse-error conclusion''; its numerical diagnostics are illustrations
independent of the proof; and ``The source comparison is bounded and makes no
exhaustive priority claim''.  Its pinned snapshot \texttt{6591a56b3} contains
neither this report nor the lattice-peak report of
Section~\ref{dpn:ct:sec:transfer}.

\subsection{The critical relative equivalent}\label{dpn:ct:sec:main}
For real $\alpha>0$ and $t>0$ let $X_k$, $p_k$ and $S=\sum_kkX_k$ be as in
\eqref{dpn:eq:pk}, and let
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:ct:eq:R}
 R_{\ge2}=\sum_{k\ge2}X_k
\end{equation}
be the number of selected parts of size at least two.  The saddle $t=t_n$ is
defined exactly by $\E S=n$, for every real $n>0$.  Put
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:ct:eq:quantities}
\begin{gathered}
 \mu_R=\E R_{\ge2},\qquad B_{RR}=\Var R_{\ge2},\qquad
 B_{RS}=\operatorname{Cov}(R_{\ge2},S),\\
 B=\Var S,\qquad Q_\perp=B_{RR}-\frac{B_{RS}^2}{B}.
\end{gathered}
\end{equation}
Thus $Q_\perp$ is the variance of $R_{\ge2}$ after linear regression on $S$.
With $M$ the larger root of $M^\alpha e^{-tM}=1$ (the transition site of
\eqref{dpn:eq:phase}), $s=\log M$ and $H=M/(\alpha(s-1))$ as there, set
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:ct:eq:scales}
 \Lambda=\frac{\alpha(s-1)}{M^{1/3}},\qquad\text{so}\qquad
 H=\frac{M^{2/3}}{\Lambda},\quad
 \alpha=\frac{\Lambda M^{1/3}}{s-1},\quad
 t=\frac{\Lambda s}{s-1}M^{-2/3}.
\end{equation}
The \emph{critical regime} is: $M\to\infty$ with $\Lambda$ in an arbitrary
fixed compact interval $\mathfrak J\subset(0,\infty)$; all constants and
limits below are uniform on $\mathfrak J$ unless stated otherwise.  In it
$\alpha\to\infty$ and $\alpha/\log M\to\infty$.  Define the positive
$1$-periodic function
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:ct:eq:Theta}
 \Theta(\mu,Q)=\sum_{j\in\mathbb Z}\exp(-2\pi^2Qj^2-2\pi\ii j\mu)
 =\frac1{\sqrt{2\pi Q}}\sum_{l\in\mathbb Z}
   \exp\Bigl(-\frac{(l-\mu)^2}{2Q}\Bigr),\qquad Q>0,
\end{equation}
the equality being Poisson summation for a Gaussian.

\begin{theorem}[Critical theta equivalent; source 100-93]\label{dpn:ct:thm:theta}
For positive integers $n$ in the critical regime,
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:ct:eq:main}
 a_\alpha(n)=\frac{\exp(F(t)+nt)}{\sqrt{2\pi B}}
          \bigl\{\Theta(\mu_R,Q_\perp)+o(1)\bigr\},
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:ct:eq:limits}
 n\sim\frac{M^2}{2},\qquad B\sim M^2H,\qquad
 Q_\perp=\frac{\pi^2}{3\Lambda^3}+o(1).
\end{equation}
$\Theta(\mu_R,Q_\perp)$ is bounded above and away from zero, uniformly in the
phase $\mu_R$, so \eqref{dpn:ct:eq:main} is a uniform relative equivalent.
\end{theorem}
The exact phase $\mu_R$ cannot in general be replaced by a coarse leading
approximation.  In particular $R_{\ge2}$ excludes $X_1$: although the lower
bulk becomes frozen, $p_1=1/(1+e^t)\to\frac12$, and
Section~\ref{dpn:ct:sec:partone} explains why including it in a Gaussian count
gives a wrong variance and phase \src{100-93}.

\begin{corollary}[A direct criterion; source 100-93]\label{dpn:ct:cor:direct}
Let $\alpha=\alpha(n)>0$.  If
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:ct:eq:direct}
 \frac{\alpha\log\sqrt{2n}}{(2n)^{1/6}}\longrightarrow\Lambda_\infty\in(0,\infty),
\end{equation}
then the exact saddle lies in the critical regime, $M/\sqrt{2n}\to1$ and
$\Lambda\to\Lambda_\infty$, so Theorem~\ref{dpn:ct:thm:theta} applies.
\end{corollary}

\subsection{Uniform edge estimates}\label{dpn:ct:sec:edge}
Let $\varpi(x)=\alpha\log x-tx$ be the log odds, as in
Section~\ref{dpn:rb:sec:boundary}, and $v_k=p_k(1-p_k)$.  At the upper root,
$\varpi(M)=0$ and $\varpi'(M)=-1/H$, and uniformly on bounded $u$-intervals
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:ct:eq:edgeprofile}
 \varpi(M+Hu)=-u+O\Bigl(\frac{M^{-1/3}u^2}{\log M}\Bigr).
\end{equation}
For large $M$, $\varpi'\le-1/(2H)$ on $[M/2,M]$ and $\varpi'\le-1/H$ on
$[M,\infty)$, so both $v_k$ and the defect probability $\min(p_k,1-p_k)$ are
at most $\exp(-|k-M|/(2H))$ on $[M/2,\infty)$.  On $[2,M/2]$ concavity bounds
$\varpi$ below by its smaller endpoint value; $\varpi(2)=\alpha(\log2-2\log M/M)$
and $\varpi(M/2)=\alpha(\frac{\log M}2-\log2)$, so $\varpi\ge c_{\mathfrak J}\alpha$
there, with $c_{\mathfrak J}>0$.  Since $\alpha/\log M\to\infty$, every
$M^{A_0}e^{-c_{\mathfrak J}\alpha}$ with fixed $A_0$ tends to zero.  This
controls the frozen lower bulk without a small-phase approximation
\src{100-93}.

\begin{lemma}[Edge moments; source 100-93]\label{dpn:ct:lem:moments}
For every fixed integer $r\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:ct:eq:moments}
 \mathsf T_r:=\sum_{k\ge2}(k-M)^rv_k
 =H^{r+1}\left\{\int_\R u^r\frac{e^u}{(1+e^u)^2}\dd u+o(1)\right\},
\end{equation}
and $\sum_{k\ge2}|k-M|^rv_k=O_r(H^{r+1})$.  In particular $B_{RR}=\mathsf T_0\sim H$,
$\mathsf T_1=o(H^2)$ and $\mathsf T_2\sim\frac{\pi^2}3H^3$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}[Proof \src{100-93}]
On bounded $u$-intervals, \eqref{dpn:ct:eq:edgeprofile} and the mesh
$1/H\to0$ give uniform Riemann-sum convergence; the exponential edge bound
gives a uniform integrable majorant for every absolute moment; the far lower
interval contributes at most $M^{r+1}e^{-c_{\mathfrak J}\alpha}$.  The limit
density is the standard logistic density, whose bilateral exponential moment
is $\pi z/\sin(\pi z)$ for $|z|<1$, with second derivative $\pi^2/3$ at $0$.
\end{proof}
Compare Lemma~\ref{dpn:lem:scales} and \eqref{dpn:eq:profilemoment}, the same
logistic profile at fixed $\alpha$, where the lower bulk is not frozen.

Put $v_1=p_1(1-p_1)$.  Directly from the definitions,
\begin{align}
 B_{RS}&=MB_{RR}+\mathsf T_1,\qquad
 B=M^2B_{RR}+2M\mathsf T_1+\mathsf T_2+v_1,\label{dpn:ct:eq:CV}\\
 Q_\perp&=\frac{B_{RR}\mathsf T_2-\mathsf T_1^2+B_{RR}v_1}{B}.\label{dpn:ct:eq:Qexact}
\end{align}
Consequently $B\sim M^2H$ and
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:ct:eq:Qlimit}
 Q_\perp=\frac{\pi^2H^3}{3M^2}+o(1)=\frac{\pi^2}{3\Lambda^3}+o(1);
\end{equation}
the contribution of part one to $Q_\perp$ is $O(M^{-2})$.  The leading terms
$M^2B_{RR}^2$ of $B_{RR}B$ and of $B_{RS}^2$ cancel in \eqref{dpn:ct:eq:Qexact}:
conditioning on the size removes the variance $B_{RR}\sim H$ of the count down
to order $H^3/M^2$.  The same defect estimates compare $\E S$ with
$\sum_{k\le M}k$, with difference
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:ct:eq:meanerror}
 O(MH+H^2)+O(M^2e^{-c_{\mathfrak J}\alpha})=O(MH),
\end{equation}
so $n=M^2/2+O(MH)$, the mean estimate in \eqref{dpn:ct:eq:limits}.  The exact
regression-centre identity
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:ct:eq:betadiff}
 \frac{B_{RS}}B-\frac1M=-\frac{M\mathsf T_1+\mathsf T_2+v_1}{MB}
\end{equation}
implies
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:ct:eq:centeraccuracy}
 \frac{B_{RS}}B=\frac1M+o\Bigl(\frac H{M^2}\Bigr),\qquad
 \sqrt B\Bigl(\frac{B_{RS}}B-\frac1M\Bigr)=o(1),
\end{equation}
because the three terms $-\mathsf T_1/\sqrt B$, $-\mathsf T_2/(M\sqrt B)$ and
$-v_1/(M\sqrt B)$ tend to zero: $H^{3/2}/M$ is bounded and
$H^{5/2}/M^2=O(M^{-1/3})$ \src{100-93}.

\begin{proof}[Proof of Corollary~\ref{dpn:ct:cor:direct} \src{100-93}]
Fix $0<\varepsilon<1$ and put $M_\pm=(1\pm\varepsilon)\sqrt{2n}$, with trial
saddles $t_\pm=\alpha\log M_\pm/M_\pm$.  Their critical parameters tend to
$\Lambda_\infty(1\pm\varepsilon)^{-1/3}$, so the uniform mean estimate applies at
both, and their means lie above and below $n$ for large $n$.  On $M>e$,
$\alpha\log M/M$ decreases in $M$ while the mean decreases in $t$, so the
exact root lies between $M_-$ and $M_+$.  Letting $\varepsilon\downarrow0$
gives $M/\sqrt{2n}\to1$ and then $\Lambda\to\Lambda_\infty$.  This uses the edge
mean estimate rather than presupposing the location of the saddle.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The local Fourier satellites}\label{dpn:ct:sec:satellites}
Let $\phi(\theta)=\E\exp(\ii\theta(S-n))$; at the saddle this is $\phi_t$ of
Section~\ref{dpn:sec:llt}.  For every integer $j$, since $R_{\ge2}$ is
integer valued,
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:ct:eq:phaseidentity}
 \phi(\theta)=e^{-2\pi\ii j\mu_R}\,
   \E\exp\bigl(\ii[\theta(S-n)-2\pi j(R_{\ge2}-\mu_R)]\bigr),
\end{equation}
with no further $n$-dependent phase because the saddle mean is exactly $n$.
Set
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:ct:eq:satellitecenter}
 \theta_j=2\pi j\,\frac{B_{RS}}B.
\end{equation}
The variance inside the expectation in \eqref{dpn:ct:eq:phaseidentity} is
exactly
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:ct:eq:variancecomplete}
 B(\theta-\theta_j)^2+4\pi^2j^2Q_\perp.
\end{equation}

\begin{lemma}[Satellites; source 100-93]\label{dpn:ct:lem:local}
For fixed $j\in\mathbb Z$ and $y\in\R$,
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:ct:eq:locallimit}
 \phi\Bigl(\theta_j+\frac y{\sqrt B}\Bigr)
 =\exp\bigl(-2\pi\ii j\mu_R-2\pi^2j^2Q_\perp-y^2/2\bigr)\{1+o(1)\},
\end{equation}
uniformly on bounded sets of $(j,y)$ and on $\mathfrak J$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}[Proof \src{100-93}]
At $\theta=\theta_j+y/\sqrt B$ the centred Bernoulli phases in
\eqref{dpn:ct:eq:phaseidentity} are $\omega_1=\theta$ and
$\omega_k=\theta k-2\pi j$ for $k\ge2$.  By \eqref{dpn:ct:eq:centeraccuracy},
$\theta=O(1/M)$ and
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:ct:eq:alphabound}
 |\omega_k|\le C\Bigl(\frac{|k-M|}M+\frac HM+H^{-1/2}\Bigr),
\end{equation}
so the absolute edge moments give
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:ct:eq:cubic}
 \sum_{k\ge2}v_k|\omega_k|^3=O(H^4/M^3+H^{-1/2})=O(M^{-1/3}).
\end{equation}
This weighted estimate alone does not permit a small-angle logarithm on the
whole tail.  Fix a large constant $C_1$.  In the active band
$|k-M|\le C_1H\log M$ the largest $|\omega_k|$ tends to zero uniformly, and for
a Bernoulli variable with parameter $p$ and small real $\omega$ the branch of
the logarithm continuous from zero satisfies
$\log\E e^{\ii\omega(X-p)}=-\frac12p(1-p)\omega^2+O(p(1-p)|\omega|^3)$, uniformly in
$0\le p\le1$; summing over the band has error $o(1)$ by \eqref{dpn:ct:eq:cubic}.
For a frozen lower-tail variable, $p=1-\epsilon$, the exact factor is
$e^{\ii\epsilon\omega}\{1+\epsilon(e^{-\ii\omega}-1)\}$, and for a frozen upper-tail
variable, $p=\epsilon$, it is $e^{-\ii\epsilon\omega}\{1+\epsilon(e^{\ii\omega}-1)\}$;
in both cases
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:ct:eq:frozenbound}
 \bigl|\E e^{\ii\omega(X-p)}-1\bigr|\le\epsilon(|\omega|+2).
\end{equation}
For every prescribed $A>0$, a large $C_1$ makes the sum of the right side
outside the band $O(M^{-A})$: between $M/2$ and $M-C_1H\log M$ use
$e^{-(M-k)/(2H)}$, below $M/2$ use $e^{-c_{\mathfrak J}\alpha}$, above
$M+C_1H\log M$ use $e^{-(k-M)/H}$; the linear weight in
\eqref{dpn:ct:eq:alphabound}, or the coarser $O(1+k/M)$, changes only
polynomial factors.  The same bounds with $\omega_k^2$ show that the discarded
tail variance is $o(1)$.  Every characteristic factor has modulus at most
one, so a telescoping product bound and \eqref{dpn:ct:eq:frozenbound} make
the whole frozen-tail product $1+o(1)$, without a logarithm across a large
phase.  Part one has $\omega_1=O(1/M)$ and contributes $1+o(1)$; its variance
may also be kept in the exact quadratic expression.  Now
\eqref{dpn:ct:eq:variancecomplete} gives \eqref{dpn:ct:eq:locallimit}.
\end{proof}

\subsubsection{The exceptional part of size one}\label{dpn:ct:sec:partone}
If $R_{\ge2}$ were replaced by $R_{\ge2}+X_1$, the part-one satellite phase
would be $\theta-2\pi j$, not small for fixed $j\ne0$.  Its exact centred
characteristic factor tends to $\exp(2\pi\ii jp_1)$, which cancels the added
mean phase; it has no Gaussian attenuation.  A Gaussian replacement would
instead introduce a spurious limiting conditional variance $\frac14$ and a
half-unit mean shift.  Excluding $X_1$ from $R_{\ge2}$ while keeping it in $S$
and $B$ is therefore necessary for the satellite calculation \src{100-93}.

\subsection{Uniform control of the Fourier complement}\label{dpn:ct:sec:complement}
Choose the odd consecutive block $\mathsf B=\{k_{\mathsf B}-m_{\mathsf B},\dots,
k_{\mathsf B}+m_{\mathsf B}\}$, with $k_{\mathsf B}$ an integer nearest $M$,
$m_{\mathsf B}=\lfloor H/2\rfloor$ and $N_{\mathsf B}=2m_{\mathsf B}+1$.  Then
$N_{\mathsf B}/H\to1$, $|k_{\mathsf B}-M|\le\frac12$, and all $v_k$ on
$\mathsf B$ are bounded below by a positive constant.

\begin{lemma}[A two-scale block inequality; source 100-93]\label{dpn:ct:lem:block}
For all sufficiently large critical parameters, with an absolute $c'>0$,
\begin{align}
 \sum_{k\in\mathsf B}\sin^2(k\theta/2)
 &\ge c'\bigl\{H\operatorname{dist}(M\theta,2\pi\mathbb Z)^2+H^3\theta^2\bigr\},
 &&|\theta|\le\frac1{2H},\label{dpn:ct:eq:blocksmall}\\
 \sum_{k\in\mathsf B}\sin^2(k\theta/2)&\ge c'H,
 &&\frac1{2H}\le|\theta|\le\pi.\label{dpn:ct:eq:blocklarge}
\end{align}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}[Proof \src{100-93}]
With the normalized Dirichlet kernel
$D_N(\theta)=\sin(N\theta/2)/(N\sin(\theta/2))$ (equal to $1$ at $0$),
symmetry gives
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:ct:eq:dirichlet}
 \sum_{k\in\mathsf B}\sin^2(k\theta/2)
 =\frac{N_{\mathsf B}}2\bigl\{1-D_{N_{\mathsf B}}(\theta)\cos(k_{\mathsf B}\theta)\bigr\}.
\end{equation}
For $|\theta|\le1/(2H)$, $D_{N_{\mathsf B}}$ is bounded below by a positive
constant and $1-D_{N_{\mathsf B}}(\theta)=N_{\mathsf B}^{-1}\sum_{|i|\le m_{\mathsf B}}
(1-\cos(i\theta))\ge c''N_{\mathsf B}^2\theta^2$.  Since
$1-\cos(k_{\mathsf B}\theta)\ge c''\operatorname{dist}(k_{\mathsf B}\theta,2\pi\mathbb Z)^2$,
the decomposition $1-D\cos=(1-D)+D(1-\cos)$ gives the bound with centre
$k_{\mathsf B}$; the distance function is Lipschitz, and
$\operatorname{dist}(M\theta,2\pi\mathbb Z)^2\le2\operatorname{dist}(k_{\mathsf B}\theta,2\pi\mathbb Z)^2+\theta^2/2$,
whose last term is absorbed by $N_{\mathsf B}^3\theta^2$.  For
\eqref{dpn:ct:eq:blocklarge} take $A=4\pi$.  If $|\theta|\ge A/N_{\mathsf B}$,
$\sin(|\theta|/2)\ge|\theta|/\pi$ gives $|D_{N_{\mathsf B}}|\le\frac14$.  On the
remaining interval $x=N_{\mathsf B}|\theta|/2$ ranges over a compact subset of
$(0,2\pi]$ bounded away from zero, where
$|D_{N_{\mathsf B}}(\theta)|=|\sin x/x|\{1+O(N_{\mathsf B}^{-2})\}$ and
$\sup|\sin x/x|<1$.  Hence $|D_{N_{\mathsf B}}|\le1-\eta'$ for a fixed
$\eta'>0$, and \eqref{dpn:ct:eq:dirichlet} proves \eqref{dpn:ct:eq:blocklarge}.
This includes the transition just above a small multiple of $1/H$, where a
crude geometric-sum estimate alone is insufficient.
\end{proof}
\mergenoted{Lemma~\ref{dpn:lem:block} gives the second bound and the
term $H^3\theta^2$ of the first.  The new term $H\operatorname{dist}(M\theta,
2\pi\mathbb Z)^2$ of \eqref{dpn:ct:eq:blocksmall} is what resolves the cells
around the resonances $2\pi j/M$; it is the block-level form of the action
in Theorem~\ref{dpn:thm:resonance}.}

A Bernoulli factor satisfies
$|1-p_k+p_ke^{\ii k\theta}|=\sqrt{1-4v_k\sin^2(k\theta/2)}\le\exp(-2v_k\sin^2(k\theta/2))$.
Dropping every factor outside $\mathsf B$, Lemma~\ref{dpn:ct:lem:block} gives
\begin{align}
 |\phi(\theta)|&\le\exp\bigl\{-c_{\mathfrak J}\bigl[H\operatorname{dist}(M\theta,2\pi\mathbb Z)^2
   +H^3\theta^2\bigr]\bigr\},&&|\theta|\le1/(2H),\label{dpn:ct:eq:phismall}\\
 |\phi(\theta)|&\le e^{-c_{\mathfrak J}H},&&1/(2H)\le|\theta|\le\pi.\label{dpn:ct:eq:philarge}
\end{align}

\begin{lemma}[Cell bound; source 100-93]\label{dpn:ct:lem:integrated}
Let $\mathcal I_j=\{\theta:|M\theta-2\pi j|\le\pi\}$.  Uniformly in all
integers $j$,
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:ct:eq:cellbound}
 \int_{\mathcal I_j\cap\{|\theta|\le1/(2H)\}}|\phi(\theta)|\dd\theta
 \le\frac{C_{\mathfrak J}}{M\sqrt H}\,e^{-c_{\mathfrak J}j^2}.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}[Proof \src{100-93}]
Write $M\theta=2\pi j+x$, $|x|\le\pi$.  The exponent in \eqref{dpn:ct:eq:phismall}
is $Hx^2+\Lambda^{-3}(2\pi j+x)^2$, because $H^3/M^2=\Lambda^{-3}$.  Since
$\Lambda^{-3}$ stays bounded above and below, for large $M$,
$Hx^2+\Lambda^{-3}(2\pi j+x)^2\ge(H/2)x^2+2\pi^2\Lambda^{-3}j^2$, by
$(u+v)^2\ge u^2/2-v^2$ and $H\ge2\Lambda^{-3}$.  Integrating the Gaussian in $x$
with $\dd\theta=\dd x/M$ gives the claim, also for truncated cells.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{dpn:ct:thm:theta} \src{100-93}]
For fixed $j$, \eqref{dpn:ct:eq:centeraccuracy} gives
$\sqrt B(\theta_j-2\pi j/M)=o(1)$.  In the coordinate $y=\sqrt B(\theta-\theta_j)$
the cell endpoints tend to $\mp\infty$, and \eqref{dpn:ct:eq:phismall} gives an
integrable majorant $C_je^{-c_{\mathfrak J}y^2}$.  Lemma~\ref{dpn:ct:lem:local}
and dominated convergence give
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:ct:eq:cellasymptotic}
 \int_{\mathcal I_j}\phi(\theta)\dd\theta
 =\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}B}\,e^{-2\pi\ii j\mu_R-2\pi^2j^2Q_\perp}+o(B^{-1/2})
\end{equation}
for every fixed $j$, uniformly in the parameters, with no assumption that
$\mu_R$ converges: its exact unit-modulus phase is kept.  The outer range
contributes $O(e^{-c_{\mathfrak J}H})=o(B^{-1/2})$, and
Lemma~\ref{dpn:ct:lem:integrated} bounds the cells with $|j|>j_0$ by
$C_{\mathfrak J}B^{-1/2}\sum_{|j|>j_0}e^{-c_{\mathfrak J}j^2}$.  Summing
\eqref{dpn:ct:eq:cellasymptotic} over finitely many $j$ and then letting
$j_0\to\infty$, Fourier inversion at the integer $n$ gives
$\Prob_t(S=n)=(2\pi B)^{-1/2}\{\Theta(\mu_R,Q_\perp)+o(1)\}$, and
\eqref{dpn:eq:tiltidentity} is exact.  Finally, the Fourier series in
\eqref{dpn:ct:eq:Theta} is uniformly bounded on compact $Q$-intervals, and the
Gaussian series with $l$ an integer nearest $\mu$ gives
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:ct:eq:thetalower}
 \Theta(\mu,Q)\ge\frac1{\sqrt{2\pi Q}}\exp\Bigl(-\frac1{8Q}\Bigr),
\end{equation}
positive on compact $Q$-intervals, which turns the additive error into a
uniform relative one.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Integer powers obstruct a uniform single Gaussian}\label{dpn:ct:sec:obstruction}
Let $G_\alpha(n)=\exp(F(t)+nt)/\sqrt{2\pi B}$, the single Gaussian $G$ of
Section~\ref{dpn:sec:checks} with exact saddle and exact variance, so no
continuum approximation enters the comparison.

\begin{corollary}[Two limits of the single-Gaussian ratio; source 100-93]\label{dpn:ct:cor:obstruction}
For every fixed $\Lambda_0>0$ and each sufficiently large integer power
$\alpha=p$ there are integer indices $n^{(0)}_p$ and $n^{(1/2)}_p$ with
$\Lambda\to\Lambda_0$ along which
\begin{align}
 \frac{a_p(n^{(0)}_p)}{G_p(n^{(0)}_p)}&\longrightarrow
 \Theta\Bigl(0,\frac{\pi^2}{3\Lambda_0^3}\Bigr)>1,\label{dpn:ct:eq:phasezero}\\
 \frac{a_p(n^{(1/2)}_p)}{G_p(n^{(1/2)}_p)}&\longrightarrow
 \Theta\Bigl(\frac12,\frac{\pi^2}{3\Lambda_0^3}\Bigr)<1.\label{dpn:ct:eq:phasehalf}
\end{align}
Thus no single-Gaussian relative equivalent holds uniformly over the
integer-power critical array.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}[Proof \src{100-93}]
For each large integer $p$ let $M_p>e^4$ be the unique root of
$p(\log M_p-1)/M_p^{1/3}=\Lambda_0$ on that branch (unique by differentiation;
it exists because the maximum at $M=e^4$ grows linearly with $p$), and let
$n^0_p$ be the corresponding real mean.  At fixed $\alpha=p$, exactly,
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:ct:eq:phasederivatives}
 \frac{\dd\mu_R}{\dd n}=\frac{B_{RS}}B\sim\frac1{M_p},\qquad
 \frac{\dd n}{\dd M}=\frac B{MH}\sim M_p.
\end{equation}
On an $n$-interval of length $DM_p$ with fixed $D>2$, $M$ changes by $O(1)$ and
$\Lambda=\Lambda_0+o(1)$ uniformly (first on a bounded $M$-interval by the
second identity, then on its image), and by the first identity $\mu_R$
traverses $D+o(1)$ units.  So the interval contains real indices where $\mu_R$
is an integer and where it is a half-integer; rounding them to integers moves
$\mu_R$ by $O(1/M_p)$ and keeps $\Lambda\to\Lambda_0$.
Theorem~\ref{dpn:ct:thm:theta} gives the limits.  At phase $0$ the nonconstant
Fourier terms are positive.  At phase $\frac12$, pairing terms gives
$\Theta(\frac12,Q)-1=2\sum_{m\ge1}\{-e^{-2\pi^2Q(2m-1)^2}+e^{-2\pi^2Q(2m)^2}\}<0$,
and $\Theta(\frac12,Q)>0$ by the Gaussian form.
\end{proof}
This is a qualitative nonuniformity statement.  It does not contradict the
compact-fixed-power expansions (Theorem~\ref{dpn:thm:saddle},
Section~\ref{dpn:rb:sec:relative}), in which $\Lambda\to0$, and it supplies no
effective power or index at which either limit becomes numerically accurate
\src{100-93}.

\subsection{Theta-adjusted inversion}\label{dpn:ct:sec:inverse}
For fixed $\alpha>0$, continue the exact saddle quantities to real $x>0$ (the
saddle $t(x)$ of Section~\ref{dpn:jh:sec:threshold}) and define
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:ct:eq:PsiTheta}
 \Psi_\Theta(x)=F(t(x))+xt(x)-\tfrac12\log(2\pi B)+\log\Theta(\mu_R,Q_\perp)
 =\Psi_0(x)+\log\Theta(\mu_R,Q_\perp),
\end{equation}
that is, the Gaussian inverse $\Psi_0$ of \eqref{dpn:jh:eq:psi0} plus the
logarithm of the theta factor.  The quantifiers are joint: $\alpha$ is fixed
for differentiation or inversion and then tends to infinity through critical
locations.  Nothing here is a fixed-$\alpha$, $n\to\infty$ statement
\src{100-93}.

\begin{proposition}[Derivative control; source 100-93]\label{dpn:ct:prop:deriv}
Uniformly on $\mathfrak J$,
$\Psi_\Theta'(x)=t(x)+O(M^{-1})$ and $\Psi_\Theta'(x)/t(x)=1+O(M^{-1/3})$.  In
particular the real interpolation is eventually strictly increasing on each
such large-$M$ interval.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}[Proof \src{100-93}]
All sums are locally differentiable in $t$ by exponential domination.  With
$\rho_{RS}=B_{RS}/B$, $r_1=0$ and $r_k=1$ for $k\ge2$, exponential tilting gives
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:ct:eq:derivs}
 \frac{\dd x}{\dd t}=-B,\quad \frac{\dd t}{\dd x}=-\frac1B,\quad
 \frac{\dd\mu_R}{\dd x}=\rho_{RS},\quad
 \frac{\dd B}{\dd t}=-\sum_{k\ge1}k^3v_k(1-2p_k).
\end{equation}
Since $Q_\perp=\Var(R_{\ge2}-\rho_{RS}S)$ and $\operatorname{Cov}(R_{\ge2}-\rho_{RS}S,S)=0$,
the derivative of $\rho_{RS}$ cancels at the point of differentiation, and
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:ct:eq:Qt}
 \frac{\dd Q_\perp}{\dd t}=-\sum_{k\ge1}(r_k-\rho_{RS}k)^2kv_k(1-2p_k),
\end{equation}
which can also be checked by differentiating $B_{RR}-B_{RS}^2/B$.  The edge
moments give $\sum k^3v_k=O(M^3H)$, so $|(\log B)'(x)|=O((MH)^{-1})$.  By
\eqref{dpn:ct:eq:betadiff}, $|1-\rho_{RS}M|=O(H/M)$; with $d=k-M$,
$(1-\rho_{RS}k)^2\le C[(H/M)^2+(d/M)^2]$, so
$\sum_{k\ge2}k(1-\rho_{RS}k)^2v_k\le C[\frac{H^2}{M^2}(MH+H^2)+\frac1{M^2}(MH^3+H^4)]
=O(H^3/M)=O(M)$.  Part one contributes $O(t/M^2)$ to the absolute
$Q_\perp$-sum and $O(t)$ to the absolute $B$-sum; the range $2\le k\le M/2$
contributes $O(M^2e^{-c_{\mathfrak J}\alpha})$ and $O(M^4e^{-c_{\mathfrak J}\alpha})$.
Hence $|\dd Q_\perp/\dd t|=O(M)$ and $|\dd Q_\perp/\dd x|=O((MH)^{-1})=O(M^{-5/3})$.
The theta series and its first derivatives in $\mu$ and $Q$ converge
uniformly on compact positive $Q$-intervals, and \eqref{dpn:ct:eq:thetalower}
bounds the derivatives of $\log\Theta$.  Also $(F(t(x))+xt(x))'=t(x)$ exactly.
Since $\dd\mu_R/\dd x\sim1/M$, these bounds give the first assertion, and
$t\sim1/H$ gives the second.  No derivative of the unspecified forward $o(1)$
error is taken.
\end{proof}

At fixed $\alpha$,
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:ct:eq:windowderivs}
 \frac{\dd t}{\dd M}=-\frac1{MH},\qquad
 \frac{\dd x}{\dd M}=\frac B{MH}\sim M,\qquad
 \frac{\dd\log\Lambda}{\dd M}=\frac{1/(\log M-1)-1/3}M,
\end{equation}
so if $x_0$ has saddle parameters $M_0,t_0$, then for $|h|=o(M_0^{2/3})$
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:ct:eq:windowM}
 M(x_0+h)-M_0=O(|h|/M_0)=o(M_0^{-1/3}),\qquad
 \frac{\Lambda(x_0+h)}{\Lambda(x_0)},\ \frac{t(x_0+h)}{t_0}=1+O(|h|/M_0^2).
\end{equation}
These hold first on a slightly enlarged compact $\Lambda$-interval, and by
continuity on bounded multiples of $1/t_0\asymp M_0^{2/3}$; hence
$\Psi_\Theta'=t_0(1+o(1))$ there.  The coefficients increase strictly for
$n\ge1$ by the injection of Section~\ref{dpn:sec:inverse} (source 100-93's
Lemma~6.2, also for nonintegral $\alpha$).

\begin{theorem}[Theta-adjusted inverse; source 100-93]\label{dpn:ct:thm:inverse}
Suppose the real critical-window solution $n_\Theta(Y)$ of
$\Psi_\Theta(n_\Theta)=Y$ has $M_0\to\infty$ and $\Lambda_0$ in a fixed compact
positive interval, and let $N_\alpha(Y)$ be as in Theorem~\ref{dpn:thm:inverse}.
Then
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:ct:eq:inverseerror}
 N_\alpha(Y)-n_\Theta(Y)=o(1/t_0)=o(M_0^{2/3}).
\end{equation}
More precisely, there is $\varrho\to\infty$ with $\varrho=o(1/t_0)$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:ct:eq:bracket}
 \log a_\alpha(\lfloor n_\Theta-\varrho\rfloor)<Y<\log a_\alpha(\lceil n_\Theta+\varrho\rceil),
\end{equation}
so $\lfloor n_\Theta-\varrho\rfloor<N_\alpha(Y)\le\lceil n_\Theta+\varrho\rceil$.  At
sequence values,
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:ct:eq:exactrange}
 \Psi_\Theta^{-1}\bigl(\log a_\alpha(n)\bigr)=n+o\bigl(1/t(n)\bigr).
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}[Proof \src{100-93}]
Theorem~\ref{dpn:ct:thm:theta} and positivity give
$\log a_\alpha(m)=\Psi_\Theta(m)+o(1)$ uniformly at critical integers; let
$e_{\max}\to0$ be the supremum of this error on a slightly enlarged compact
interval, put $\varsigma=\sqrt{e_{\max}+t_0}$ and $\varrho=\varsigma/t_0$, so
$\varsigma\to0$, $e_{\max}=o(\varsigma)$, $t_0=o(\varsigma)$, $\varrho\to\infty$ and
$\varrho=o(1/t_0)$.  By Proposition~\ref{dpn:ct:prop:deriv} and
\eqref{dpn:ct:eq:windowM},
$\Psi_\Theta(\lfloor n_\Theta-\varrho\rfloor)=Y-\varsigma(1+o(1))$ and
$\Psi_\Theta(\lceil n_\Theta+\varrho\rceil)=Y+\varsigma(1+o(1))$; rounding costs
$O(t_0)=o(\varsigma)$ and the forward error is $o(\varsigma)$, which proves
\eqref{dpn:ct:eq:bracket}.  Strict monotonicity gives the rest; at a sequence
value the threshold is the index itself.
\end{proof}
The brackets are asymptotic, not certified at any finite parameter.  If a
certified forward error bound $e$ and a slope lower bound $m_\bullet>0$ were
available on a neighbourhood, endpoints at distance $d$ with $m_\bullet d>e$
would give certified inequalities by the same argument; no such bound is
supplied.  The allowed discrepancy $o(M^{2/3})$ may diverge, so
nearest-integer recovery is not justified in this regime \src{100-93}.

\begin{remark}[The theta shift of the threshold; \wrt]\label{dpn:ct:rem:shift}
Let $n_*(Y)$ be the Gaussian inverse of Section~\ref{dpn:jh:sec:threshold},
$\Psi_0(n_*)=Y$; in the critical regime $\Psi_0'=t-\kappa_3/(2B^2)=t(1+o(1))$,
since $\kappa_3=O(M^3H)$ and $B^2\asymp M^4H^2$.  Then
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:ct:eq:shift}
 n_\Theta(Y)=n_*(Y)-\frac{\log\Theta(\mu_R,Q_\perp)}{t}+o\Bigl(\frac1t\Bigr),
\end{equation}
with $\mu_R,Q_\perp,t$ evaluated at either point.  Indeed
$\Psi_0(n_\Theta)-\Psi_0(n_*)=-\log\Theta$ at $n_\Theta$, which is $O(1)$; so
$|n_\Theta-n_*|=O(1/t)$, on which window \eqref{dpn:ct:eq:windowM} gives
$\Psi_0'=t(1+o(1))$, and $\mu_R$ moves by $O(1/(tM))=o(1)$ and $Q_\perp$ by
$o(1)$, so $\log\Theta$ changes by $o(1)$.  With
Theorem~\ref{dpn:ct:thm:inverse}, the threshold $N_\alpha(Y)$ is displaced from
the single-Gaussian inverse by $-\log\Theta/t$, oscillating with the phase
$\mu_R$.  This displacement is of order at most $1/t\asymp M^{2/3}$, and of
exact order $1/t$ at phases bounded away from the zeros of
$\log\Theta(\cdot,Q_\perp)$.  These occur twice per phase period: with
$q=e^{-2\pi^2Q}$ one has $\Theta(\mu,Q)=\vartheta_3(\pi\mu,q)$, whose Jacobi
product $\prod_{m\ge1}(1-q^{2m})(1+2q^{2m-1}\cos2\pi\mu+q^{4m-2})$ is even
and strictly decreasing in $\mu\in[0,\frac12]$, and
$\Theta(0,Q)>1>\Theta(\frac12,Q)$, so $\log\Theta(\cdot,Q)$ vanishes exactly
at $\mu\equiv\pm\mu_0(Q)\pmod1$ for one $\mu_0(Q)\in(0,\frac12)$; there the
displacement is $o(1/t)$.  In particular it is of exact order $1/t$ near
$\mu_R\in\mathbb Z$ and near $\mu_R\in\mathbb Z+\frac12$.  (Dated note,
5~October 2026: the first version of this sentence read ``displaced from the
single-Gaussian inverse by $-\log\Theta/t$, of exact order $1/t\asymp M^{2/3}$
and oscillating with the phase $\mu_R$''; an independent check made by the
intake after the write found that wording overstated at the two zeros of
$\log\Theta$ per period, and the rest of the remark valid.)  By the proof of
Corollary~\ref{dpn:ct:cor:obstruction} the displacement is near
$-\log\Theta(0,\cdot)/t<0$ and near $-\log\Theta(\frac12,\cdot)/t>0$ on every
index interval of length a fixed multiple of $M$, so no constant correction of
order $1/t$ is valid.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[Leading logarithm; source 100-93]\label{dpn:ct:prop:leadinglog}
Uniformly in the critical regime, with $K=\sqrt{2n}$,
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:ct:eq:leadinglog}
 \log a_\alpha(n)=\alpha K(\log K-1)+O(M)\sim\alpha K(\log K-1),
\end{equation}
and the same holds for $\Psi_\Theta(n)$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}[Proof \src{100-93}]
Let $m=\lfloor M\rfloor$.  For large critical parameters $\varpi$ is nonnegative
on $2\le k\le m$ and negative for $k>M$.  Since
$0\le\log(1+e^\varpi)-\max(\varpi,0)\le e^{-|\varpi|}$, the edge and frozen
estimates give $F(t)=\alpha\log(m!)-t\{m(m+1)/2-1\}+O(H)$, the part-one term
being $O(1)$.  By \eqref{dpn:ct:eq:meanerror}, $n-m(m+1)/2=O(MH)$, while
$t=O(1/H)$, and Stirling's formula gives $F(t)+nt=\alpha M(\log M-1)+O(M)$.
The variance logarithm is $O(\log M)$ and $\log\Theta=O(1)$.  Also $K-M=O(H)$,
and the mean value theorem gives
$\alpha\{K(\log K-1)-M(\log M-1)\}=O(\alpha H\log M)=O(M)$.  The main term is of
order $M^{4/3}$, which gives the relative equivalence.
\end{proof}
Solving the leading equation $Y=\alpha K(\log K-1)$ gives exactly the Lambert
data of \eqref{dpn:eq:Wlead}: $K_0=Y/(\alpha W_0(Y/(\alpha e)))$ and
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:ct:eq:Lambert}
 n_0=\frac{K_0^2}2=\frac{Y^2}{2\alpha^2W_0(Y/(\alpha e))^2},
\end{equation}
with the principal branch $W_0$ (source 100-93 writes $z_L$, $n_L$; its $W$
is the width $H$).  For $Y=\log a_\alpha(n)$ with critical $n$,
Proposition~\ref{dpn:ct:prop:leadinglog} gives $n_0/n\to1$.  Conversely, for a
joint sequence $(\alpha,Y)$ with $K_0\to\infty$ and
$\Lambda_0=\alpha(R_0-1)/K_0^{1/3}$ in a fixed compact subset of $(0,\infty)$,
trial-root bracketing as in Corollary~\ref{dpn:ct:cor:direct} shows that the
exact saddle at $n_0$ has $M/K_0\to1$; the saddles at $(1\pm\varepsilon)n_0$ stay
in a slightly enlarged compact critical interval, and their leading
logarithms divided by $Y$ tend to $\sqrt{1\pm\varepsilon}$.  Letting
$\varepsilon\downarrow0$,
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:ct:eq:lambertconclusion}
 N_\alpha(Y)\sim\frac{Y^2}{2\alpha^2W_0(Y/(\alpha e))^2},
\end{equation}
and a nearby critical solution of $\Psi_\Theta(n)=Y$ exists, to which
Theorem~\ref{dpn:ct:thm:inverse} then applies.  Formula
\eqref{dpn:ct:eq:Lambert} by itself is only a leading relative inverse
\src{100-93}.
\mergenoted{This is the leading term of Theorem~\ref{dpn:thm:inverse}, now in
a joint limit in which $\alpha$ grows; Theorem~\ref{dpn:thm:inverse} and its
corrections are fixed-$\alpha$ statements and are not extended here.}

%% ---------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Relation to the fixed-power sections}\label{dpn:ct:sec:relation}
\paragraph{The satellites are the resonances of Section~\ref{dpn:sec:resonance}.}
The damping $2\pi^2Q_\perp j^2$ of the $j$th satellite is, by
\eqref{dpn:ct:eq:Qlimit},
$\frac{2\pi^4j^2}3\frac{H^3}{M^2}+o(1)$: exactly the action of
Theorem~\ref{dpn:thm:resonance} at $\ell=j$.  At fixed $\alpha$ that action is
of order $M/s^3\to\infty$, so the noncentral arcs are exponentially small; in
the critical window $H^3/M^2=\Lambda^{-3}$ is bounded and every satellite
survives.  Section~\ref{dpn:sec:resonance} says that its theorem ``does not
yet determine the shifted secondary saddles, their phases, or the
contribution of their full neighborhoods''.  In the critical window
Theorem~\ref{dpn:ct:thm:theta} determines all three: the centres
$\theta_j=2\pi jB_{RS}/B=2\pi j/M+o(B^{-1/2})$, the phases $-2\pi j\mu_R$, and the
full-cell contributions \eqref{dpn:ct:eq:cellasymptotic}, with relative error
$o(1)$ and no effective constant.

\paragraph{Questions of Section~\ref{dpn:sec:future}.}
Project~1 (an effective multi-saddle expansion) is answered at leading
order in the critical window only; at fixed $\alpha$ it stays open.
Project~4 (growing moments) gains one sharp range: for $\Lambda\asymp1$, that
is $\alpha\asymp M^{1/3}/\log M$, single-saddle dominance fails
(Corollary~\ref{dpn:ct:cor:obstruction}) and the theta law holds; whether
single-saddle dominance holds for every $\alpha=o(M^{1/3}/\log M)$, and the
localization near maximal-norm partitions as $\Lambda\to\infty$, stay open
(questions 100-93.4--5).  Project~6 (conditioned fluctuations of the number of
parts) is touched: $Q_\perp$ is the regression variance of the number of parts
at least two given the size, and \eqref{dpn:ct:eq:Qexact} exhibits the
cancellation that project anticipates ($B_{RR}\sim H$ against
$Q_\perp\sim\pi^2H^3/(3M^2)$); this is proved in the window only.  Dated notes at
those projects record this.

\paragraph{Not an explanation of the fixed-$\alpha$ finite-size errors.}
Source 100-93's theorem does not explain the residuals of
Section~\ref{dpn:sec:checks} at $\alpha=3,4,5$, $n=5000$.  It needs the lower
bulk to be frozen ($\alpha/\log M\to\infty$), which fails at fixed $\alpha$, and
at fixed $\alpha$ one has $\Lambda\to0$, outside its window.  Numerically, its
formula evaluated with the exact moments at this report's exact saddles gives
$\Theta(\mu_R,Q_\perp)=1.0000055$, $0.98057$ and $1.19429$ at $\alpha=3,4,5$,
$n=5000$ (exact $Q_\perp=0.535$, $0.229$, $0.114$, larger than
$\pi^2/(3\Lambda^3)$ because the lower bulk still contributes), against the
observed ratios $0.96163$, $1.23052$ and $1.58110$; its own diagnostics at
$\alpha=8$ (Table~\ref{dpn:ct:tab:diagnostics}) still differ from $\Theta$ by
$3\,\%$ and $16\,\%$.  (\wrt; 30-digit sums, uncertified.)  A direct
decomposition of those residuals into Fourier cells is recorded, as numerical
evidence only, in the batch-100 note of Section~\ref{dpn:jh:sec:numerics};
the formula that would explain them is question~100.6.

\paragraph{The phase average is the single Gaussian (\wrt).}
For every $Q>0$,
\begin{equation}\label{dpn:ct:eq:average}
 \int_0^1\Theta(\mu,Q)\dd\mu=1,
\end{equation}
since only the term $j=0$ of the uniformly convergent Fourier series in
\eqref{dpn:ct:eq:Theta} survives integration.  So the single Gaussian $G$ is
the phase average of the theta law, and
Corollary~\ref{dpn:ct:cor:obstruction}'s two limits lie on either side of it.
Source 100-93 does not state this.

%% ---------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Relation to the lattice-peak transfer theorem}\label{dpn:ct:sec:transfer}
Part~II of \file{oeis-sequence-asymptotics/a357825-theta-ballot-power-sums}
(written in \texttt{2b257ba94}, labels \texttt{tbs:lpt:}) proves a general
lattice-peak transfer theorem \cite{tbs}: for a positive row $C_{n,k}$ on a
moving lattice of span $d_n$, raised to a power $r_n$, its Theorem
\texttt{tbs:lpt:thm:transfer} gives $S_n(r_n)/\Omega_n^{r_n}=\vartheta(q_n,\delta_n)+o(1)$
with $\vartheta(q,\delta)=\sum_{j\in\mathbb Z}e^{-q(j-\delta)^2/2}$, under a local
quadratic law (H1), Gaussian or exponential domination (H2) or (H2e), and
interior completeness (H3).  Source 100-93 is not a power sum, and its theorem
is not a corollary of that one.  It is the same mechanism in Fourier-dual
form.  By \eqref{dpn:ct:eq:Theta},
\[
 \Theta(\mu,Q)=(2\pi Q)^{-1/2}\,\vartheta(1/Q,\mu),
\]
and $\sqrt{2\pi B}\,\Prob_t(S=n)=\sqrt{2\pi B}\sum_l\Prob_t(S=n,R_{\ge2}=l)$.  Read
with the row $l\mapsto C_{n,l}=\Prob_t(S=n,R_{\ge2}=l)$, $r_n=1$, $d_n=1$, peak
$x_n=\mu_R$ (so $\delta_n\equiv\mu_R\pmod1$), $q_n=1/Q_\perp\to3\Lambda^3/\pi^2$
and $\Omega_n=(2\pi\sqrt{BQ_\perp})^{-1}$ (the bivariate Gaussian density of
$(S,R_{\ge2})$ at its mean, since $BQ_\perp=BB_{RR}-B_{RS}^2$), the conclusion of
the transfer theorem is exactly Theorem~\ref{dpn:ct:thm:theta}.  But:
\begin{itemize}
\item hypothesis (H1) for this row is a local limit theorem for the pair
  $(S,R_{\ge2})$ on the fibre $S=n$, which source 100-93 does not prove: it
  integrates the one-dimensional characteristic function over cells, and its
  Gaussian satellites are the Fourier modes of $\vartheta$, not lattice sites;
\item (H2e) would follow from log-concavity of $l\mapsto\Prob_t(S=n,R_{\ge2}=l)$
  (Part~II's Proposition \texttt{tbs:lpt:prop:logconcave}), which is open;
\item in Part~II's normalization the critical narrowing comes from a growing
  power $r_n$ with curvature scale $\sigma_n\to\infty$; here $r_n=1$ and the
  growing $\alpha$ narrows the conditional law of $R_{\ge2}$ itself to width
  $\sqrt{Q_\perp}=O(1)$, so $\sigma_n$ stays bounded (the transfer theorem's
  proof uses only (H1)--(H3)).
\end{itemize}
Proving (H1) and (H2e) for this row is question~100-93.7.  The further
correspondences are of the same kind.  Corollary~\ref{dpn:ct:cor:obstruction}
is the two-cluster-value conclusion of Part~II's Corollary
\texttt{tbs:lpt:cor:clusters}, derived here from
Theorem~\ref{dpn:ct:thm:theta}.  Theorem~\ref{dpn:ct:thm:inverse} and
Remark~\ref{dpn:ct:rem:shift} have the form of Part~II's one-step inverse
transfer (Theorem \texttt{tbs:lpt:thm:inverse}) with $G=\Psi_0$ and the theta
term $\log\Theta$, whose regularity ratios hold here
($G''/G'^2\asymp-1/(Bt^2)=O(H/M^2)$, $(\log\Theta)'/G'=O(H/M)$); but that
theorem assumes $G'\to\infty$ or $G'$ bounded below, whereas here
$G'=t\to0$, so Remark~\ref{dpn:ct:rem:shift} proves the first-order statement
directly, and its last sentence is the conclusion of Part~II's Corollary
\texttt{tbs:lpt:cor:no-constant} in this case.  The phase average
\eqref{dpn:ct:eq:average} is Part~II's \texttt{tbs:lpt:eq:theta-average} after
the factor $(2\pi Q)^{-1/2}$.  Not covered by Part~II: the exclusion of part
one (Section~\ref{dpn:ct:sec:partone}), the frozen-tail product, and the
transition range of Lemma~\ref{dpn:ct:lem:block}.  A reciprocal note in
Part~II is proposed with this batch.

%% ---------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Numerical diagnostics \src{100-93}}\label{dpn:ct:sec:diagnostics}
Table~\ref{dpn:ct:tab:diagnostics} estimates the normalized multiplier
$\sqrt{2\pi B}\,\Prob_t(S=n)=a_\alpha(n)/G_\alpha(n)$.  These are Fourier
diagnostics, not exact integer coefficients or interval-certified enclosures;
the theorems do not depend on them.  For each integer power and target
$\Lambda$, the program selects a real saddle with $\mu_R$ integral or half
integral near a reference large-root saddle, rounds the mean index to the
nearest integer and re-solves the discrete saddle equation there.  The data
files record the integer $n$, the actual $M$ and $\Lambda$, and the final
phase discrepancy; every displayed $\Theta$ uses the finite discrete moments
and the final exact-saddle phase.  Across the 18 cases the phase discrepancy
satisfies $M|\mu_R-\mu_{\rm target}|<0.470$.
\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering\small
